[Exams] AWS Certified Machine Learning Engineer MLA-C01
Practice Exam 3 - AWS Certified Machine Learning Engineer (MLA-C01) - Results
Attempt 1

Question 1Skipped

An agricultural cooperative is building a member support application that provides automated responses to grower queries.


The application uses Amazon Q Business to retrieve answers to questions about crop insurance programs and regional subsidy packages.


However, the cooperative must ensure that the API responses do not include any references to discontinued or no-longer-available subsidy programs. 


Which solution will meet this requirement?


Correct answer
Use the blocked phrase functionality in Amazon Q Business to filter out responses containing references to discontinued subsidy programs before returning results.
Explanation
Correct. The blocked phrase functionality in Amazon Q Business is a built-in guardrail specifically designed to prevent certain terms or phrases from appearing in API responses. By configuring blocked phrases to include the names of discontinued or unavailable subsidy programs, the cooperative ensures those references are filtered at the application level without requiring any external processing. This approach integrates natively with Amazon Q Business, minimizes operational overhead, and applies filters in real time. Keyword tip: When a question involves suppressing specific content from Amazon Q Business API responses, look for the blocked phrase or global controls functionality as the lowest-overhead, natively integrated solution.
Set up an AWS Lambda function to process API responses from Amazon Q Business and remove any references to discontinued subsidy programs before displaying the results.
Explanation
Incorrect. Introducing an AWS Lambda function to post-process API responses from Amazon Q Business adds unnecessary latency, operational complexity, and ongoing maintenance overhead. The blocked phrase functionality in Amazon Q Business already handles this use case natively at the API level, making an external Lambda-based filtering layer redundant and architecturally inefficient. Keyword tip: When a native AWS service feature already satisfies the stated requirement, an external Lambda-based workaround is typically the incorrect option on the MLA-C01 exam due to the added complexity, latency, and operational burden it introduces.
Use Amazon Comprehend to analyze the text of API responses and block any responses containing references to discontinued subsidy programs.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon Comprehend is a Natural Language Processing (NLP) service designed for tasks such as entity recognition, sentiment analysis, and topic modeling. It does not integrate natively with Amazon Q Business to intercept and block API responses in real time. Implementing Amazon Comprehend for this purpose would require custom integration code, introduce additional latency, and significantly increase architectural complexity compared to the native blocked phrase functionality already available within Amazon Q Business. Keyword tip: Amazon Comprehend is a distractor in scenarios requiring real-time content filtering within Amazon Q Business; it is designed for offline or asynchronous text analysis workflows, not API response guardrails.
Configure an Amazon Kendra retriever to exclude any content related to discontinued subsidy programs from the response dataset.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon Kendra is an enterprise search and document retrieval service that can be configured as a retriever within Amazon Q Business to source indexed content. However, it does not provide phrase-level filtering of API responses generated by Amazon Q Business; configuring Kendra addresses source document indexing and retrieval scope, not the suppression of specific terms in generated output. Keyword tip: Amazon Kendra is a distractor when the requirement is to filter response content at the API output level rather than at the document ingestion or retrieval stage.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests the ability to select real-time stream processing architectures for ML feature aggregation.

It focuses on satisfying two requirements at the same time: low-latency aggregation at one-minute intervals and minimal operational overhead.

It is tied to the Data Preparation for Machine Learning (ML) domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Use Amazon SageMaker Processing jobs to periodically pull data from the Kinesis Data Streams application, aggregate it, and write the results to Amazon S3: This option is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Processing is designed for batch preprocessing, model evaluation, and offline processing jobs. It is not the right service for continuous, low-latency stream aggregation.

    Set up an Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose delivery stream to read data from the Kinesis Data Streams application, perform aggregations via an intermediary AWS Lambda function, and write the results to an Amazon S3 bucket with a buffer window of 1 minute: This is a correct option. Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose can read from Amazon Kinesis Data Streams, use AWS Lambda for transformation logic, and deliver results to Amazon S3 with a configurable buffer window. This provides a low-maintenance, near-real-time pattern.

    Use Amazon OpenSearch Service to ingest data directly from the Kinesis Data Streams application and configure an OpenSearch dashboard to perform real-time minute-by-minute aggregations: This option is incorrect. Amazon OpenSearch Service is useful for search, log analytics, and dashboards. It is not the primary service for stateful stream processing or reliable windowed feature aggregation.

    Use Amazon Managed Service for Apache Flink to process and aggregate the data in real time from the Kinesis Data Streams application. Write the aggregated results to an Amazon S3 bucket for downstream analytics and feature generation: This is a correct option. Amazon Managed Service for Apache Flink is designed for stateful, low-latency stream processing and supports time-window aggregations over data from Amazon Kinesis Data Streams.

    Launch an Amazon EMR cluster with Apache Spark Streaming to process the Kinesis Data Streams application. Aggregate the data in real time and save the results to an Amazon Redshift table: This option is incorrect. Amazon EMR with Apache Spark Streaming can process streaming data, but it requires cluster provisioning, scaling, tuning, and maintenance. That conflicts with the minimal operational overhead requirement.

Why Kinesis Data Firehose with Lambda and Managed Service for Apache Flink fit this scenario.

The scenario requires aggregating streaming data every minute.

The data is coming from Amazon Kinesis Data Streams.

The output must be available for downstream analytics and feature generation.

The solution must also minimize operational overhead.

That leads to two valid managed patterns.

The first pattern is Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose with an AWS Lambda transformation.

Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose can read from Amazon Kinesis Data Streams.

It can buffer records for a configured interval, such as one minute.

An AWS Lambda function can transform or aggregate records before the results are written to Amazon S3.

This is a good fit for lightweight, near-real-time aggregation with minimal infrastructure management.

The second pattern is Amazon Managed Service for Apache Flink.

This is the stronger fit when the scenario emphasizes stateful stream processing, real-time analytics, and windowed aggregations.

It supports tumbling and sliding windows, which are common for one-minute feature aggregation.

It also removes the need to manage Flink clusters manually.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Real-time stream processing: Use Amazon Managed Service for Apache Flink.

    Near-real-time serverless delivery: Use Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose.

    One-minute aggregation window: Use Flink windowing or Firehose buffering.

    Kinesis-native source: Read from Amazon Kinesis Data Streams.

    Minimal operational overhead: Avoid self-managed clusters.

    Downstream ML readiness: Write aggregated features to Amazon S3.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Kinesis streaming data + one-minute aggregations + low operational overhead = Amazon Managed Service for Apache Flink or Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose with AWS Lambda.

Remember:

    Amazon Managed Service for Apache Flink: Best for stateful, low-latency stream processing and time-window aggregation.

    Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose: Managed delivery service with buffering and optional AWS Lambda transformation.

    AWS Lambda: Lightweight transformation or aggregation logic.

    Amazon SageMaker Processing: Batch processing, not continuous streaming.

    Amazon OpenSearch Service: Search and analytics store, not a stream processing engine.

    Amazon EMR: Powerful, but higher operational overhead than managed/serverless alternatives.

Official AWS references.

    Streaming ETL with Apache Flink and Amazon Kinesis Data Analytics.

    Writing to Amazon Data Firehose using Kinesis Data Streams.

Key exam terms: Amazon Managed Service for Apache Flink, Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose, tumbling window aggregation, AWS Lambda transformation, buffer window, minimal operational overhead, real-time feature generation, Amazon Kinesis Data Streams.

Keep practicing.

Question 2Skipped

A petrochemical company, PetroSynth Analytics, has trained an ML model in Amazon SageMaker to predict pipeline throughput and delivery schedules based on real-time sensor and flow-rate data.


The company needs to deploy the model in a production environment to meet the following requirements: 


- Ensure high availability to provide predictions without interruptions.

- Achieve low latency for real-time predictions on delivery schedules.

- Process input data sizes ranging between 1 KB and 5 MB.

- Handle unpredictable surges in requests during peak operational hours.

- Scale dynamically to match fluctuating demand throughout the day.


Which of the following represents the best solution for the given scenario?   



Deploy the model using Amazon SageMaker asynchronous inference to handle unpredictable surges and process requests efficiently to meet strict low-latency requirements.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Asynchronous Inference is designed for use cases where requests can be queued and processed with higher latency tolerance — typically for large payloads exceeding 6 MB or long processing times where a near-real-time response is acceptable. It does not satisfy strict low-latency requirements because responses are not returned synchronously to the caller, making it unsuitable for the real-time delivery schedule predictions required by PetroSynth Analytics. Keyword tip: when you see 'large payload > 6 MB,' 'long processing time,' or 'latency-tolerant workload,' asynchronous inference is appropriate — eliminate it whenever the scenario demands synchronous, sub-second responses.
Use Amazon SageMaker serverless inference to automatically scale inferences based on demand, eliminating the need for endpoint management.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Serverless Inference is designed for workloads with idle periods between traffic spurts and can tolerate cold starts. Although it eliminates infrastructure management overhead, it is not suitable for strict, consistent low-latency real-time requirements, as cold starts can introduce unpredictable latency spikes that would disrupt time-sensitive operational decisions at PetroSynth Analytics. Keyword tip: when you see 'cold start tolerance' or 'intermittent traffic,' serverless inference is appropriate — but eliminate it immediately when the scenario specifies strict millisecond-latency SLAs or continuous availability.
Correct answer
Deploy the model using Amazon SageMaker real-time inference with an auto-scaling endpoint to manage bursts in traffic and provide high availability.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon SageMaker real-time inference provides dedicated, persistently available endpoints optimized for millisecond-latency predictions. It supports payload sizes up to 6 MB and synchronous processing times up to 60 seconds, fully covering the 1 KB–5 MB input size requirement stated in the scenario. Combined with Application Auto Scaling, the endpoint dynamically adjusts the number of instances to absorb unpredictable traffic surges during peak operational hours while maintaining continuous high availability. Keyword tip: when you see 'low latency,' 'high availability,' 'dynamic scaling,' and 'real-time predictions,' a SageMaker real-time inference endpoint paired with Application Auto Scaling is the canonical MLA-C01 answer.
Deploy the model using Amazon SageMaker batch transform to process predictions in batches during peak hours to optimize resource utilization.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Batch Transform is intended for offline, large-scale inference on entire datasets. It cannot serve real-time, synchronous predictions with low latency because it processes requests in bulk and returns results only after the entire job completes, making it incompatible with the continuous, on-demand prediction requirements of PetroSynth Analytics. Keyword tip: when you see 'large dataset,' 'offline inference,' or 'no real-time requirement,' batch transform is the correct choice — never for live, low-latency, synchronous predictions.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests the ability to choose the correct Amazon SageMaker inference deployment mode based on latency, availability, scalability, and payload size requirements.

It focuses on selecting Amazon SageMaker Real-Time Inference with auto scaling when the workload requires consistently low latency, continuous availability, and the ability to absorb traffic spikes.

It is tied to the Deployment and Orchestration of ML Workflows domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Use Amazon SageMaker serverless inference to automatically scale inferences based on demand, eliminating the need for endpoint management: This option is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Serverless Inference is useful for intermittent workloads that can tolerate cold starts. It is not the best fit when the scenario requires consistently low latency and continuous high availability.

    Deploy the model using Amazon SageMaker batch transform to process predictions in batches during peak hours to optimize resource utilization: This option is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Batch Transform is designed for offline batch inference over datasets. It does not provide synchronous real-time predictions.

    Deploy the model using Amazon SageMaker real-time inference with an auto-scaling endpoint to manage bursts in traffic and provide high availability: This is the correct option. Amazon SageMaker Real-Time Inference provides persistent endpoints for low-latency synchronous predictions. With Application Auto Scaling, the endpoint can scale to handle unpredictable bursts in traffic.

    Deploy the model using Amazon SageMaker asynchronous inference to handle unpredictable surges and process requests efficiently to meet strict low-latency requirements: This option is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Asynchronous Inference is designed for latency-tolerant workloads, larger payloads, or longer processing times. It queues requests and does not return synchronous low-latency responses.

Why real-time inference with auto scaling fits this scenario.

The scenario requires real-time predictions.

The application needs low latency.

The endpoint must remain highly available.

Traffic can spike during peak operational periods.

Payload sizes are moderate and fit within the real-time inference limits.

That points to Amazon SageMaker Real-Time Inference.

Amazon SageMaker Real-Time Inference uses persistent endpoints.

These endpoints are designed for synchronous request-response workloads where applications need immediate predictions.

When paired with Application Auto Scaling, the endpoint can increase or decrease capacity based on demand.

This allows the system to handle bursts without switching to batch or asynchronous processing.

Amazon SageMaker Serverless Inference is not ideal because cold starts can affect latency.

Amazon SageMaker Asynchronous Inference is not ideal because the workload requires synchronous responses.

Amazon SageMaker Batch Transform is not ideal because it is for offline inference jobs, not live prediction APIs.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Low latency: Use Amazon SageMaker Real-Time Inference.

    Synchronous prediction: Return responses directly to the caller.

    High availability: Keep a persistent endpoint available.

    Traffic spikes: Use Application Auto Scaling.

    Moderate payload size: Stay within real-time endpoint payload limits.

    Managed deployment: Use Amazon SageMaker endpoint hosting.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Low latency + synchronous predictions + high availability + traffic spikes = Amazon SageMaker Real-Time Inference with Application Auto Scaling.

Remember:

    Amazon SageMaker Real-Time Inference: Persistent endpoint for low-latency synchronous predictions.

    Application Auto Scaling: Scales endpoint capacity based on demand.

    Amazon SageMaker Serverless Inference: Intermittent traffic, cold-start tolerant workloads.

    Amazon SageMaker Asynchronous Inference: Latency-tolerant workloads, larger payloads, queued processing.

    Amazon SageMaker Batch Transform: Offline batch inference.

    Payload size: Real-time inference supports moderate synchronous payloads; very large payloads often point to asynchronous inference.

Official AWS references.

    Amazon SageMaker hosting FAQs.

    Deploy a model in Amazon SageMaker.

Key exam terms: Amazon SageMaker Real-Time Inference, Application Auto Scaling, Amazon SageMaker Serverless Inference cold start, Amazon SageMaker Asynchronous Inference, Amazon SageMaker Batch Transform, low latency, high availability, payload size limits, synchronous endpoint.

Question 3Skipped

You are a machine learning engineer at a telecommunications provider called NexWave Communications, working on a deep learning model to analyze network signal sequences derived from large-scale device telemetry datasets.


The model requires significant computational resources for training due to the complexity and size of the dataset, which consists of billions of individual sequence records collected from subscriber devices across the network. Additionally, once deployed, the model must provide real-time inferences for network operations center applications, requiring low-latency predictions.



Your task is to choose the appropriate compute environment for both training and inference.


  Which of the following compute environment configurations is the MOST SUITABLE for meeting the training and inference requirements of this use case?


Use Amazon SageMaker with p3 instances (NVIDIA V100 GPUs) for training, and deploy the model using ml.m5.large instances (Intel Skylake CPUs) for real-time inference to balance cost and performance.
Explanation
Incorrect. While p3 instances with NVIDIA V100 GPUs provide solid deep learning training performance, deploying on ml.m5.large instances (Intel Skylake CPUs) introduces a significant bottleneck for real-time inference at scale. General-purpose CPU-based instances are not optimized for low-latency deep learning inference and would be unable to meet the sub-millisecond prediction requirements of a live network operations center application. This pairing trades away inference performance for apparent cost savings, violating the stated low-latency constraint. Keyword tip: When you see 'low-latency real-time inference' requirements, general-purpose CPU instances such as m5 or m4 are typically distractors — look for purpose-built inference hardware instead.
Use Amazon EC2 with g4dn instances (NVIDIA T4 GPUs) for both training and inference, optimizing for cost-efficiency while maintaining moderate GPU performance.
Explanation
Incorrect. g4dn instances with NVIDIA T4 GPUs offer a cost-efficient entry point for GPU workloads but are not optimized for training extremely large deep learning models at the scale of billions of sequence records. The T4 GPU is designed primarily for inference and lighter training tasks; it lacks the HBM2 memory capacity, NVLink interconnect bandwidth, and tensor core throughput of A100-class GPUs needed for billion-scale sequence training. Furthermore, raw g4dn EC2 instances do not provide the managed SageMaker training infrastructure or the specialized low-latency characteristics of AWS Inferentia-based ml.inf1 instances. Keyword tip: g4dn is a cost-optimization distractor when the scenario clearly demands high-performance large-scale training and purpose-built ultra-low-latency inference.
Use Amazon SageMaker with ml.c5.large instances (Intel Cascade Lake CPUs) for training and inference, relying on the CPU's high memory bandwidth for both tasks.
Explanation
Incorrect. ml.c5.large instances with Intel Cascade Lake CPUs are designed for compute-intensive general-purpose workloads but lack the specialized GPU hardware required for efficient large-scale deep learning training. Training a model on billions of network signal sequence records using CPU-only instances would be impractical due to prohibitively long training times and the absence of the massively parallel matrix computation capabilities that GPUs provide. This option fails both the training scale requirement and the inference latency requirement simultaneously. Keyword tip: CPU-only configurations (c5, c4) are incorrect whenever the scenario specifies deep learning training on massive datasets requiring GPU-level parallelism.
Correct answer
Use Amazon SageMaker with p4d instances (NVIDIA A100 GPUs) for training to handle large-scale deep learning workloads, and deploy the model using ml.inf1 instances (AWS Inferentia chips) for low-latency inference.
Explanation
Correct. p4d instances powered by NVIDIA A100 GPUs represent the highest tier of GPU compute available in Amazon SageMaker for deep learning training, providing NVLink interconnects, large HBM2 memory, and tensor core throughput required to handle billion-scale telemetry sequence datasets efficiently. For inference, ml.inf1 instances equipped with AWS Inferentia chips are purpose-built to deliver high-throughput, low-latency deep learning inference at a reduced cost compared to GPU-based inference alternatives. This combination directly and simultaneously satisfies both the large-scale training requirement and the real-time low-latency inference requirement of NexWave Communications' network operations use case. Keyword tip: The p4d (training) + ml.inf1 (inference) pairing is the canonical SageMaker answer pattern for scenarios that combine 'maximum deep learning training performance' with 'low-latency production inference at scale.'
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests the ability to select the right Amazon SageMaker compute instance types for different ML lifecycle phases.

It focuses on choosing high-performance GPU instances for large-scale deep learning training and purpose-built inference instances for low-latency real-time predictions.

It is tied to the Deployment and Orchestration of ML Workflows domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Use Amazon SageMaker with p3 instances for training, and deploy the model using ml.m5.large instances for real-time inference to balance cost and performance: This option is incorrect. p3 instances with NVIDIA V100 GPUs can support deep learning training, but ml.m5.large instances are general-purpose CPU instances. They are not optimized for low-latency deep learning inference at scale.

    Use Amazon SageMaker with ml.c5.large instances for training and inference, relying on the CPU's high memory bandwidth for both tasks: This option is incorrect. ml.c5.large instances are CPU-based and are not suitable for large-scale deep learning training on billions of records. They also do not provide purpose-built hardware acceleration for low-latency inference.

    Use Amazon EC2 with g4dn instances for both training and inference, optimizing for cost-efficiency while maintaining moderate GPU performance: This option is incorrect. g4dn instances with NVIDIA T4 GPUs can be useful for cost-effective GPU workloads, but they are not the best match for very large-scale deep learning training. Using raw Amazon EC2 also adds more operational management compared with Amazon SageMaker.

    Use Amazon SageMaker with p4d instances for training to handle large-scale deep learning workloads, and deploy the model using ml.inf1 instances for low-latency inference: This is the correct option. p4d instances with NVIDIA A100 GPUs are designed for high-performance deep learning training. ml.inf1 instances use AWS Inferentia chips and are optimized for high-throughput, low-latency deep learning inference.

Why p4d for training and ml.inf1 for inference fit this scenario.

The scenario has two separate requirements.

First, the model must be trained on a massive deep learning dataset.

Second, the deployed model must support low-latency real-time inference.

Training and inference do not always need the same instance family.

For training, the workload needs high parallel compute, GPU acceleration, and fast interconnect performance.

That points to p4d instances with NVIDIA A100 GPUs.

These instances are designed for large-scale deep learning workloads and can handle demanding model training jobs more efficiently than CPU-based or smaller GPU options.

For inference, the priority changes.

The goal is not training throughput.

The goal is fast, cost-efficient prediction serving.

That points to ml.inf1 instances.

ml.inf1 instances use AWS Inferentia, which is purpose-built for deep learning inference.

This makes them a strong fit for production workloads that require low latency and high throughput.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Large-scale deep learning training: Use p4d instances.

    GPU acceleration: Use NVIDIA A100 GPUs for training.

    Real-time inference: Deploy on an inference-optimized instance family.

    Low latency: Use ml.inf1 with AWS Inferentia.

    Managed ML infrastructure: Use Amazon SageMaker.

    Avoid CPU bottlenecks: Do not use m5 or c5 for demanding deep learning inference or training.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Large-scale deep learning training + real-time low-latency inference = p4d for training and ml.inf1 for inference.

Remember:

    p4d: High-performance GPU training with NVIDIA A100.

    ml.inf1: Inference-optimized instances powered by AWS Inferentia.

    p3: GPU training option, but not the strongest fit when the scenario demands very large-scale performance.

    m5 and c5: General-purpose or compute-optimized CPU instances, not ideal for deep learning acceleration.

    g4dn: Cost-effective GPU option, but weaker for massive training workloads.

    Training vs inference: Choose instance types based on the lifecycle phase and performance requirement.

Official AWS references.

    Amazon EC2 instance types.

    Available instance types for Amazon SageMaker notebook instances.

Key exam terms: p4d instances, NVIDIA A100 GPU, ml.inf1, AWS Inferentia, low-latency inference, large-scale deep learning training, Amazon SageMaker compute, instance type selection.

Keep practicing.

Question 4Skipped

A sports analytics company, StatEdge Analytics, collects real-time clickstream data from its fan engagement web portal to analyze user behavior and recommend personalized content such as highlight reels and merchandise options.


The clickstream data is continuously streamed into an Amazon Kinesis Data Streams application.


The company needs to aggregate the data at one-minute intervals to calculate features such as page views and time spent per user session.


The solution must provide low-latency processing and require minimal operational overhead. Which of the following options will meet these requirements? (Select TWO.)
Launch an Amazon EMR cluster with Apache Spark Streaming to process the Kinesis Data Streams application. Aggregate the data in real time and save the results to an Amazon Redshift table.
Explanation
Incorrect. Although Amazon EMR with Apache Spark Streaming can process Kinesis Data Streams in real time, launching and managing an EMR cluster introduces significant operational overhead, including cluster provisioning, scaling, patching, and cost management, which directly contradicts the minimal-overhead requirement. Additionally, writing high-frequency, small aggregated results to Amazon Redshift is suboptimal compared to Amazon S3, as Redshift is architected for analytical query workloads rather than high-frequency incremental micro-batch writes. Keyword tip: 'EMR cluster management' is a reliable signal of high operational overhead in MLA-C01 scenarios — whenever minimal overhead is an explicit constraint, self-managed cluster solutions like EMR should be eliminated in favor of fully managed serverless alternatives.
Use Amazon OpenSearch Service to ingest data directly from the Kinesis Data Streams application and configure an OpenSearch dashboard to perform real-time minute-by-minute aggregations.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon OpenSearch Service is optimized for full-text search, log analytics, and ad hoc querying — not for stateful real-time stream aggregation with precise time windows. While OpenSearch can ingest data from Kinesis Data Streams via integrations, configuring it to perform reliable, repeatable minute-by-minute feature aggregations would require complex custom workarounds and introduce unnecessary operational overhead, violating the minimal-overhead requirement. Keyword tip: OpenSearch Service is a consistent distractor in feature engineering pipeline questions; it is a search and analytics store, not a stream processing engine, and should be eliminated when stateful windowed aggregation is required.
Correct selection
Use Amazon Managed Service for Apache Flink to process and aggregate the data in real time from the Kinesis Data Streams application. Write the aggregated results to an Amazon S3 bucket for downstream analytics and feature generation.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon Managed Service for Apache Flink (formerly Amazon Kinesis Data Analytics for Apache Flink) is a fully managed service that provides native, low-latency integration with Kinesis Data Streams and requires no cluster provisioning or infrastructure management. It supports stateful stream processing with tumbling or sliding time windows, making it ideal for calculating features such as page views and session duration at precise one-minute intervals. Writing aggregated results to Amazon S3 enables seamless downstream feature engineering and integration with SageMaker or other analytics services. Keyword tip: 'Managed Service for Apache Flink + Kinesis Data Streams + tumbling window aggregation' is the primary AWS-native pattern for low-latency, low-overhead real-time feature generation — a combination that satisfies both stated constraints simultaneously.
Correct selection
Set up an Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose delivery stream to read data from the Kinesis Data Streams application, perform aggregations via an intermediary AWS Lambda function, and write the results to an Amazon S3 bucket with a buffer window of 1 minute.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose natively integrates with Kinesis Data Streams as a source and supports near-real-time delivery with configurable buffer windows (minimum 60 seconds, satisfying the 1-minute interval requirement). By attaching an AWS Lambda transformation function to the Firehose delivery stream, StatEdge Analytics can perform custom aggregations — such as page view counts and session duration calculations — before writing results to Amazon S3. This is a fully managed, serverless solution that requires minimal operational overhead with no infrastructure to provision or maintain. Keyword tip: 'Kinesis Data Firehose + Lambda transformation + buffer window' is the canonical AWS pattern for lightweight, low-maintenance near-real-time feature aggregation pipelines — a combination that directly satisfies both low-latency and minimal-overhead constraints.
Use Amazon SageMaker Processing jobs to periodically pull data from the Kinesis Data Streams application, aggregate it, and write the results to Amazon S3.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Processing jobs are designed for batch-oriented workloads such as data preprocessing, model evaluation, and post-training analysis. They are not suited for continuous real-time stream processing, and periodically polling from Kinesis Data Streams introduces significant latency that violates the low-latency requirement. This approach also adds scheduling complexity rather than reducing operational overhead. Keyword tip: When 'SageMaker Processing' appears in a real-time streaming scenario requiring low latency, recognize it as a batch processing service misapplied to a streaming use case — a reliable distractor on the MLA-C01 exam.
Overall explanation

It also removes the need to manage Flink clusters manually.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Real-time stream processing: Use Amazon Managed Service for Apache Flink.

    Near-real-time serverless delivery: Use Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose.

    One-minute aggregation window: Use Flink windowing or Firehose buffering.

    Kinesis-native source: Read from Amazon Kinesis Data Streams.

    Minimal operational overhead: Avoid self-managed clusters.

    Downstream ML readiness: Write aggregated features to Amazon S3.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Kinesis streaming data + one-minute aggregations + low operational overhead = Amazon Managed Service for Apache Flink or Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose with AWS Lambda.

Remember:

    Amazon Managed Service for Apache Flink: Best for stateful, low-latency stream processing and time-window aggregation.

    Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose: Managed delivery service with buffering and optional AWS Lambda transformation.

    AWS Lambda: Lightweight transformation or aggregation logic.

    Amazon SageMaker Processing: Batch processing, not continuous streaming.

    Amazon OpenSearch Service: Search and analytics store, not a stream processing engine.

    Amazon EMR: Powerful, but higher operational overhead than managed/serverless alternatives.

Official AWS references.

    Streaming ETL with Apache Flink and Amazon Kinesis Data Analytics.

    Writing to Amazon Data Firehose using Kinesis Data Streams.

Key exam terms: Amazon Managed Service for Apache Flink, Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose, tumbling window aggregation, AWS Lambda transformation, buffer window, minimal operational overhead, real-time feature generation, Amazon Kinesis Data Streams.

Question 5Skipped

A hospitality and hotel chain company, StayWell Hotels, has developed a recommendation ML model to enhance the guest experience on its booking platform by personalizing room upgrade and amenity suggestions.


Before fully deploying the model in production, the company wants to perform online validation by routing 20% of the traffic to the new model while the remaining traffic continues to use the existing model.


Which solution will implement the online validation with minimal operational overhead?


Correct answer
Leverage the production variants feature to add the new model into the existing SageMaker endpoint. Assign a weight of 0.2 to the new model variant and use Amazon CloudWatch to monitor the number of invocations.
Explanation
Correct. SageMaker's production variants feature allows multiple model versions to be hosted behind a single endpoint, with traffic distributed according to proportional VariantWeight values. Setting the new model variant's weight to 0.2 and retaining the existing model's weight at 0.8 routes exactly 20% of live inference requests to the new model, satisfying StayWell Hotels' validation requirement. Amazon CloudWatch automatically emits per-variant metrics such as Invocations and Latency with no additional instrumentation, enabling continuous monitoring without extra tooling or automation infrastructure. This makes production variants the lowest-overhead solution for steady-state online validation. Keyword tip: SageMaker production variants + proportional VariantWeight (e.g., 0.2 vs. 0.8) = native A/B testing with live user responses and minimal operational overhead.
Leverage the shadow variants feature to add the new model into the existing SageMaker endpoint. Assign a weight of 0.2 to the new model variant and use Amazon CloudWatch to monitor the number of invocations.
Explanation
Incorrect. The SageMaker shadow variants feature sends a mirrored copy of inference requests from the production variant to a shadow variant for internal logging and comparison; however, the shadow variant's responses are never returned to end users. This design is suited for offline shadow testing — validating a new model's behavior privately without any user-facing impact. The StayWell Hotels scenario explicitly requires that 20% of live guests receive responses from the new model so that real booking interactions validate its recommendations, which is fundamentally incompatible with the shadow testing pattern. Keyword tip: Shadow variants = silent parallel evaluation with logged-only outputs; production variants = live traffic splitting where callers receive real responses from each variant.
Choose canary traffic shifting in SageMaker and configure the capacity_percent parameter to 20%. Set up Amazon CloudWatch alarms to monitor the endpoint's metrics.
Explanation
Incorrect. Canary traffic shifting in SageMaker is a deployment safety guardrail designed to validate a new fleet incrementally before completing a full traffic migration, not to maintain a steady-state traffic split for ongoing online validation. Although the capacity_percent parameter can divert a portion of traffic to a new fleet, this mechanism is inherently transitional — it is intended to conclude once the new fleet is fully promoted or rolled back. It also requires additional automation and CloudWatch alarm configuration to manage the shift lifecycle, resulting in higher operational overhead than production variants. Keyword tip: SageMaker canary traffic shifting = transitional deployment safety mechanism for fleet upgrades, not a steady-state online validation or A/B testing strategy.
Leverage the production variants feature to add the new model into the existing SageMaker endpoint. Assign a weight of 20 to the new model variant and use Amazon CloudWatch to monitor the number of invocations.
Explanation
Incorrect. While using the production variants feature is the right general approach, assigning an absolute weight of 20 to the new model variant does not produce 20% traffic routing in isolation. SageMaker distributes traffic proportionally based on the relative ratio of each variant's weight to the total sum of all weights. For example, if the existing model retains weight 1 and the new model is assigned weight 20, the new model would receive approximately 95% of all traffic — the inverse of the intended 20%. Keyword tip: When using SageMaker production variants, always evaluate VariantWeight values as ratios relative to the sum of all variant weights, not as standalone percentages.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests understanding of Amazon SageMaker traffic-splitting mechanisms for online model validation.

It focuses on choosing SageMaker production variants when the requirement is to send a fixed percentage of live traffic to a new model while returning real responses to users.

It is tied to the ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Leverage the production variants feature to add the new model into the existing SageMaker endpoint. Assign a weight of 20 to the new model variant and use Amazon CloudWatch to monitor the number of invocations: This option is incorrect. VariantWeight values are proportional, not absolute percentages. If the old model has weight 1 and the new model has weight 20, the new model receives most of the traffic, not 20%.

    Leverage the production variants feature to add the new model into the existing SageMaker endpoint. Assign a weight of 0.2 to the new model variant and use Amazon CloudWatch to monitor the number of invocations: This is the correct option. Amazon SageMaker production variants allow multiple model versions behind one endpoint, with traffic split proportionally by VariantWeight. A 0.2 weight for the new model and 0.8 for the existing model routes 20% of traffic to the new model.

    Choose canary traffic shifting in SageMaker and configure the capacity_percent parameter to 20%. Set up Amazon CloudWatch alarms to monitor the endpoint's metrics: This option is incorrect. Canary traffic shifting is mainly used as a deployment safety mechanism for gradual rollout or rollback. It is not the best fit for steady-state A/B testing where 20% of live users should continuously receive responses from the new model.

    Leverage the shadow variants feature to add the new model into the existing SageMaker endpoint. Assign a weight of 0.2 to the new model variant and use Amazon CloudWatch to monitor the number of invocations: This option is incorrect. SageMaker shadow variants mirror requests to a new model for comparison, but the shadow model's responses are not returned to users. The scenario requires live users to receive responses from the new model.

Why SageMaker production variants fit this scenario.

The company wants to validate a new model online.

The new model must receive a specific portion of live traffic.

Users must receive real responses from whichever model handles the request.

The setup should require minimal operational overhead.

That points to Amazon SageMaker production variants.

Production variants allow multiple models to run behind the same SageMaker endpoint.

Each variant has a VariantWeight.

Traffic is routed proportionally based on the relative weights of all variants.

For example:

    Existing model weight: 0.8

    New model weight: 0.2

This sends 80% of requests to the existing model and 20% of requests to the new model.

Amazon CloudWatch can then monitor per-variant metrics such as invocations and latency.

This makes production variants a strong fit for A/B testing and steady-state online validation.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Live traffic split: Use SageMaker production variants.

    20% model validation: Set proportional VariantWeight values.

    Real user responses: Use production variants, not shadow variants.

    Minimal operational overhead: Avoid custom routing logic.

    Per-variant observability: Use Amazon CloudWatch metrics.

    Steady-state A/B testing: Avoid canary deployment when the goal is persistent validation.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Live A/B testing + real responses + fixed traffic percentage + one SageMaker endpoint = production variants with VariantWeight.

Remember:

    Production variants: Live traffic splitting across model versions.

    VariantWeight: Proportional routing value, not a standalone percentage.

    Shadow variants: Silent evaluation; responses are not returned to users.

    Canary traffic shifting: Gradual rollout safety mechanism, not persistent A/B testing.

    Amazon CloudWatch: Monitors per-variant metrics such as invocations and latency.

    A/B testing: Strong signal for SageMaker production variants.

Official AWS references.

    Validate models in Amazon SageMaker.

Question 6Skipped

A healthcare diagnostics provider, MediScan Analytics, has tasked its ML team to analyze patient transaction data for enhancing its fraudulent billing detection system and to identify potential patient segments for targeted outreach campaigns.


The company has a dataset with multiple features, including procedure costs, claim frequency, and facility location data.  The tasks are listed below, and the ML team must use Amazon SageMaker built-in algorithms to complete these tasks: 


- Reduce the dimensionality of the dataset to improve model performance and visualization.

- Perform cluster analysis to identify distinct patient groups. - Detect anomalous billing transactions that may indicate fraudulent claims. 


Which combination of SageMaker built-in algorithms should the ML team use to meet the requirements?


XGBoost for dimensionality reduction, K-Means for cluster analysis, and Random Cut Forest (RCF) for anomaly detection
Explanation
Incorrect. XGBoost is a supervised learning algorithm designed for regression, binary classification, multiclass classification, and ranking problems — it does not perform dimensionality reduction in any form. Assigning XGBoost to a dimensionality reduction task reflects a fundamental misunderstanding of its purpose within the SageMaker built-in algorithm catalog. K-Means and RCF are correctly assigned, but the first component disqualifies this option entirely. Keyword tip: whenever a distractor assigns XGBoost to an unsupervised task such as dimensionality reduction or clustering, it can be immediately eliminated as XGBoost is strictly a supervised algorithm.
Correct answer
Principal Component Analysis (PCA) for dimensionality reduction, K-Means for cluster analysis, and Random Cut Forest (RCF) for anomaly detection
Explanation
Correct. This combination fully satisfies all three stated requirements using exclusively Amazon SageMaker built-in algorithms. PCA (Principal Component Analysis) is an unsupervised algorithm that reduces the number of features while retaining maximum variance, making it the canonical SageMaker solution for dimensionality reduction on high-dimensional datasets such as MediScan Analytics' billing records. K-Means is an unsupervised clustering algorithm that partitions data points into k discrete clusters based on feature similarity, directly addressing the patient segmentation requirement. Random Cut Forest (RCF) is a purpose-built unsupervised anomaly detection algorithm that assigns an anomaly score to each data point, making it ideal for flagging fraudulent billing transactions. Keyword tip: for MLA-C01 scenarios requiring unsupervised dimensionality reduction, clustering, and anomaly detection using SageMaker built-in algorithms, the definitive combination is PCA + K-Means + RCF.
Principal Component Analysis (PCA) for dimensionality reduction, K-Means for cluster analysis, and Neural Networks for anomaly detection
Explanation
Incorrect. While PCA and K-Means correctly address the dimensionality reduction and clustering requirements, ""Neural Networks"" is not a dedicated SageMaker built-in algorithm for anomaly detection. Although SageMaker supports deep learning frameworks such as TensorFlow and PyTorch, the purpose-built built-in algorithm for anomaly detection in SageMaker is Random Cut Forest (RCF); a generic neural network reference does not constitute a SageMaker built-in algorithm and does not satisfy the constraint stated in the question. Keyword tip: for anomaly detection using SageMaker built-in algorithms, RCF is the only purpose-built managed option; references to neural networks or deep learning frameworks in this context are distractors that violate the built-in-only constraint.
Linear Learner for dimensionality reduction, DBSCAN for cluster analysis, and Random Cut Forest (RCF) for anomaly detection
Explanation
Incorrect. The Amazon SageMaker Linear Learner algorithm is a supervised learning algorithm used for classification and regression problems — it is not designed for dimensionality reduction. Additionally, DBSCAN is not a SageMaker built-in algorithm; it is available in open-source libraries such as scikit-learn but is not natively offered as a SageMaker managed algorithm, violating the explicit constraint in the question. Keyword tip: whenever an option includes an algorithm absent from the SageMaker built-in algorithm catalog (e.g., DBSCAN, Isolation Forest), it fails the 'built-in algorithms only' constraint and must be eliminated regardless of whether the other algorithms in the combination are correct.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of Amazon SageMaker built-in unsupervised algorithms.

It focuses on mapping three common ML tasks to the correct native algorithms: dimensionality reduction, clustering, and anomaly detection.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    XGBoost for dimensionality reduction, K-Means for cluster analysis, and Random Cut Forest for anomaly detection: This option is incorrect. XGBoost is a supervised algorithm used for classification, regression, and ranking. It does not perform dimensionality reduction. Although K-Means and Random Cut Forest are correctly assigned, the incorrect use of XGBoost makes the full option invalid.

    Linear Learner for dimensionality reduction, DBSCAN for cluster analysis, and Random Cut Forest for anomaly detection: This option is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Linear Learner is a supervised algorithm for classification and regression, not dimensionality reduction. DBSCAN is also not an Amazon SageMaker built-in algorithm, so it violates the built-in-only requirement.

    Principal Component Analysis for dimensionality reduction, K-Means for cluster analysis, and Random Cut Forest for anomaly detection: This is the correct option. Principal Component Analysis (PCA) is used for dimensionality reduction, K-Means is used for clustering, and Random Cut Forest (RCF) is used for anomaly detection. All three are Amazon SageMaker built-in algorithms.

    Principal Component Analysis for dimensionality reduction, K-Means for cluster analysis, and Neural Networks for anomaly detection: This option is incorrect. PCA and K-Means are correctly matched, but “Neural Networks” is not the purpose-built Amazon SageMaker built-in algorithm for anomaly detection. The correct built-in anomaly detection algorithm is Random Cut Forest.

Why PCA, K-Means, and Random Cut Forest fit this scenario.

The scenario requires three different unsupervised ML capabilities.

The first requirement is dimensionality reduction.

That maps to Principal Component Analysis (PCA).

PCA reduces the number of features while preserving as much variance as possible.

This is useful when the dataset has many features, correlated columns, or high-dimensional inputs.

The second requirement is cluster analysis.

That maps to K-Means.

K-Means groups similar records into clusters without requiring labeled target values.

This is useful for segmentation and pattern discovery.

The third requirement is anomaly detection.

That maps to Random Cut Forest (RCF).

RCF assigns anomaly scores to records and helps identify unusual behavior, outliers, or suspicious observations.

This combination satisfies the built-in-only constraint because all three algorithms are available as Amazon SageMaker built-in algorithms.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Dimensionality reduction: Use Principal Component Analysis (PCA).

    Cluster analysis: Use K-Means.

    Anomaly detection: Use Random Cut Forest (RCF).

    Unsupervised learning: Use algorithms that do not require labeled targets.

    Built-in-only constraint: Avoid algorithms outside the Amazon SageMaker built-in catalog.

    Avoid supervised distractors: Do not choose XGBoost or Linear Learner for unsupervised tasks.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Dimensionality reduction + clustering + anomaly detection + SageMaker built-in algorithms = PCA + K-Means + RCF.

    Principal Component Analysis (PCA): Dimensionality reduction.

Question 7Skipped

A sports analytics company called AthleteEdge Analytics is developing an ML model to predict whether professional athletes are at risk of developing a rare but critical chronic injury requiring immediate intervention. Missing an at-risk athlete (a false negative) could delay treatment, potentially leading to career-ending consequences.


However, incorrectly predicting the injury (a false positive) may result in unnecessary medical evaluations, which are significantly less costly compared to missing an actual case. 


Which model characteristic should AthleteEdge Analytics prioritize to best meet its requirements?


Prioritize a model with high precision
Explanation
Incorrect. High precision minimizes false positives (i.e., it reduces unnecessary medical evaluations), but it often comes at the cost of increased false negatives. In this scenario, a precision-focused model would allow more at-risk athletes to go undetected without intervention, which is the exact outcome AthleteEdge Analytics must avoid given the asymmetric cost structure. Keyword tip: Precision optimizes for fewer false positives; it is the preferred metric when incorrectly flagging a negative case is the primary concern, not when missing a true positive carries severe consequences.
Prioritize a model with low recall
Explanation
Incorrect. A model with low recall produces more false negatives, meaning it misses a greater proportion of true positive cases. In this scenario, that directly translates to at-risk athletes being overlooked and not receiving timely intervention, which is explicitly identified as the most costly and dangerous outcome. Optimizing toward low recall would be counter to the stated business and safety requirements. Keyword tip: Low recall always means more false negatives — avoid this design choice when missing a positive case carries severe or irreversible consequences in the given scenario.
Prioritize a model with high accuracy
Explanation
Incorrect. Accuracy measures the overall proportion of correct predictions across both classes. For imbalanced datasets — where positive cases (at-risk athletes) are rare — a model can achieve high accuracy by predicting the majority class (not at risk) almost exclusively, while still missing the vast majority of true positive cases. This makes accuracy a misleading and dangerous primary metric in rare-event detection scenarios with asymmetric costs. Keyword tip: Accuracy is unreliable for imbalanced classification tasks involving rare events; prefer recall, precision, or F1-score depending on the specific asymmetric cost structure described in the scenario.
Correct answer
Prioritize a model with high recall
Explanation
Correct. Recall is defined as: Recall = True Positives / (True Positives + False Negatives). It measures the proportion of actual positive cases that the model correctly identifies. When the cost of a false negative far exceeds the cost of a false positive — as is the case here where missing an at-risk athlete can lead to career-ending injury — maximizing recall ensures that nearly all true positive cases are flagged, even at the expense of generating more false positives and incurring additional medical evaluations. Keyword tip: When a scenario explicitly states that missing a positive case (false negative) carries severe, irreversible consequences, prioritizing high recall is the correct design choice for the evaluation metric.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests understanding of ML evaluation metrics and how to select the right metric when false positives and false negatives have different business impacts.

It focuses on recognizing that when false negatives are much more costly than false positives, the model should prioritize high recall.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Prioritize a model with high recall: This is the correct option. Recall measures how many actual positive cases the model correctly identifies. High recall reduces false negatives, which is critical when missing a true positive case has severe consequences.

    Prioritize a model with low recall: This option is incorrect. Low recall means the model misses more actual positive cases. That increases false negatives, which is exactly what the scenario wants to avoid.

    Prioritize a model with high precision: This option is incorrect. Precision reduces false positives, but it may allow more false negatives. Precision is more important when incorrectly flagging a negative case is the bigger problem.

    Prioritize a model with high accuracy: This option is incorrect. Accuracy can be misleading, especially with imbalanced datasets. A model can achieve high accuracy by predicting the majority class while still missing many rare positive cases.

Why high recall fits this scenario.

The scenario describes a binary classification problem.

The cost of a false negative is very high.

That means missing a true positive case is worse than incorrectly flagging a negative case.

In this type of situation, the model should prioritize recall.

Recall is calculated as:

Recall = True Positives / (True Positives + False Negatives)

High recall means the model captures most of the actual positive cases.

This may increase false positives, but that trade-off is acceptable when the cost of missing a real positive case is severe.

For example, if the model is identifying at-risk athletes, missing an actually at-risk athlete could lead to serious consequences.

It is better to flag more athletes for review than to miss someone who truly needs intervention.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Minimize false negatives: Prioritize recall.

    Catch rare positive cases: Use a metric that focuses on true positives.

    Handle asymmetric cost: Optimize for the more expensive error type.

    Avoid misleading accuracy: Do not rely on accuracy when classes may be imbalanced.

    Accept more false positives when necessary: Trade unnecessary review for safer detection.

    Use the correct classification metric: Match the metric to the business risk.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

False negative is worse than false positive = prioritize recall.

Remember:

    Recall: Reduces false negatives.

    Precision: Reduces false positives.

    Accuracy: Can mislead on imbalanced datasets.

    False negative: The model misses a real positive case.

    False positive: The model incorrectly flags a negative case as positive.

    High-risk detection: Usually favors recall.

    Precision-recall trade-off: Increasing recall may reduce precision.

Official AWS references.

    Amazon SageMaker Autopilot metrics and validation.

    Using image-level metrics in Amazon Rekognition Custom Labels.

Key exam terms: recall, false negative, false positive, precision, accuracy, imbalanced dataset, asymmetric cost, rare-event detection, classification metrics, precision-recall trade-off.

Question 8Skipped

You are a machine learning engineer at GenomePath Analytics, a pharmaceutical research organization that has deployed an ML model on Amazon SageMaker to provide real-time predictions for a drug interaction screening application.


The model is critical for laboratory operations and must operate with high availability and low latency.


Recently, researchers have reported increased latency in receiving predictions, and there have been occasional timeout errors disrupting screening workflows. To maintain the performance and reliability of the model, you need to configure monitoring and alerting tools to troubleshoot and resolve these issues promptly.


  Which of the following approaches is the MOST EFFECTIVE for configuring and using Amazon CloudWatch tools to troubleshoot and analyze the performance of your ML solution?
Rely on the SageMaker console to monitor model performance metrics, and configure CloudWatch Logs to store training logs without setting up specific alarms, analyzing logs only when issues arise.
Explanation
Incorrect. Relying solely on the SageMaker console for monitoring is a reactive, manual approach that is insufficient for production systems with high availability requirements. The SageMaker console provides some visibility into endpoint status and metrics, but it does not offer automated alerting or threshold-based notifications. Storing training logs in CloudWatch Logs without configuring alarms means the team will only investigate issues after researchers report them — which directly contradicts the proactive monitoring requirement for a critical drug interaction screening application. Keyword tip: Any answer that proposes analyzing logs 'only when issues arise' is inherently reactive and incompatible with high-availability, low-latency production ML inference requirements.
Use CloudWatch Logs Insights to manually search and analyze logs from the SageMaker training jobs, and configure CloudWatch Alarms to track CPU and memory usage on the EC2 instances running the model.
Explanation
Incorrect. While CloudWatch Logs Insights is a powerful tool for querying and analyzing log data, focusing exclusively on SageMaker training job logs and EC2 instance-level CPU and memory metrics does not address the core problem. The reported issues — increased latency and timeout errors — are inference-time phenomena occurring at the SageMaker endpoint level, not during training. Endpoint-specific metrics such as InvocationLatency, ModelLatency, and 4XX/5XX error counts are the correct signals to monitor for a production real-time endpoint. Keyword tip: When the scenario involves a deployed real-time endpoint and production inference issues, always focus monitoring on endpoint-level metrics (InvocationLatency, ModelLatency, error counts) rather than training job or EC2 host-level metrics.
Enable Amazon CloudTrail for logging all API calls, and use CloudWatch Logs to monitor network traffic. Create CloudWatch Alarms based on API activity to detect performance issues.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon CloudTrail is an audit and governance service designed to record AWS API calls for compliance and security investigations — it is not intended for real-time performance monitoring of ML endpoints. Monitoring network traffic at the VPC flow log level similarly does not provide application-layer insights such as model inference latency, prediction errors, or container-level timeout causes. This option is a classic MLA-C01 distractor that conflates audit tooling with operational performance monitoring. Keyword tip: CloudTrail answers 'who did what and when' for governance and compliance purposes, not 'why is my endpoint slow' for inference performance troubleshooting — never use CloudTrail as the primary tool for ML endpoint performance analysis.
Correct answer
Enable Amazon CloudWatch Logs to capture detailed logs from the SageMaker endpoint, configure CloudWatch Alarms to monitor invocation latency and error rates, and set up notifications to alert the team when thresholds are breached.
Explanation
Correct. This is the most comprehensive and operationally sound approach for the described scenario. Amazon SageMaker automatically publishes endpoint metrics — including InvocationLatency, ModelLatency, Invocations, InvocationsPerInstance, and HTTP error counts — to Amazon CloudWatch. By enabling CloudWatch Logs on the SageMaker endpoint, engineers can capture detailed container logs to investigate root causes of latency spikes and timeout errors. Configuring CloudWatch Alarms on the relevant metrics enables threshold-based alerting, and integrating with Amazon SNS for notifications ensures the team can respond proactively before user impact escalates. Keyword tip: For real-time SageMaker endpoint monitoring, the winning combination is CloudWatch Logs (endpoint container logs) + CloudWatch Alarms (invocation latency and error rate metrics) + SNS notifications for proactive, automated alerting.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests operational monitoring for deployed Amazon SageMaker real-time inference endpoints using Amazon CloudWatch.

It focuses on monitoring endpoint-level metrics, capturing endpoint logs, configuring threshold-based alarms, and notifying the team proactively when inference performance degrades.

It is tied to the ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Use CloudWatch Logs Insights to manually search and analyze logs from the SageMaker training jobs, and configure CloudWatch Alarms to track CPU and memory usage on the EC2 instances running the model: This option is incorrect. The issue is happening at inference time on a deployed Amazon SageMaker endpoint. Training job logs and host-level Amazon EC2 metrics do not directly address endpoint invocation latency, model latency, or inference error rates.

    Enable Amazon CloudWatch Logs to capture detailed logs from the SageMaker endpoint, configure CloudWatch Alarms to monitor invocation latency and error rates, and set up notifications to alert the team when thresholds are breached: This is the correct option. Amazon SageMaker publishes endpoint metrics to Amazon CloudWatch, and endpoint container logs can be captured in Amazon CloudWatch Logs. CloudWatch Alarms can monitor latency and error thresholds, and Amazon SNS can notify the team automatically.

    Rely on the SageMaker console to monitor model performance metrics, and configure CloudWatch Logs to store training logs without setting up specific alarms, analyzing logs only when issues arise: This option is incorrect. This is a reactive monitoring pattern. Production inference workloads require proactive alerting, not manual console checks or log analysis only after users report problems.

    Enable Amazon CloudTrail for logging all API calls, and use CloudWatch Logs to monitor network traffic. Create CloudWatch Alarms based on API activity to detect performance issues: This option is incorrect. AWS CloudTrail is used for API audit logging and governance. It does not monitor inference latency, timeout errors, model container behavior, or endpoint performance.

Why CloudWatch Logs, CloudWatch Alarms, and SNS fit this scenario.

The model is deployed as a real-time Amazon SageMaker endpoint.

The application is experiencing latency increases and timeout errors.

The team needs proactive operational monitoring.

That points to Amazon CloudWatch.

Amazon SageMaker automatically emits endpoint-level metrics to Amazon CloudWatch.

Important metrics include InvocationLatency, ModelLatency, Invocations, InvocationsPerInstance, and HTTP error counts such as 4XX and 5XX errors.

Amazon CloudWatch Logs provides deeper visibility into endpoint container logs.

These logs help investigate root causes such as application errors, timeout behavior, dependency failures, or container-level issues.

CloudWatch Alarms can evaluate key metrics against thresholds.

When a threshold is breached, the alarm can trigger an Amazon SNS notification so the team receives an alert without waiting for manual investigation.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Production inference monitoring: Use Amazon CloudWatch endpoint metrics.

    Latency tracking: Monitor InvocationLatency and ModelLatency.

    Error detection: Monitor invocation errors and timeout-related signals.

    Root cause analysis: Capture endpoint logs in Amazon CloudWatch Logs.

    Proactive alerting: Configure CloudWatch Alarms.

    Team notification: Use Amazon SNS.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

SageMaker real-time endpoint + latency/errors + proactive alerting = Amazon CloudWatch Logs + CloudWatch Alarms + Amazon SNS.

Remember:

    InvocationLatency: End-to-end latency observed by the Amazon SageMaker endpoint.

    ModelLatency: Time spent by the model container processing the request.

    Amazon CloudWatch Logs: Stores endpoint container logs for troubleshooting.

    CloudWatch Alarms: Provides threshold-based automated alerting.

    Amazon SNS: Sends notifications to subscribed teams or systems.

    AWS CloudTrail: API audit logging, not endpoint performance monitoring.

    Training logs: Not the primary source for production inference degradation.

Question 9Skipped

A hospitality and hotel chain company, StayBridge Hotels, wants to build an ML solution to predict customer churn. The company's dataset includes thousands of rows with features related to guest demographics, booking history, and loyalty program interactions.


The dataset is stored in CSV format in Amazon S3.


The ML engineer must select an appropriate built-in Amazon SageMaker algorithm for training the model. The algorithm should handle tabular data and be capable of performing supervised learning for a binary classification problem. 


Which Amazon SageMaker built-in algorithm should the ML engineer use? 



BlazingText
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker BlazingText provides highly optimized implementations of Word2vec and text classification algorithms. It is designed for natural language processing (NLP) tasks such as sentiment analysis, named entity recognition, and machine translation — none of which apply to structured tabular data such as guest demographics or booking history. Applying BlazingText to a binary classification problem on tabular features would yield no meaningful result, as the algorithm operates on text corpora rather than numerical or categorical feature vectors. Keyword tip: when you see NLP, word embeddings, or text classification tasks, consider BlazingText — not tabular ML problems involving structured demographic or transactional features.
Linear Learner
Explanation
Incorrect. Linear Learner is a supervised learning algorithm capable of solving both classification and regression problems using linear models, and it can technically perform binary classification. However, it assumes a largely linear relationship between input features and the target variable, making it generally less effective than XGBoost for complex tabular data where non-linear interactions between features — such as the interplay between booking frequency, loyalty tier, and cancellation patterns — drive predictive accuracy. For churn prediction, where feature interactions are often non-linear and intricate, XGBoost consistently outperforms linear approaches. Keyword tip: Linear Learner is appropriate for large-scale problems with predominantly linear relationships or as a quick baseline model; when the scenario prioritizes predictive accuracy on tabular data with complex feature interactions, XGBoost is the stronger SageMaker built-in choice.
Correct answer
XGBoost (eXtreme Gradient Boosting)
Explanation
Correct. XGBoost (eXtreme Gradient Boosting) is a supervised learning algorithm based on gradient-boosted decision trees that is specifically designed to handle tabular data and supports binary classification, multiclass classification, regression, and ranking tasks. It is widely recognized for its robust handling of diverse feature types and distributions, its rich set of tunable hyperparameters, and its consistently strong performance on structured datasets such as those containing guest demographics and booking history. XGBoost is the canonical Amazon SageMaker built-in algorithm for churn prediction use cases and is explicitly documented as such in AWS ML guidance. Keyword tip: for tabular data + supervised learning + binary classification in Amazon SageMaker, XGBoost is the primary built-in algorithm to select, particularly when the dataset contains mixed feature types and potential non-linear relationships.
K-Means
Explanation
Incorrect. K-Means is an unsupervised learning algorithm used for clustering tasks. It attempts to find discrete groupings within data by minimizing intra-cluster variance, but it does not use labeled output variables and therefore cannot perform supervised learning or binary classification. Using K-Means for churn prediction would be fundamentally misaligned with the problem objective, since predicting a known label (churn = yes/no) requires a supervised approach. Keyword tip: whenever the scenario requires predicting a labeled binary outcome, eliminate unsupervised algorithms such as K-Means and Principal Component Analysis (PCA) immediately, as they lack the ability to learn from ground-truth labels.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of Amazon SageMaker built-in algorithms and how to match the algorithm to the ML problem type.

It focuses on selecting XGBoost when the scenario describes supervised binary classification on structured tabular data.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    BlazingText: This option is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker BlazingText is designed for NLP workloads such as text classification and Word2vec embeddings. It is not the right algorithm for structured tabular data such as customer demographics, booking history, or transaction records.

    K-Means: This option is incorrect. K-Means is an unsupervised clustering algorithm. It does not use labeled target variables, so it cannot directly solve a supervised binary classification problem such as churn prediction.

    XGBoost: This is the correct option. XGBoost is a supervised gradient-boosted tree algorithm that works very well with structured tabular datasets. It supports binary classification, multiclass classification, regression, and ranking.

    Linear Learner: This option is technically possible for binary classification, but it is not the best fit here. Linear Learner is based on linear models, while churn prediction often involves non-linear relationships and feature interactions. XGBoost is usually the stronger built-in choice for this type of tabular classification problem.

Why XGBoost fits this scenario.

The scenario describes a labeled dataset.

The target is a binary outcome, such as churn or no churn.

The data is structured and tabular.

That points to a supervised classification algorithm.

XGBoost is one of the strongest Amazon SageMaker built-in algorithms for this pattern.

It uses gradient-boosted decision trees.

This makes it effective for tabular datasets with numerical, categorical, and engineered features.

It can capture non-linear relationships between features, such as the interaction between booking frequency, customer profile, loyalty status, purchase behavior, or cancellation history.

K-Means does not fit because it is unsupervised.

BlazingText does not fit because it is for text-based NLP workloads.

Linear Learner can be used as a baseline, but it is less flexible when the data contains complex non-linear patterns.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Labeled data: Use a supervised algorithm.

    Binary classification: Use XGBoost for churn prediction.

    Structured tabular data: Prefer tree-based models over NLP algorithms.

    Non-linear feature interactions: Use gradient-boosted decision trees.

    SageMaker-native implementation: Use the built-in Amazon SageMaker XGBoost algorithm.

    Avoid wrong learning paradigms: Do not choose unsupervised algorithms for labeled prediction tasks.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Structured tabular data + labeled outcome + binary classification + SageMaker built-in algorithm = XGBoost.

Remember:

    XGBoost: Strong choice for supervised tabular classification and regression.

    K-Means: Unsupervised clustering, not churn prediction.

    BlazingText: NLP and text classification.

    Linear Learner: Linear classification or regression baseline.

    Binary classification: Predicts one of two classes.

    Churn prediction: Classic supervised tabular ML use case.

Official AWS references.

    Amazon SageMaker XGBoost algorithm.

    Build, tune, and deploy an end-to-end churn prediction model using Amazon SageMaker Pipelines.

Key exam terms: XGBoost, Amazon SageMaker built-in algorithms, binary classification, tabular data, supervised learning, gradient-boosted decision trees, churn prediction, K-Means unsupervised.
