Question 10Skipped

You are a data scientist working on a clinical trial eligibility scoring model for GenesisPharma Research, a pharmaceutical research organization.


The model predicts whether a patient application should be approved or rejected for enrollment in a clinical trial based on various features such as biomarker levels, medical history, and prior treatment outcomes.


The organization is particularly concerned about ensuring that the model is fair and does not discriminate against any demographic group, such as age, gender, or ethnicity. To address this, you need to select the appropriate evaluation metrics to assess both the model's performance and any potential bias. 

Given these requirements, which combination of evaluation metrics and bias detection methods is MOST APPROPRIATE for ensuring fair and accurate model predictions?
Measure the model's performance using accuracy and AUC-ROC, and check for bias by comparing feature distributions across demographic groups
Explanation
Incorrect. Accuracy is not a sufficient performance metric when the dataset is imbalanced, as it can remain high even when the model systematically misclassifies a minority class. Moreover, comparing feature distributions across demographic groups is a data-level exploratory check that does not reveal whether the trained model produces biased predictions for those groups — a model can pass this check and still yield disparate outcomes. On the MLA-C01 exam, distinguish between data-level bias checks (feature distributions) and model-level fairness metrics (true positive rate parity, equal opportunity); the latter are required when assessing model fairness in high-stakes regulated settings. Keyword tip: 'Feature distribution comparison' signals a preprocessing-stage check, not a post-training fairness metric — use this distinction to eliminate distractors that pair it with accuracy on regulated-domain questions.
Use accuracy as the primary evaluation metric and perform feature importance analysis to ensure that the model's decisions are driven by relevant features
Explanation
Incorrect. Accuracy alone is a misleading metric in scenarios with class imbalance, which is common in clinical trial eligibility datasets where rejections may far outnumber approvals. Furthermore, feature importance analysis explains which features drive predictions but does not directly measure whether the model treats demographic groups equitably — it is an input-level explanation, not an outcome-level fairness measure. On the MLA-C01 exam, when fairness and bias are explicit requirements, look for answers that combine robust performance metrics (F1, AUC-ROC) with outcome-level fairness measures (equal true positive rates, equal opportunity) rather than input-level explanations. Keyword tip: When you see 'fairness' and 'demographic groups' in a question, eliminate any option that relies solely on accuracy or feature importance as the bias detection mechanism.
Correct answer
Evaluate the model using F1 score and AUC-ROC, and assess whether the model has similar true positive rates across different demographic groups
Explanation
Correct. The F1 score balances precision and recall, making it robust for imbalanced datasets such as clinical trial eligibility applications where one class may dominate. AUC-ROC evaluates model discrimination across all classification thresholds, providing a threshold-independent view of performance. Assessing equal true positive rates — also known as equal opportunity — across demographic groups is a direct, outcome-level fairness metric that identifies whether the model consistently benefits or disadvantages specific groups, a critical requirement in regulated, high-stakes biomedical decision-making contexts. Amazon SageMaker Clarify supports this type of post-training bias analysis through metrics such as Conditional Demographic Disparity in Labels (CDDL). Keyword tip: The combination of F1 + AUC-ROC for performance and true positive rate parity (equal opportunity) for fairness is the pattern to recognize when a question specifies imbalanced data, regulated use cases, and demographic non-discrimination requirements.
Evaluate the model using F1 score and AUC-ROC, and check for bias by comparing feature distributions across demographic groups
Explanation
Incorrect. F1 score and AUC-ROC are appropriate performance metrics for an imbalanced classification problem; however, comparing feature distributions across demographic groups is a preprocessing or exploratory step, not a direct measure of model bias. A model can exhibit nearly identical feature distributions across groups yet still produce disparate approval or rejection rates for those groups after training. On the MLA-C01 exam, this option is a carefully constructed distractor because it uses the correct performance metrics but pairs them with an insufficient, data-level bias detection method rather than an outcome-level fairness metric. Keyword tip: Correct performance metrics paired with an incorrect fairness method is a classic MLA-C01 distractor pattern — always verify that the bias detection mechanism operates at the model output level, not just the input data level.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests the ability to select the right model evaluation and fairness metrics for a high-stakes, regulated classification problem.

It focuses on recognizing that accuracy is weak for imbalanced datasets, and that fairness must be assessed using model outcome metrics, not only input feature comparisons.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Use accuracy as the primary evaluation metric and perform feature importance analysis to ensure that the model's decisions are driven by relevant features: This option is incorrect. Accuracy can be misleading when classes are imbalanced. Feature importance explains which inputs influence predictions, but it does not directly measure whether outcomes are fair across demographic groups.

    Measure the model's performance using accuracy and AUC-ROC, and check for bias by comparing feature distributions across demographic groups: This option is incorrect. AUC-ROC is useful, but accuracy is still not enough for imbalanced classification. Also, comparing feature distributions is a data-level check, not a direct model fairness metric.

    Evaluate the model using F1 score and AUC-ROC, and check for bias by comparing feature distributions across demographic groups: This option is partially correct but still incorrect overall. F1 score and AUC-ROC are appropriate performance metrics for imbalanced classification, but feature distribution comparison does not directly show whether the trained model produces fair outcomes.

    Evaluate the model using F1 score and AUC-ROC, and assess whether the model has similar true positive rates across different demographic groups: This is the correct option. F1 score handles imbalance better than accuracy by balancing precision and recall. AUC-ROC evaluates discrimination across thresholds. Comparing true positive rates across demographic groups checks outcome-level fairness.

Why F1 score, AUC-ROC, and true positive rate parity fit this scenario.

The scenario describes a regulated classification problem.

The dataset is imbalanced.

The model must be evaluated for both performance and fairness.

That requires two types of metrics.

First, the model needs robust classification metrics.

F1 score is useful when one class is much more frequent than the other.

It balances precision and recall, making it better than accuracy for imbalanced datasets.

AUC-ROC evaluates how well the model separates classes across different classification thresholds.

Second, the model needs an outcome-level fairness check.

Comparing feature distributions across demographic groups is not enough.

A dataset can look balanced at the input level while the trained model still produces unfair outcomes.

Assessing whether the model has similar true positive rates across demographic groups measures whether qualified individuals are being correctly identified at comparable rates.

This is related to the fairness concept known as equal opportunity.

In AWS, Amazon SageMaker Clarify supports bias analysis and can help evaluate fairness-related metrics during ML governance workflows.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Imbalanced classification: Use F1 score instead of relying on accuracy.

    Threshold-aware evaluation: Use AUC-ROC.

    Regulated decision-making: Include fairness assessment.

    Outcome-level bias detection: Compare true positive rates across demographic groups.

    Avoid input-only checks: Do not rely only on feature distribution comparison.

    ML governance support: Use Amazon SageMaker Clarify for bias analysis workflows.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Imbalanced classification + regulated use case + demographic fairness = F1 score, AUC-ROC, and true positive rate parity.

Remember:

    Accuracy: Often misleading with imbalanced datasets.

    F1 score: Balances precision and recall.

    AUC-ROC: Measures class separation across thresholds.

    True positive rate parity: Checks whether groups receive comparable correct positive outcomes.

    Equal opportunity: Fairness concept based on similar true positive rates across groups.

    Feature distribution comparison: Data-level check, not enough for post-training fairness.

    Amazon SageMaker Clarify: Supports bias detection and explainability workflows.

Official AWS references.

    Conditional Demographic Disparity in Labels in Amazon SageMaker Clarify.

    Learn how Amazon SageMaker Clarify helps detect bias.

Key exam terms: F1 score, AUC-ROC, class imbalance, equal opportunity, true positive rate parity, Amazon SageMaker Clarify, post-training bias metrics, outcome-level fairness.

Question 11Skipped

A data engineer at a regional banking institution, NorthBridge Financial, is tasked with preparing datasets for an ML project.


The datasets contain missing values, duplicate records, and outliers.


The data engineer must clean and consolidate these datasets into a single data frame, ensuring the data is properly prepared for training a machine learning model.


What is the most efficient approach to address these requirements?


Manually import and merge the datasets, then save the consolidated data to an Amazon S3 bucket. Use Amazon Athena to perform data transformations, and leverage its integration with Amazon QuickSight for visualizing the transformed data.
Explanation
Incorrect. Manually importing and merging datasets before saving to Amazon S3 is an inefficient, error-prone process that does not scale for enterprise banking workloads. While Amazon Athena can execute SQL-based transformations against data stored in S3 and Amazon QuickSight provides visualization capabilities, this multi-step manual workflow requires orchestrating disparate services and lacks the built-in ML-specific data quality checks needed for model training preparation. This approach is far less efficient than a purpose-built, integrated ML data preparation tool. Keyword tip: When a question describes manual or multi-tool workflows for ML data cleaning, prefer managed, low-code solutions designed specifically for ML data preparation over ad hoc combinations of query and visualization services.
Use the Amazon SageMaker Python SDK to consolidate these datasets into a single pandas data frame and run data transformations on it.
Explanation
Incorrect. While using the SageMaker Python SDK with pandas is technically feasible for data consolidation and transformation, it requires substantial custom coding for each individual cleaning operation — missing value imputation, duplicate removal, and outlier handling — with no built-in automation or visual data quality reporting. This approach demands significantly more engineering effort than Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler, which provides pre-built visual transforms and automated quality insights for all these tasks out of the box. Keyword tip: When a question emphasizes efficiency and low-code development for ML data preparation within SageMaker, prefer Data Wrangler over custom SDK-based pandas implementations.
Utilize Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth to import and consolidate the datasets into a single data frame. Employ the human-in-the-loop functionality to clean and prepare the data for further processing.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth is designed for building high-quality labeled training datasets by routing annotation tasks to human workforces sourced from Amazon Mechanical Turk, AWS-managed vendors, or private internal teams. It is not suited for programmatic data cleaning tasks such as removing duplicate records, imputing missing values, or detecting statistical outliers in structured banking datasets. Applying a human-in-the-loop process to automated data cleansing would be both costly and operationally impractical at scale. Keyword tip: Ground Truth is associated with data labeling and annotation workflows; Data Wrangler is associated with data transformation, cleaning, and preprocessing.
Correct answer
Leverage Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler to import the datasets and merge them into a unified data frame. Utilize Data Wrangler's transformation functionalities to clean and preprocess the data.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler is a feature of Amazon SageMaker Studio that provides an end-to-end, low-code solution for importing, merging, transforming, featurizing, and analyzing data. It supports consolidating multiple datasets into a unified data frame, and its Data Quality and Insights Report automatically identifies missing values, outliers, and duplicate rows without requiring custom code. Built-in transforms such as 'Drop duplicates' under 'Manage rows' directly address the data quality issues described, making Data Wrangler the most efficient and purpose-built choice for this scenario. Keyword tip: When a scenario involves cleaning missing values, duplicates, and outliers with minimal coding inside a SageMaker-integrated environment, SageMaker Data Wrangler is the correct answer.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of AWS-managed data preparation services and their correct use cases in an ML workflow.

It focuses on choosing Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler when the requirement is to import, merge, clean, and preprocess datasets with minimal coding effort.

It is tied to the Data Preparation for Machine Learning (ML) domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Manually import and merge the datasets, then save the consolidated data to an Amazon S3 bucket. Use Amazon Athena to perform data transformations, and leverage its integration with Amazon QuickSight for visualizing the transformed data: This option is inefficient and operationally fragmented. Amazon Athena is useful for SQL querying over data in Amazon S3, and Amazon QuickSight is useful for visualization, but this approach requires manual merging and lacks integrated ML-specific data quality checks.

    Leverage Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler to import the datasets and merge them into a unified data frame. Utilize Data Wrangler's transformation functionalities to clean and preprocess the data: This is the correct option. SageMaker Data Wrangler provides a low-code environment for importing, joining, cleaning, transforming, analyzing, and preparing data for ML training. It includes built-in capabilities for missing values, duplicate records, outliers, and data quality analysis.

    Utilize Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth to import and consolidate the datasets into a single data frame. Employ the human-in-the-loop functionality to clean and prepare the data for further processing: SageMaker Ground Truth is used for data labeling and annotation workflows. It is not designed for structured data cleaning tasks such as duplicate removal, missing value handling, or outlier detection.

    Use the Amazon SageMaker Python SDK to consolidate these datasets into a single pandas data frame and run data transformations on it: This approach is technically possible, but it requires custom code and manual implementation of cleaning logic. It does not provide the same low-code workflow, visual interface, or automated data quality insights as SageMaker Data Wrangler.

Why SageMaker Data Wrangler fits this scenario.

The scenario requires data preparation before model training.

The datasets need to be imported and merged.

They contain missing values, duplicate records, and outliers.

The goal is to clean and consolidate everything into a single data frame efficiently.

That points directly to Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler.

Data Wrangler is designed for ML data preparation inside the SageMaker ecosystem.

It provides a visual, low-code interface for data import, joins, transformations, feature engineering, and analysis.

Its Data Quality and Insights Report can help identify common issues such as missing values, duplicate rows, and outliers.

Built-in transforms can then be used to clean the dataset without building a custom pandas or PySpark pipeline from scratch.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Import multiple datasets: Use Data Wrangler data import capabilities.

    Merge into one data frame: Use Data Wrangler joins and transformations.

    Handle missing values: Use built-in cleaning transforms.

    Remove duplicates: Use built-in duplicate handling.

    Detect outliers: Use Data Wrangler analysis and data quality insights.

    Minimize coding effort: Use a managed low-code preprocessing tool.

Question 12Skipped

A water utility company, AquaFlow Systems, is training a machine learning model in Amazon SageMaker to detect anomalies in water distribution network sensor readings that may indicate pipe failures or unauthorized usage.


The company has observed that training jobs are consuming a significant amount of computational resources, leading to increased energy costs. As a utility provider with strict sustainability mandates, AquaFlow Systems wants to adopt practices that reduce energy usage and optimize training efficiency while maintaining model accuracy. 


Which actions will reduce the energy usage and computational resources associated with the company's training jobs? (Select TWO.)


Correct selection
Use AWS Trainium-based instances for training the ML model to improve energy efficiency and reduce costs with optimized deep learning hardware
Explanation
Correct. AWS Trainium is purpose-built silicon designed specifically for deep learning training workloads. Trainium-based instances (e.g., trn1) deliver high-performance training at significantly lower energy consumption compared to traditional GPU-based instances, because the hardware is architected to accelerate the tensor and matrix operations that dominate neural network training. This directly aligns with AquaFlow Systems' sustainability mandates and cost-reduction goals without sacrificing model accuracy on sensor anomaly detection. Keyword tip: whenever an MLA-C01 scenario mentions energy efficiency, sustainability mandates, or cost optimization for training deep learning models, AWS Trainium is the primary hardware-level solution to evaluate.
Correct selection
Leverage Amazon SageMaker Debugger to monitor training jobs for non-converging conditions and terminate jobs early to save computational resources
Explanation
Correct. Amazon SageMaker Debugger provides real-time visibility into training jobs and can automatically detect non-converging conditions such as vanishing gradients, exploding gradients, or stagnant loss values. By configuring built-in rules that trigger early stopping when a training job is unlikely to converge to a useful model, SageMaker Debugger prevents prolonged execution of wasteful training runs, directly reducing both computational resource consumption and energy costs for AquaFlow Systems. Keyword tip: in MLA-C01 scenarios combining training efficiency with sustainability, SageMaker Debugger's early-stopping capability via built-in rules is the go-to software-level optimization strategy to distinguish from hardware-level solutions like Trainium.
Increase the instance size for training jobs to ensure that each job uses more resources for faster execution and reduced energy consumption
Explanation
Incorrect. Increasing the instance size does not inherently reduce energy consumption. Over-provisioning compute resources leads to wasted capacity and higher energy usage rather than improved efficiency. The correct approach for AquaFlow Systems is to right-size instances or adopt purpose-built, energy-efficient hardware such as AWS Trainium, which is optimized specifically for the matrix operations that dominate deep learning workloads. Keyword tip: in MLA-C01 scenarios asking about resource efficiency or sustainability, options suggesting 'larger instances for speed' are typically distractors that conflate raw compute power with overall energy optimization.
Disable distributed training to reduce energy consumption by training models on a single instance
Explanation
Incorrect. Disabling distributed training does not reduce total energy consumption — it increases the wall-clock time required to complete a training job, which typically results in equal or higher cumulative energy usage. Distributed training spreads the workload across multiple instances and can complete jobs significantly faster, thereby reducing overall energy expenditure per training run. Keyword tip: in MLA-C01 sustainability questions, 'disable distributed training' is a distractor that conflates per-instance resource usage with the total energy consumed across the full job duration.
Increase the size of the training dataset to ensure the model has more data to process, reducing the likelihood of non-convergence
Explanation
Incorrect. Increasing the training dataset size may improve model generalization but will substantially increase the volume of data processed per epoch and extend overall training duration, leading to greater computational resource consumption and higher energy costs — the opposite of what AquaFlow Systems requires. This option confuses the goal of improving model accuracy with the goal of reducing resource utilization, which are often in tension. Keyword tip: in MLA-C01 questions focused on resource reduction and sustainability, options that expand input data volume are distractors that conflate accuracy improvement with efficiency optimization.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of sustainable ML training strategies on AWS.

It focuses on combining hardware-level efficiency with workflow-level training optimization: AWS Trainium for efficient deep learning training and Amazon SageMaker Debugger for detecting inefficient or non-converging training jobs.

It is tied to the Deployment and Orchestration of ML Workflows domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Increase the instance size for training jobs to ensure that each job uses more resources for faster execution and reduced energy consumption: This option is incorrect. Larger instances do not automatically reduce energy consumption. Over-provisioning can waste capacity and increase total cost. Sustainable training requires right-sized or purpose-built hardware, not simply bigger instances.

    Disable distributed training to reduce energy consumption by training models on a single instance: This option is incorrect. Disabling distributed training can increase wall-clock training time. Longer training duration can consume equal or greater total resources, especially for large deep learning workloads.

    Use AWS Trainium-based instances for training the ML model to improve energy efficiency and reduce costs with optimized deep learning hardware: This is a correct option. AWS Trainium is purpose-built for deep learning training. It powers Amazon EC2 Trn1 instances and is designed to improve training performance and cost efficiency for large-scale models.

    Leverage Amazon SageMaker Debugger to monitor training jobs for non-converging conditions and terminate jobs early to save computational resources: This is a correct option. Amazon SageMaker Debugger can monitor training jobs and detect issues such as non-convergence, vanishing gradients, exploding gradients, or stagnant loss. Stopping bad runs early reduces wasted compute.

    Increase the size of the training dataset to ensure the model has more data to process, reducing the likelihood of non-convergence: This option is incorrect. More data can sometimes improve model quality, but it also increases training time and compute consumption. It does not directly address sustainability or resource efficiency.

Why Trainium and SageMaker Debugger fit this scenario.

The scenario requires reducing cost and improving sustainability during ML training.

That requires optimization at two levels.

The first level is hardware.

AWS Trainium is designed specifically for deep learning training workloads.

It accelerates the tensor and matrix operations used in neural network training.

Using Trainium-based instances can improve cost efficiency compared with general-purpose GPU choices when the workload is compatible.

The second level is training workflow management.

Amazon SageMaker Debugger helps monitor training behavior in real time.

It can detect inefficient or unhealthy training patterns such as stagnant loss, exploding gradients, vanishing gradients, or jobs that are unlikely to converge.

When a training job is not making useful progress, stopping it early avoids wasting compute, time, and energy.

Together, these two approaches reduce resource waste without simply scaling up hardware or increasing the amount of data processed.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Hardware-level efficiency: Use AWS Trainium.

    Training workload optimization: Use purpose-built ML accelerators.

    Workflow-level efficiency: Use Amazon SageMaker Debugger.

    Avoid wasteful runs: Stop non-converging jobs early.

    Reduce training cost: Improve utilization and avoid unnecessary compute time.

    Support sustainability goals: Reduce unnecessary energy consumption across training runs.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Sustainable ML training + cost reduction + deep learning = AWS Trainium + Amazon SageMaker Debugger.

Remember:

    AWS Trainium: Purpose-built accelerator for deep learning training.

    Amazon EC2 Trn1: Instance family powered by AWS Trainium.

    Amazon SageMaker Debugger: Monitors training behavior and detects training issues.

    Early stopping: Prevents long-running wasteful jobs.

    Larger instances: Not automatically more sustainable.

Question 13Skipped

A weather and climate research agency, AtmoSense Research, has collected historical data containing information about research stations that required extended technical intervention from the data engineering support team.


The organization now seeks to build an ML model to forecast whether future research stations will need long-term support.


  What approach should the organization take to build and implement this predictive model?



Correct answer
Logistic regression
Explanation
Correct. Logistic regression is a supervised learning algorithm designed for binary or multi-class classification problems — precisely the type of problem described here, where the outcome is whether a research station will or will not require long-term support. It models the relationship between input features (e.g., ticket frequency, issue severity, resolution time) and a discrete outcome using a sigmoid function, producing a probability score that maps to a class label. Amazon SageMaker's built-in Linear Learner algorithm supports a classification objective and is the natural AWS-native implementation for logistic regression at scale. Keyword tip: when a scenario explicitly describes labeled historical data and a binary yes/no prediction target, logistic regression (or any classification algorithm) is the correct choice over regression or unsupervised methods.
Linear regression
Explanation
Incorrect. Linear regression is a supervised learning algorithm used to predict continuous numeric values — such as temperature readings, precipitation levels, or equipment uptime duration — by modeling a linear relationship between independent and dependent variables. Because the target variable in this scenario is binary (whether a research station will or will not need long-term support), linear regression is fundamentally inappropriate; it does not produce class probabilities or categorical outputs and can generate predictions outside the valid probability range [0, 1]. In MLA-C01, the decisive differentiator between linear and logistic regression is the nature of the output: continuous numeric output → linear regression; discrete categorical output → logistic regression. Keyword tip: any scenario describing a yes/no, will/will-not, or belongs-to-class-A/B target variable eliminates linear regression as a valid option.
Anomaly Detection
Explanation
Incorrect. Anomaly Detection is an unsupervised technique used to identify rare or unusual data points that deviate significantly from an established pattern, and it is most relevant in use cases such as fraud detection, network intrusion monitoring, or equipment fault alerting. It is not suited for predicting a known categorical outcome from labeled historical data, because it has no mechanism to learn from explicit class labels. In this scenario, AtmoSense Research has labeled records indicating which stations required extended support, making supervised classification the appropriate paradigm. Keyword tip: when labeled training data and a defined binary prediction target are explicitly stated, anomaly detection is always an incorrect choice on the MLA-C01 exam.
Principal Component Analysis (PCA) Algorithm
Explanation
Incorrect. Principal Component Analysis (PCA) is an unsupervised dimensionality reduction algorithm that transforms a high-dimensional feature space into a smaller set of uncorrelated principal components while preserving maximum variance. PCA does not predict outcomes or class labels; it is used as a preprocessing or data exploration step, not as a standalone predictive model. Because AtmoSense Research holds labeled historical data and needs to forecast a binary outcome, a supervised classification algorithm is required — not a dimensionality reduction technique. Keyword tip: PCA appears as a distractor in MLA-C01 scenarios where the task clearly involves supervised classification with an existing labeled dataset.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests the ability to select the correct ML algorithm type based on the prediction target.

It focuses on distinguishing classification from regression and identifying logistic regression when the target is binary or categorical.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Logistic regression: This is the correct option. Logistic regression is a supervised learning algorithm used for binary or multi-class classification. It is appropriate when the model must predict a categorical outcome, such as yes/no, true/false, or will/will not.

    Principal Component Analysis algorithm: This option is incorrect. Principal Component Analysis (PCA) is an unsupervised dimensionality reduction technique. It reduces feature space, but it does not predict labels or classify outcomes.

    Anomaly Detection: This option is incorrect. Anomaly detection identifies unusual or rare patterns in data. It is not the right choice when the dataset is labeled and the task is to predict a known binary outcome.

    Linear regression: This option is incorrect. Linear regression predicts continuous numerical values. It is not appropriate for a binary target variable because the output must be a class or probability, not an unrestricted numeric value.

Why logistic regression fits this scenario.

The scenario describes historical labeled data.

The target variable is binary.

The model must predict whether something will or will not happen.

That points to a supervised classification algorithm.

Logistic regression is designed for this type of problem.

It estimates the probability that an input belongs to a class.

For example, it can predict whether a research station will require long-term support based on historical support records, issue severity, ticket frequency, resolution time, or operational signals.

This is different from linear regression.

Linear regression is used when the output is continuous, such as cost, duration, temperature, or demand volume.

It is also different from PCA and anomaly detection because those are unsupervised techniques and do not learn directly from labeled outcomes.

In AWS, Amazon SageMaker Linear Learner can be used for both classification and regression objectives, making it relevant for implementing this kind of supervised classification workflow.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Labeled historical data: Use supervised learning.

    Binary target: Use classification.

    Yes/no prediction: Use logistic regression.

    Probability output: Produce a class probability before assigning a label.

    Avoid regression mismatch: Do not use linear regression for categorical targets.

    Avoid unsupervised distractors: Do not choose PCA or anomaly detection for labeled classification.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Labeled data + binary yes/no target = logistic regression or another classification algorithm.

Remember:

    Logistic regression: Classification for categorical outcomes.

    Linear regression: Regression for continuous numeric outcomes.

    PCA: Dimensionality reduction, not prediction.

    Anomaly detection: Finds unusual records, not known class labels.

    Supervised learning: Uses labeled examples.

    Binary classification: Predicts one of two classes.

    Amazon SageMaker Linear Learner: Supports classification and regression objectives.

Official AWS references.

    What is linear regression?

    What is anomaly detection?

Key exam terms: logistic regression, binary classification, supervised learning, Amazon SageMaker Linear Learner, labeled historical data, categorical target variable, PCA dimensionality reduction, Amazon SageMaker built-in algorithms.

Question 14Skipped

A pharmaceutical research organization trained a clinical trial outcome forecasting ML model by preprocessing the training data using AWS Glue DataBrew.


During preprocessing, the ML engineer used z-score normalization to standardize the training data.

Now, PharmaGen Analytics needs to preprocess real-time assay and compound consumption data for inference by applying the same z-score normalization parameters as the training data to ensure consistency in predictions. 


Which solution will meet this requirement? 



Correct answer
Export the z-score normalization recipe from AWS Glue DataBrew and apply the same recipe to preprocess the real-time assay data for inference.
Explanation
Correct. AWS Glue DataBrew allows recipes to be exported and reapplied to new datasets without recomputing transformation statistics. The RESCALE_OUTLIERS_WITH_Z_SCORE recipe action captures the exact normalization parameters — mean (μ) and standard deviation (σ) — derived from the training data. By exporting and reapplying this recipe to the real-time assay data, PharmaGen Analytics ensures the identical transformation is applied at inference time, preserving the statistical alignment between training and serving inputs that is required for consistent model predictions. Keyword tip: 'export and reapply DataBrew recipe' is the canonical AWS pattern for enforcing preprocessing parameter consistency between training and inference stages.
Apply a SageMaker Processing job to perform z-score normalization on the real-time assay data and automatically derive new parameters.
Explanation
Incorrect. A SageMaker Processing job configured to automatically derive new normalization parameters will compute mean and standard deviation from the real-time assay data rather than from the original training dataset. This breaks the training-serving consistency required for correct inference, as the model receives inputs scaled to a different statistical distribution than it learned from, causing skewed or incorrect predictions. Keyword tip: 'automatically derive new parameters' during inference preprocessing is a distractor pattern indicating a failure to reuse training-time statistics — the key distinction from the correct export-and-reapply approach.
Use AWS Lambda to create a custom function for z-score normalization that calculates new parameters for each batch of assay data.
Explanation
Incorrect. Computing new normalization parameters for each incoming batch of data produces different scaling per batch and introduces statistical inconsistencies relative to the training data distribution. At inference time, the model would receive inputs scaled with a different mean and standard deviation than it was trained on, leading to unreliable and potentially biased predictions. Keyword tip: 'calculates new parameters for each batch' is a strong distractor signal indicating a feature preprocessing consistency violation, regardless of whether AWS Lambda or another compute service is used.
Manually calculate the z-score normalization parameters again for the real-time assay data and use these parameters during inference preprocessing.
Explanation
Incorrect. Manually recalculating z-score normalization parameters using the real-time assay data introduces train-serve skew, because the mean and standard deviation derived from the inference dataset will differ from those derived from the training dataset. This directly violates the requirement to reuse the same normalization parameters established during training, resulting in inconsistently scaled model inputs and degraded prediction reliability. Keyword tip: whenever you see 'manually recalculate parameters for new data,' recognize this as a train-serve consistency violation in AWS Glue DataBrew preprocessing workflows.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests the MLOps principle of training-serving consistency in feature preprocessing.

It focuses on ensuring that z-score normalization parameters, such as mean and standard deviation, are calculated from the training data and then reused unchanged during inference.

It is tied to the Data Preparation for Machine Learning (ML) domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Manually calculate the z-score normalization parameters again for the real-time assay data and use these parameters during inference preprocessing: This option is incorrect. Recalculating mean and standard deviation from real-time inference data creates a different scaling distribution from the one used during training. That introduces train-serve skew.

    Use AWS Lambda to create a custom function for z-score normalization that calculates new parameters for each batch of assay data: This option is incorrect. AWS Lambda could run preprocessing code, but calculating new parameters for each batch breaks preprocessing consistency. The model would receive inputs scaled with different statistics than the training data.

    Export the z-score normalization recipe from AWS Glue DataBrew and apply the same recipe to preprocess the real-time assay data for inference: This is the correct option. AWS Glue DataBrew recipes can be exported and reapplied to new datasets. Reusing the original recipe preserves the same training-time normalization behavior during inference.

    Apply a SageMaker Processing job to perform z-score normalization on the real-time assay data and automatically derive new parameters: This option is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Processing can perform preprocessing, but automatically deriving new parameters from inference data violates training-serving consistency.

Why exporting and reapplying the DataBrew recipe fits this scenario.

The model was trained using z-score normalization.

Z-score normalization depends on two parameters:

    Mean.

    Standard deviation.

These parameters must be computed from the training dataset.

At inference time, the same parameters must be reused.

If the system recalculates mean and standard deviation from real-time assay data, the model receives features on a different scale than the one it learned during training.

That creates train-serve skew.

AWS Glue DataBrew supports repeatable data preparation through recipes.

By exporting the original z-score normalization recipe and applying it to the real-time assay data, the team preserves the same transformation logic used during training.

This keeps training and inference preprocessing aligned.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Training-serving consistency: Reuse the same preprocessing recipe.

    Z-score normalization: Preserve the training-time mean and standard deviation.

    No parameter recomputation: Do not derive new statistics from inference data.

    Repeatable preprocessing: Export and reapply the AWS Glue DataBrew recipe.

    Reliable inference: Keep feature scaling aligned with the model's training distribution.

    Avoid train-serve skew: Use the same transformation behavior across training and serving.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Training preprocessing must match inference preprocessing = export and reapply the original AWS Glue DataBrew recipe.

Remember:

    Z-score normalization: Uses mean and standard deviation.

    Training statistics: Must come from the training dataset.

    Inference data: Should be transformed using the training-time parameters.

    Train-serve skew: Happens when training and inference transformations differ.

    AWS Glue DataBrew recipe: Reusable transformation definition.

    RESCALE_OUTLIERS_WITH_Z_SCORE: DataBrew recipe action related to z-score-based rescaling.

    Recomputing parameters at inference: Incorrect when consistency is required.

Official AWS references.

    AWS Glue DataBrew RESCALE_OUTLIERS_WITH_Z_SCORE recipe action.

    Common data preparation transformations in AWS Glue DataBrew.

Key exam terms: AWS Glue DataBrew, recipe export and reapply, z-score normalization, train-serve skew, training-serving consistency, RESCALE_OUTLIERS_WITH_Z_SCORE, preprocessing parameters, inference pipeline.

Question 15Skipped

A data science team at a consumer electronics manufacturer, NovaTech Devices, is onboarding new ML engineers and needs to establish a foundational understanding of how neural networks are trained before deploying models to production.

Which of the following statements best describes how model training works in deep learning? 


Model training in deep learning involves manually setting the weights and biases of a neural network based on predefined rules established by domain experts.
Explanation
Incorrect. Weights and biases in a neural network are not manually assigned by domain experts based on predefined rules. That approach describes rule-based or expert systems, which predate modern machine learning. In deep learning, weights and biases are learned automatically from data through optimization algorithms such as stochastic gradient descent, not hand-crafted. Keyword tip: When a question describes 'manually setting weights' or 'predefined rules' as a training mechanism, this signals a rule-based system distractor that contradicts the learned-parameter nature of neural networks.
Model training in deep learning relies exclusively on ensemble methods such as support vector machines and gradient-boosted decision trees to construct predictive models.
Explanation
Incorrect. Support vector machines (SVMs) and gradient-boosted decision trees are classical machine learning algorithms that are categorically distinct from deep learning. Deep learning specifically refers to multi-layer neural network architectures optimized through gradient-based methods, not ensemble or kernel-based approaches. While SVMs and boosted trees are effective predictive modeling tools, conflating them with deep learning reveals a fundamental misunderstanding of ML taxonomy. Keyword tip: Mixing classical ML algorithms — SVMs, decision trees, boosted ensembles — into a definition of deep learning is a recurring distractor pattern; the presence of 'neural network' and 'multiple hidden layers' distinguishes deep learning from these approaches.
Correct answer
Model training in deep learning involves using large datasets to iteratively adjust the weights and biases of a neural network through techniques such as gradient descent to minimize prediction error.
Explanation
Correct. Deep learning model training feeds large datasets through a neural network and iteratively adjusts weights and biases using optimization techniques such as gradient descent. During each forward pass, the model computes a loss function measuring prediction error; backpropagation then calculates gradients with respect to each parameter, and the optimizer updates the parameters to reduce that error. This cycle repeats across many epochs until the model converges and generalizes well to held-out validation data. Keyword tip: The combination of 'gradient descent,' 'iterative weight updates,' 'backpropagation,' and 'minimize prediction error' are the definitive identifiers for the correct description of deep learning training.
Model training in deep learning requires no input data; the neural network automatically learns from predefined algorithms encoded at initialization.
Explanation
Incorrect. Input data is a fundamental prerequisite for training any deep learning model. Neural networks do not encode knowledge from predefined algorithms at initialization; they begin with randomized weights and must observe labeled or unlabeled examples to update their parameters meaningfully. This option describes a technically impossible scenario for standard supervised or unsupervised deep learning workflows. Keyword tip: Any option claiming a neural network 'requires no data' is an immediate disqualifier in ML training questions, regardless of the surrounding scenario.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests foundational knowledge of how deep learning model training works.

It focuses on recognizing that neural networks learn by iteratively adjusting weights and biases using data, loss functions, backpropagation, and gradient-based optimization.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Model training in deep learning involves manually setting the weights and biases of a neural network based on predefined rules established by domain experts: This option is incorrect. Neural network weights and biases are not manually assigned by experts. They are learned automatically from data during training.

    Model training in deep learning involves using large datasets to iteratively adjust the weights and biases of a neural network through techniques such as gradient descent to minimize prediction error: This is the correct option. Deep learning training uses data, a loss function, backpropagation, and optimization algorithms such as gradient descent to update model parameters and reduce prediction error.

    Model training in deep learning requires no input data; the neural network automatically learns from predefined algorithms encoded at initialization: This option is incorrect. Deep learning models require input data. They usually start with initialized weights and then learn useful patterns by processing examples during training.

    Model training in deep learning relies exclusively on ensemble methods such as support vector machines and gradient-boosted decision trees to construct predictive models: This option is incorrect. Support vector machines and gradient-boosted decision trees are classical ML methods. Deep learning is based on neural network architectures, not SVMs or tree ensembles.

Why iterative weight updates with gradient descent fit this scenario.

Deep learning models are neural networks.

They contain learnable parameters, mainly weights and biases.

Training begins with initialized parameter values.

The model then processes input data through a forward pass.

The forward pass produces predictions.

A loss function compares those predictions with the expected output.

The model then uses backpropagation to calculate how much each parameter contributed to the error.

An optimizer, such as gradient descent or a variant of it, updates the weights and biases to reduce that error.

This process repeats over many batches and epochs.

Over time, the model learns patterns from the data and improves its ability to generalize to unseen examples.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Uses input data: Deep learning requires training examples.

    Learns parameters automatically: Weights and biases are updated from data.

    Minimizes prediction error: Training optimizes a loss function.

    Uses backpropagation: Gradients are computed through the network.

    Uses gradient descent: Parameters are updated iteratively.

    Distinguishes deep learning from classical ML: Neural networks are not the same as SVMs or boosted trees.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Deep learning training = neural network + forward pass + loss function + backpropagation + gradient descent.

Remember:

    Weights and biases: Learnable neural network parameters.

    Loss function: Measures prediction error.

    Forward pass: Produces predictions from inputs.

    Backpropagation: Computes gradients for each parameter.

    Gradient descent: Updates parameters to reduce loss.

    Epoch: One full pass through the training dataset.

    Classical ML distractors: SVMs, decision trees, and boosted trees are not definitions of deep learning.

Official AWS references.

    What is artificial intelligence?

Key exam terms: deep learning, gradient descent, backpropagation, weights and biases, loss function, neural network, iterative training, ML Model Development.

Question 16Skipped

An automotive manufacturer, DriveVision Motors, uses an ML model to generate text descriptions for vehicle damage images uploaded by customers after collision incidents.


Each uploaded image can be as large as 40 MB in size. To cost-effectively handle the inference requests for these uploads, the images are saved in an Amazon S3 bucket.

A data engineer needs to implement a scalable processing solution that can adapt to fluctuations in demand while minimizing operational overhead. 


What is the best solution to meet these requirements?
Create an Amazon SageMaker Real-Time Inference endpoint. Upon each image upload, trigger an AWS Lambda function to make an inference request.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Real-Time Inference is optimized for workloads requiring millisecond latency, but it supports payload sizes of only up to 6 MB and processing times of up to 60 seconds. A 40 MB image payload exceeds the Real-Time Inference payload limit, making this option technically infeasible for this scenario regardless of the Lambda integration pattern. Keyword tip: whenever a payload size exceeds 6 MB, Real-Time Inference must be eliminated from consideration.
Create an Amazon SageMaker Serverless Inference endpoint and a scaling policy. Run a script to make an inference request for each image.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Serverless Inference is designed for workloads with idle periods between traffic spurts and can tolerate cold starts, but it has a maximum request and response payload size of 4 MB. Since each vehicle damage image can be up to 40 MB, this option violates the payload size constraint and is not suitable for this use case. Keyword tip: whenever you see a payload size requirement exceeding 4 MB, immediately eliminate Serverless Inference as a candidate.
Correct answer
Create an Amazon SageMaker Asynchronous Inference endpoint and a scaling policy. Upon each image upload, trigger an AWS Lambda function to make an inference request.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon SageMaker Asynchronous Inference queues incoming requests and processes them asynchronously, supporting payload sizes up to 1 GB and processing times up to one hour, making it well-suited for 40 MB vehicle damage image payloads. It also supports autoscaling the instance count to zero when no requests are being processed, directly addressing the cost-effectiveness and scalability requirements. Triggering an AWS Lambda function on each Amazon S3 upload event seamlessly integrates the upload workflow with the inference endpoint without requiring manual scripts or additional infrastructure management. Keyword tip: large payloads exceeding 6 MB, variable traffic with idle periods, near-real-time latency tolerance, and cost optimization through scale-to-zero autoscaling are the signature indicators for Amazon SageMaker Asynchronous Inference.
Utilize Amazon SageMaker Batch Transform for inference to process the images stored in the Amazon S3 bucket in bulk.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Batch Transform is a cost-effective, managed solution for offline bulk predictions on large datasets stored in Amazon S3. However, it is designed to process data in structured file formats such as CSV or JSON, and native image file formats are not supported as direct Batch Transform input without additional preprocessing. Keyword tip: if the scenario involves direct image file processing rather than structured tabular data, Batch Transform is not the appropriate choice.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests the ability to select the correct Amazon SageMaker inference mode based on payload size, latency tolerance, traffic pattern, cost, and operational overhead.

It focuses on choosing Amazon SageMaker Asynchronous Inference when requests contain large payloads, traffic is variable, and the workload can tolerate asynchronous processing.

It is tied to the Deployment and Orchestration of ML Workflows domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Create an Amazon SageMaker Serverless Inference endpoint and a scaling policy. Run a script to make an inference request for each image: This option is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Serverless Inference is useful for intermittent workloads, but it has a payload size limit that is too small for 40 MB image files.

    Utilize Amazon SageMaker Batch Transform for inference to process the images stored in the Amazon S3 bucket in bulk: This option is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Batch Transform is designed for offline batch inference, commonly with structured input formats. It is not the best fit for event-driven image inference immediately after each upload.

    Create an Amazon SageMaker Asynchronous Inference endpoint and a scaling policy. Upon each image upload, trigger an AWS Lambda function to make an inference request: This is the correct option. Amazon SageMaker Asynchronous Inference supports large payloads, queues requests, processes them asynchronously, and can scale down to zero when idle. AWS Lambda can trigger the inference request after each Amazon S3 upload.

    Create an Amazon SageMaker Real-Time Inference endpoint. Upon each image upload, trigger an AWS Lambda function to make an inference request: This option is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Real-Time Inference is designed for synchronous low-latency requests, but it has a smaller payload limit than required for 40 MB images.

Why SageMaker Asynchronous Inference fits this scenario.

The input payloads are large image files.

Each image can be around 40 MB.

The workload does not require an immediate synchronous response.

Traffic is variable and may include idle periods.

The company also wants cost optimization.

That points to Amazon SageMaker Asynchronous Inference.

Amazon SageMaker Asynchronous Inference is designed for requests with large payloads and longer processing times.

Requests are placed in a queue and processed asynchronously.

The inference results can be written to Amazon S3.

This mode also supports scaling the endpoint instance count down to zero when there are no requests to process.

That makes it cost-effective for variable traffic patterns.

An AWS Lambda function can be triggered by an Amazon S3 object-created event.

The function can then submit the image to the asynchronous inference endpoint.

This creates a low-overhead event-driven inference workflow.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Large payloads: Use Amazon SageMaker Asynchronous Inference.

    40 MB images: Avoid Serverless Inference and Real-Time Inference payload limits.

    Event-driven workflow: Trigger AWS Lambda from Amazon S3 uploads.

    Variable demand: Use asynchronous processing and endpoint auto scaling.

    Cost optimization: Scale endpoint capacity down to zero when idle.

    Minimal manual work: Avoid scripts, polling, or custom orchestration.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Large payloads above real-time limits + variable traffic + cost optimization + asynchronous processing = Amazon SageMaker Asynchronous Inference.

Remember:

    Amazon SageMaker Asynchronous Inference: Large payloads, queued processing, scale-to-zero support.

    Amazon SageMaker Real-Time Inference: Low-latency synchronous requests with smaller payload limits.

    Amazon SageMaker Serverless Inference: Intermittent traffic, cold-start tolerant workloads, smaller payloads.

    Amazon SageMaker Batch Transform: Offline batch inference over datasets.

    AWS Lambda: Event-driven trigger after Amazon S3 uploads.

    Amazon S3: Input and output storage layer for asynchronous inference.

Question 17Skipped

A semiconductor manufacturer, SiliconCore Technologies, operates in a hybrid cloud environment and has deployed a machine learning model on premises to provide personalized supply chain and procurement recommendations.

The model processes data stored in Amazon S3, which includes sensitive supplier and employee information such as Social Security numbers and financial transaction details.


The company must ensure that all sensitive data is identified and removed before it is processed by the model.


The company needs to implement a solution that can handle these requirements with the least operational overhead.  Which solution will meet these requirements?


Correct answer
Use Amazon Macie to automatically identify sensitive data in Amazon S3 and then call an AWS Lambda function to remove the sensitive data.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon Macie is a fully managed data security service purpose-built to automatically discover, classify, and protect sensitive data such as PII and financial information stored in Amazon S3. It requires no custom coding, generates detailed security findings, and integrates natively with Amazon EventBridge, allowing findings to trigger an AWS Lambda function for automated remediation — such as redacting or deleting sensitive objects — before they reach the on-premises model. This architecture provides the lowest operational overhead while satisfying the company's compliance and security requirements. Keyword tip: In MLA-C01 scenarios requiring automated PII detection in Amazon S3 with minimal operational overhead, Amazon Macie paired with AWS Lambda for event-driven remediation is the canonical correct pattern.
Use AWS Glue DataBrew to create a data transformation pipeline that scans for sensitive data and applies predefined rules to clean the dataset.
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS Glue DataBrew provides a visual interface for data preparation and transformation, including some built-in data masking and redaction capabilities. However, it lacks the deep, automated sensitive data discovery capabilities offered by Amazon Macie, which is purpose-built for identifying PII and financial data stored in Amazon S3. DataBrew requires more manual rule configuration and does not generate security findings or integrate natively with event-driven remediation pipelines as effectively as Macie does, resulting in higher operational overhead. Keyword tip: DataBrew is ideal for data cleansing and visual transformation tasks — not for compliance-grade, automated sensitive data discovery with event-driven remediation.
Leverage Amazon Comprehend to classify and redact sensitive entities from the data stored in Amazon S3 before it is used by the model.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon Comprehend can perform named entity recognition and classify sensitive text categories, but it is primarily designed for natural language processing and text analytics workloads, not large-scale automated sensitive data scanning across Amazon S3 buckets. Using Comprehend for this purpose would require substantial additional integration work — such as reading each S3 object, invoking the API per record, and building a custom remediation layer — significantly increasing operational complexity. Keyword tip: Amazon Comprehend is the right choice for NLP tasks such as sentiment analysis, topic modeling, or named entity recognition — not for automated, compliance-grade S3 PII scanning at enterprise scale.
Deploy an AWS Glue ETL job to scan the S3 bucket, detect sensitive data using a custom Python script, and redact the sensitive information.
Explanation
Incorrect. Although AWS Glue ETL jobs can be used for data transformation, this approach requires writing and maintaining custom Python scripts to detect sensitive data, which significantly increases operational overhead. AWS Glue is better suited for general-purpose data transformation rather than automated PII or sensitive data detection at scale. The custom development burden makes this option incompatible with the least-operational-overhead constraint. Keyword tip: When you see 'custom script' or 'custom development' combined with 'least operational overhead,' treat this as a distractor in MLA-C01 security and compliance scenarios.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of AWS security and compliance services for protecting sensitive data in ML pipelines.

It focuses on selecting Amazon Macie when the requirement is to discover sensitive data such as PII in Amazon S3 with the least operational overhead, and then automate remediation using AWS Lambda.

It is tied to the ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Deploy an AWS Glue ETL job to scan the S3 bucket, detect sensitive data using a custom Python script, and redact the sensitive information: This option is incorrect. AWS Glue can run ETL jobs, but using it to scan for sensitive data with custom Python code creates development and maintenance overhead. The scenario asks for the least operational overhead, so a purpose-built managed service is preferred.

    Leverage Amazon Comprehend to classify and redact sensitive entities from the data stored in Amazon S3 before it is used by the model: This option is incorrect. Amazon Comprehend can detect entities in text and support NLP workflows, but it is not the primary service for automated sensitive data discovery across Amazon S3 buckets at scale. It would require additional custom integration and remediation logic.

    Use AWS Glue DataBrew to create a data transformation pipeline that scans for sensitive data and applies predefined rules to clean the dataset: This option is incorrect. AWS Glue DataBrew is useful for visual data preparation and transformation, but it is not the best fit for automated, compliance-oriented sensitive data discovery in Amazon S3. It requires more manual rule configuration than Amazon Macie.

    Use Amazon Macie to automatically identify sensitive data in Amazon S3 and then call an AWS Lambda function to remove the sensitive data: This is the correct option. Amazon Macie is a managed data security service that discovers and classifies sensitive data in Amazon S3. Its findings can trigger automated remediation workflows using Amazon EventBridge and AWS Lambda.

Why Amazon Macie with AWS Lambda fits this scenario.

The data is stored in Amazon S3.

The company needs to detect sensitive information such as PII.

The solution must require the least operational overhead.

That points directly to Amazon Macie.

Amazon Macie is purpose-built to discover and classify sensitive data in Amazon S3.

It can identify categories such as personally identifiable information and financial data without requiring custom scanning logic.

When Amazon Macie generates findings, those findings can be routed through Amazon EventBridge.

Amazon EventBridge can then invoke an AWS Lambda function.

The AWS Lambda function can perform remediation actions, such as removing, quarantining, redacting, or moving sensitive objects before they are used by downstream ML workloads.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Sensitive data discovery: Use Amazon Macie.

    S3-native scanning: Analyze data stored in Amazon S3.

    PII detection: Identify personally identifiable information and other sensitive data.

    Automated remediation: Trigger AWS Lambda from Amazon Macie findings.

    Event-driven architecture: Use Amazon EventBridge for routing findings.

    Least operational overhead: Avoid custom AWS Glue, AWS Glue DataBrew, or Amazon Comprehend pipelines for S3-wide sensitive data scanning.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Sensitive data in Amazon S3 + PII detection + least operational overhead + automated remediation = Amazon Macie + Amazon EventBridge + AWS Lambda.

Remember:

    Amazon Macie: Sensitive data discovery and classification in Amazon S3.

    AWS Lambda: Executes remediation logic.

    Amazon EventBridge: Routes findings to automated workflows.

    AWS Glue: ETL and transformation, not the lowest-overhead PII discovery service.

    AWS Glue DataBrew: Visual data preparation, not compliance-grade S3 discovery.

    Amazon Comprehend: NLP entity detection, not the primary S3 sensitive data scanning service.

Official AWS references.

    Automate the archival and deletion of sensitive data using Amazon Macie.

    Use Macie to discover sensitive data as part of automated data pipelines.

Key exam terms: Amazon Macie, PII detection, Amazon S3 sensitive data scanning, AWS Lambda remediation, Amazon EventBridge, least operational overhead, hybrid cloud ML pipeline, data security compliance.

Question 18Skipped

A petrochemical company, PetroChem Solutions, uses a customer service chatbot powered by a large language model (LLM) through Amazon Bedrock to handle procurement inquiries and safety compliance questions.



Customers have reported that the chatbot provides slightly different answers when asked the same questions about contract cancellation policies or hazardous material handling procedures.



The company needs to ensure the chatbot provides consistent responses.


Which solution will meet these requirements?
Retrain the LLM with a petrochemical-industry-specific dataset to improve consistency in responses related to compliance information and contract cancellation policies
Explanation
Incorrect. Retraining the LLM is a resource-intensive and time-consuming process that addresses model knowledge gaps, not output randomness. Response inconsistency for the same input is caused by stochastic decoding parameters, not by deficiencies in training data. Retraining would not eliminate variability introduced by a high temperature or a large top-K candidate pool. Keyword tip: retraining is the right answer for improving model accuracy or expanding domain knowledge, not for controlling inference-time consistency in Amazon Bedrock deployments.
Adjust the temperature parameter in the Amazon Bedrock API to a higher value and increase the top-K parameter to limit the number of possible tokens the model can choose from, ensuring consistent and controlled responses
Explanation
Incorrect. Increasing the temperature and top-K values has the opposite effect: it introduces more randomness and expands the token candidate pool, which increases response variability rather than reducing it. This approach would make the chatbot less predictable, particularly problematic in a compliance-sensitive petrochemical context where policy accuracy is critical. Keyword tip: high temperature = creative/diverse outputs; low temperature = deterministic/consistent outputs — a common distractor in LLM inference parameter questions on the MLA-C01 exam.
Enable caching in the chatbot application to reuse previously generated responses for frequently asked questions
Explanation
Incorrect. Application-level caching can return identical stored responses for exact query matches, but it does not address the root cause of inconsistency — stochastic inference parameters. Caching fails for paraphrased or slightly varied queries, and cached responses may become stale as compliance policies or contract terms change over time. It is a workaround for latency and cost reduction, not a principled solution for LLM response consistency. Keyword tip: caching reduces latency and cost for repeated identical queries, but it is not a substitute for proper inference parameter tuning in Amazon Bedrock.
Correct answer
Adjust the temperature parameter in the Amazon Bedrock API to a lower value and decrease the top-K parameter to limit the number of possible tokens the model can choose from, ensuring consistent and controlled responses
Explanation
Correct. Lowering the temperature parameter reduces the randomness of token selection — for example, a temperature of 0.2 strongly biases the model toward the highest-probability tokens, producing near-deterministic outputs. Simultaneously decreasing the top-K parameter narrows the pool of candidate tokens from which the model samples, further limiting variability. Together, these two inference parameter adjustments directly address response inconsistency at the source without requiring retraining or architectural changes. Keyword tip: when you see 'consistent responses' or 'reduce randomness' in an Amazon Bedrock inference scenario, think low temperature + low top-K as the least-invasive, most targeted solution.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of Amazon Bedrock inference parameters and how they affect LLM response consistency.

It focuses on reducing output variability by lowering temperature and decreasing top-K.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Adjust the temperature parameter in the Amazon Bedrock API to a lower value and decrease the top-K parameter to limit the number of possible tokens the model can choose from, ensuring consistent and controlled responses: This is the correct option. Lowering temperature reduces randomness in token selection. Decreasing top-K limits the number of candidate tokens the model can sample from. Together, they make responses more consistent and controlled.

    Adjust the temperature parameter in the Amazon Bedrock API to a higher value and increase the top-K parameter to limit the number of possible tokens the model can choose from, ensuring consistent and controlled responses: This option is incorrect. Increasing temperature makes outputs more random. Increasing top-K expands the token candidate pool. Both changes increase variability instead of reducing it.

    Retrain the LLM with a petrochemical-industry-specific dataset to improve consistency in responses related to compliance information and contract cancellation policies: This option is incorrect. Retraining or fine-tuning may help with domain knowledge, but it is not the least-effort fix for inconsistent answers caused by stochastic inference settings.

    Enable caching in the chatbot application to reuse previously generated responses for frequently asked questions: This option is incorrect. Caching can reduce latency and cost for repeated identical prompts, but it does not solve the root cause of output variability. Slightly different prompts may still produce inconsistent responses.

Why lowering temperature and top-K fits this scenario.

The scenario describes an Amazon Bedrock chatbot.

The chatbot is producing inconsistent responses.

The business context requires controlled, predictable answers.

That points to inference parameter tuning.

The temperature parameter controls randomness during token generation.

A lower temperature makes the model more likely to choose the highest-probability tokens.

This produces more deterministic and consistent responses.

The top-K parameter limits how many of the highest-probability tokens are considered during generation.

A lower top-K narrows the model's choices.

That further reduces response variability.

This is better than retraining because the issue is not necessarily missing domain knowledge.

It is better than caching because it addresses the generation behavior directly.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Reduce randomness: Lower temperature.

    Limit token choices: Decrease top-K.

    Improve consistency: Make outputs more deterministic.

    Avoid unnecessary retraining: Tune inference settings first.

    Avoid application-level workaround: Do not rely on caching as the primary fix.

    Compliance-sensitive responses: Prefer controlled generation over creative variation.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Amazon Bedrock + inconsistent LLM responses + need deterministic output = lower temperature and reduce top-K.

Remember:

    temperature: Controls randomness in generation.

    Low temperature: More deterministic and consistent responses.

    High temperature: More creative and variable responses.

    top-K: Limits the candidate token pool.

    Lower top-K: Less variability.

    Retraining: Improves knowledge or task performance, not inference-time randomness.

    Caching: Helps repeated identical requests, but does not fix generation behavior.

Official AWS references.

    Amazon Bedrock inference parameters.
