Question 19Skipped

A data science team at PixelForge Games, an online gaming company, is designing a machine learning pipeline that handles both structured operational data (e.g., player telemetry, match statistics, in-game event logs) and unstructured data (e.g., player chat messages, support ticket narratives, community forum posts).


What is a key difference in feature engineering tasks for structured data compared to unstructured data in the context of machine learning?
Feature engineering tasks for structured data and unstructured data are identical and do not vary based on data type.
Explanation
Incorrect. Feature engineering tasks differ substantially based on the nature of the data. The mathematical representations, preprocessing techniques, and downstream model compatibility requirements diverge significantly between structured and unstructured data — applying tokenization to tabular player telemetry or applying standard numerical normalization directly to raw chat text would be technically inappropriate and produce unusable model inputs. Recognizing these differences is fundamental to designing correct ML pipelines. Keyword tip: Any option claiming that feature engineering is 'identical' regardless of data type is a clear distractor — always verify whether the answer accounts for structural and representational differences between data modalities.
Feature engineering for structured data focuses on image recognition, whereas for unstructured data, it focuses on numerical data analysis.
Explanation
Incorrect. Structured data encompasses numerical, categorical, and ordinal values — not image recognition, which belongs to the unstructured domain. Conversely, unstructured data such as player chat messages or forum posts is not characterized primarily by 'numerical data analysis' at the feature engineering stage. This option deliberately reverses and misrepresents the characteristics of both data types to serve as a plausible-sounding distractor. Keyword tip: When you see 'structured data + image recognition' paired together as the primary focus of feature engineering, recognize this as a distractor that conflates distinct data modalities.
Feature engineering for structured data is not necessary as the data is already in a usable format, whereas for unstructured data, extensive preprocessing is always required.
Explanation
Incorrect. Feature engineering is essential for both structured and unstructured data. Structured data such as player telemetry still requires normalization, imputation of missing values, encoding of categorical variables, and outlier treatment before it can be effectively consumed by ML models. The assertion that structured data needs no preprocessing is a pervasive misconception that should be rejected on the MLA-C01 exam. Keyword tip: Any option claiming that one data type requires 'no feature engineering' is almost always incorrect — both data types require preprocessing, though the specific techniques differ substantially.
Correct answer
Feature engineering for structured data often involves tasks such as normalization and handling missing values, while for unstructured data, it involves tasks such as tokenization and vectorization.
Explanation
Correct. For structured data such as match statistics or in-game event logs, feature engineering typically includes normalization (scaling numerical features to a common range), missing value imputation, and categorical encoding (one-hot encoding, label encoding). For unstructured data such as player chat messages or support ticket narratives, feature engineering involves tokenization (splitting text into discrete tokens), vectorization (converting tokens into numerical representations such as TF-IDF vectors or dense word embeddings), and domain-specific feature extraction. This distinction directly informs the selection of AWS pipeline components — AWS Glue DataBrew and Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler for structured preprocessing, and SageMaker Processing jobs with Hugging Face containers for NLP workflows. Keyword tip: 'Normalization + missing values → structured data' and 'tokenization + vectorization → unstructured data' is the canonical pairing tested in MLA-C01 Data Preparation questions.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests the ability to distinguish feature engineering workflows for structured and unstructured data.

It focuses on identifying which preprocessing techniques belong to tabular data and which techniques belong to unstructured text data.

It is tied to the Data Preparation for Machine Learning (ML) domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Feature engineering for structured data focuses on image recognition, whereas for unstructured data, it focuses on numerical data analysis: This option is incorrect. Structured data refers to tabular or relational data with defined columns, such as numerical and categorical values. Image recognition belongs to unstructured data, not structured data.

    Feature engineering for structured data is not necessary as the data is already in a usable format, whereas for unstructured data, extensive preprocessing is always required: This option is incorrect. Structured data still needs preprocessing before training. Common steps include missing value handling, normalization, standardization, categorical encoding, and outlier treatment.

    Feature engineering for structured data often involves tasks such as normalization and handling missing values, while for unstructured data, it involves tasks such as tokenization and vectorization: This is the correct option. Structured data often requires transformations such as scaling, imputation, and encoding. Unstructured text data requires techniques such as tokenization, vectorization, embeddings, or deep feature extraction.

    Feature engineering tasks for structured data and unstructured data are identical and do not vary based on data type: This option is incorrect. Feature engineering depends heavily on the data modality. Tabular data, text, images, and audio each require different preprocessing and representation techniques.

Why normalization and missing value handling for structured data, and tokenization and vectorization for unstructured data, fit this scenario.

Structured data is usually organized into rows and columns.

Examples include player telemetry, match statistics, transaction records, sensor readings, and customer attributes.

Because structured data already has a schema, feature engineering usually focuses on preparing the existing columns for model training.

Common tasks include:

    Handling missing values.

    Normalizing or standardizing numerical columns.

    Encoding categorical variables.

    Treating outliers.

    Creating derived tabular features.

Unstructured data is different.

It does not naturally fit into rows and columns.

Examples include chat messages, documents, images, audio files, forum posts, and support tickets.

For text data, the model cannot directly consume raw sentences.

The text must first be converted into numerical representations.

That is why tokenization and vectorization are common unstructured-data preprocessing steps.

Tokenization splits text into smaller units such as words, subwords, or tokens.

Vectorization converts those tokens into numerical representations such as TF-IDF vectors, embeddings, or transformer-based representations.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Structured data: Use normalization, missing value imputation, and categorical encoding.

    Unstructured text data: Use tokenization and vectorization.

    Correct data modality mapping: Match preprocessing to the format of the source data.

    Training readiness: Convert raw inputs into model-ready numerical features.

    AWS pipeline design: Use the right preprocessing tool for each data type.

    Avoid false equivalence: Do not assume all data types require identical feature engineering.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Structured data = normalization, missing value handling, and categorical encoding.

Unstructured text data = tokenization and vectorization.

Remember:

    Structured data: Tabular, schema-based, rows and columns.

    Unstructured data: Text, images, audio, documents, or free-form content.

    Normalization: Common for numerical structured features.

    Missing value imputation: Common for structured datasets.

    Tokenization: Splits text into tokens.

    Vectorization: Converts text into numerical model inputs.

Official AWS references.

    AWS Well-Architected Machine Learning Lens: feature engineering.

Key exam terms: feature engineering, structured data, unstructured data, normalization, missing value imputation, tokenization, vectorization, AWS Glue DataBrew, SageMaker Data Wrangler, SageMaker Processing jobs.

Question 20Skipped

A hospitality and hotel chain company, StayBridge Resorts, uses an AWS Lambda function to monitor guest booking cancellation prediction metrics from an ML model deployed in Amazon SageMaker.


The company wants to be notified via email whenever the cancellation prediction model's confidence score drops below a specific threshold, such as 80%.


The ML engineer needs to implement an automated solution that triggers email notifications whenever the confidence score breaches the threshold. 


Which of the following options should be combined to develop a solution that addresses these requirements? (Select TWO.)


Correct selection
Set up a CloudWatch alarm to send the email message
Explanation
Correct. Amazon CloudWatch Alarms provide a native and fully managed mechanism to monitor custom metrics and automate notifications via Amazon SNS. After the Lambda function publishes the model's confidence score as a custom metric to CloudWatch, a CloudWatch alarm can be configured to evaluate that metric against a defined threshold (e.g., below 80%). The alarm action targets an Amazon SNS topic with email subscribers, so when the alarm transitions to the ALARM state, SNS automatically delivers the email notification to all subscribed addresses. Keyword tip: CloudWatch alarm + SNS = automated threshold-based email alerting for ML model metrics.
Log the metrics from the Lambda function to AWS CloudTrail
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS CloudTrail is designed for auditing API calls and tracking changes made to AWS account resources — it is not a service for logging or monitoring custom application-level metrics such as an ML model's confidence score. When you need to capture and monitor custom performance metrics from a Lambda function, CloudTrail is not the right tool; it records who did what to which AWS resource, not runtime model output values. Keyword tip: CloudTrail = API audit logging for governance and compliance, not custom application metrics monitoring.
Log the metrics from the Lambda function to AWS CloudFormation
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS CloudFormation is an infrastructure-as-code service used to define and provision AWS resources declaratively. It provides no logging or metric-collection mechanism for application-level data such as a SageMaker model's confidence score generated at runtime. Choosing CloudFormation for metrics logging confuses infrastructure provisioning with operational observability. Keyword tip: CloudFormation = IaC stack provisioning, not runtime metrics collection or monitoring.
Correct selection
Log the metrics from the Lambda function to AWS CloudWatch
Explanation
Correct. AWS Lambda integrates natively with Amazon CloudWatch, enabling you to publish custom metrics directly from the Lambda function using the AWS SDK's putMetricData API call. By publishing the SageMaker model's confidence score as a custom CloudWatch metric, you make it available for monitoring, dashboarding, and alarm evaluation. The Lambda execution role must be granted the cloudwatch:PutMetricData IAM permission for this to succeed, and this step is a prerequisite to configuring any CloudWatch alarm on that metric. Keyword tip: Lambda + putMetricData = publishing custom ML model metrics to CloudWatch for downstream threshold alerting.
Set up a CloudFormation change set to send the email message
Explanation
Incorrect. A CloudFormation change set is a mechanism to preview how proposed changes to a CloudFormation stack will affect existing resources before those changes are applied. It has no capability to evaluate runtime metric thresholds or send email notifications based on model performance. Email notifications triggered by metric breaches are handled through Amazon CloudWatch alarms paired with Amazon SNS, not CloudFormation constructs. Keyword tip: CloudFormation change sets = infrastructure change preview, not operational threshold-based alerting.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of custom ML metric monitoring and automated alerting on AWS.

It focuses on publishing custom metrics from AWS Lambda to Amazon CloudWatch, then using a CloudWatch Alarm and Amazon SNS to send email notifications when a threshold is breached.

It is tied to the ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Log the metrics from the Lambda function to AWS CloudTrail: This option is incorrect. AWS CloudTrail records AWS API activity for audit and governance. It does not collect or evaluate custom runtime metrics such as ML model confidence scores.

    Log the metrics from the Lambda function to AWS CloudFormation: This option is incorrect. AWS CloudFormation is used to provision infrastructure as code. It is not a runtime logging or metrics service.

    Set up a CloudFormation change set to send the email message: This option is incorrect. AWS CloudFormation change sets preview infrastructure changes before stack updates. They do not evaluate application metrics or send threshold-based alerts.

    Set up a CloudWatch alarm to send the email message: This is a correct option. A CloudWatch Alarm can watch a custom metric and trigger an Amazon SNS notification when the metric crosses a configured threshold.

    Log the metrics from the Lambda function to AWS CloudWatch: This is a correct option. AWS Lambda can publish custom metrics to Amazon CloudWatch using the putMetricData API. Those metrics can then be used for dashboards, alarms, and automated notifications.

Why CloudWatch custom metrics and alarms fit this scenario.

The scenario requires monitoring a custom ML metric.

The metric is produced by an AWS Lambda function.

The team needs an automated email notification when the metric crosses a threshold.

That points to Amazon CloudWatch custom metrics plus a CloudWatch Alarm.

First, the AWS Lambda function publishes the custom metric to Amazon CloudWatch.

For example, the function can publish a model confidence score using the putMetricData API.

The Lambda execution role must have the cloudwatch:PutMetricData permission.

Second, a CloudWatch Alarm evaluates that metric against a threshold.

For example, the alarm can move to the ALARM state when the confidence score drops below 80%.

Third, the alarm sends a notification to an Amazon SNS topic.

Email subscribers to that topic receive the alert automatically.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Custom ML metric publishing: Use Amazon CloudWatch custom metrics.

    Lambda integration: Publish metrics from AWS Lambda.

    Required permission: Grant cloudwatch:PutMetricData to the Lambda execution role.

    Threshold evaluation: Use a CloudWatch Alarm.

    Email notification: Use Amazon SNS.

    Avoid audit-service confusion: Do not use AWS CloudTrail for application-level metrics.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Lambda-generated custom ML metric + threshold alert + email notification = Amazon CloudWatch custom metric + CloudWatch Alarm + Amazon SNS.

Remember:

    Amazon CloudWatch: Metrics, logs, dashboards, and alarms.

    putMetricData: API used to publish custom metrics.

    CloudWatch Alarm: Evaluates metric thresholds.

    Amazon SNS: Sends notifications such as email alerts.

    AWS CloudTrail: API audit logging, not custom metric monitoring.

    AWS CloudFormation: Infrastructure provisioning, not runtime observability.

    Change set: Infrastructure update preview, not alerting.

Official AWS references.

    Monitoring AWS Lambda with Amazon CloudWatch Logs.

    Create an Amazon CloudWatch alarm that sends email.

    What is AWS CloudFormation?

Key exam terms: Amazon CloudWatch custom metrics, putMetricData, CloudWatch Alarm, Amazon SNS, AWS Lambda execution role IAM permissions, threshold-based alerting, ML model confidence score monitoring, MLOps observability.

Question 21Skipped

A data science team at a semiconductor manufacturer, SiliconCore Analytics, is working on a machine learning project that requires ingesting large volumes of raw fabrication sensor readings and equipment operational data for analysis and feature engineering.


The team plans to use Amazon SageMaker for the project, and they need to efficiently ingest the data into a platform where they can perform transformations and store features for future model training. 


Which of the following approaches is the most efficient for ingesting data, performing data transformations, and then storing the engineered features?


Correct answer
Ingest the data into Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler, perform transformations, and then store the transformed data in SageMaker Feature Store
Explanation
Correct. SageMaker Data Wrangler is specifically designed to ingest, explore, and transform large volumes of raw data through a visual interface, offering over 300 built-in transformations without requiring custom code — making it well suited for processing high-dimensional fabrication sensor data at scale. After transformation, engineered features can be exported directly into SageMaker Feature Store, which provides a centralized, low-latency, and versioned repository for storing, retrieving, and sharing features across multiple model training and inference pipelines. This end-to-end, native integration within the SageMaker ecosystem eliminates the need for custom connectors and ensures consistent feature definitions across the ML lifecycle. Keyword tip: Data Wrangler (visual, large-scale transformation) → Feature Store (centralized feature storage and serving) is the canonical SageMaker data preparation and feature management pipeline for the MLA-C01 exam.
Ingest the data into AWS Glue for transformations, then store the transformed data in Amazon S3 and load it into Amazon SageMaker using the AWS SDK for Python
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS Glue is a fully managed ETL service and is technically capable of transforming large volumes of sensor data; however, this option stores the transformed data back in Amazon S3 and loads it into SageMaker via the AWS SDK for Python, bypassing SageMaker Feature Store entirely. This approach lacks a structured feature management layer, making engineered features harder to discover, version, share, and reuse consistently across multiple model training jobs — a significant MLOps gap for a production semiconductor ML pipeline. Keyword tip: if an option performs valid transformations but omits SageMaker Feature Store for engineered feature governance and reuse, it represents an incomplete MLOps solution for SageMaker-centric workflows.
Use Amazon S3 to store the raw data, then directly connect SageMaker Feature Store to ingest and transform the data
Explanation
Incorrect. While Amazon S3 is a valid and widely used storage layer for raw data in ML pipelines, SageMaker Feature Store is designed to store, retrieve, and serve already-engineered features — it does not function as a general-purpose transformation engine for complex, large-scale data preparation. Although Feature Store supports basic feature processing such as time-window aggregates at ingest time, performing the kind of complex, multi-step transformations required for fabrication sensor data demands a dedicated tool such as SageMaker Data Wrangler. Skipping a transformation layer leaves raw, unprocessed data flowing directly into a feature repository, undermining data quality. Keyword tip: SageMaker Feature Store = structured feature storage and low-latency retrieval, not a general-purpose transformation or ETL engine.
Load the data into an Amazon RDS database, then use SageMaker Clarify to ingest and transform the data for storing in the Feature Store
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon RDS is a managed relational database service designed for transactional workloads, not as a primary ingestion layer for large-scale, high-velocity ML raw sensor data pipelines. More critically, SageMaker Clarify is a bias detection and model explainability tool — it does not perform data transformation or ETL processing of any kind. The purpose-built tool for interactive, large-scale data transformation prior to feature storage within the SageMaker ecosystem is SageMaker Data Wrangler. Keyword tip: whenever an option invokes SageMaker Clarify for data transformation, ingestion, or ETL tasks, it is incorrect — Clarify is strictly an explainability and bias-detection service.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of the Amazon SageMaker data preparation ecosystem and the distinction between data transformation tools and feature storage services.

It focuses on using Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler for visual data transformation and Amazon SageMaker Feature Store for storing, versioning, and serving engineered features.

It is tied to the Data Preparation for Machine Learning (ML) domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Load the data into an Amazon RDS database, then use SageMaker Clarify to ingest and transform the data for storing in the Feature Store: This option is incorrect. Amazon RDS is a relational database service, not the preferred ingestion layer for large-scale ML sensor data transformation. More importantly, Amazon SageMaker Clarify is used for bias detection and explainability, not ETL or feature transformation.

    Use Amazon S3 to store the raw data, then directly connect SageMaker Feature Store to ingest and transform the data: This option is incorrect. Amazon S3 is a valid raw data storage layer, but Amazon SageMaker Feature Store is not a general-purpose transformation engine. It stores and serves engineered features; it does not replace a data preparation tool for complex transformations.

    Ingest the data into AWS Glue for transformations, then store the transformed data in Amazon S3 and load it into Amazon SageMaker using the AWS SDK for Python: This option is incomplete. AWS Glue can perform ETL, and Amazon S3 can store transformed data, but this design bypasses Amazon SageMaker Feature Store. That means the engineered features are not centrally managed, versioned, shared, or reused through a feature repository.

    Ingest the data into Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler, perform transformations, and then store the transformed data in SageMaker Feature Store: This is the correct option. Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler is designed for visual data preparation and transformation. Amazon SageMaker Feature Store is designed to store, retrieve, share, and serve engineered features for training and inference.

The transformation process should be managed inside the Amazon SageMaker ecosystem.

The engineered features also need to be stored and reused properly.

That points to Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler followed by Amazon SageMaker Feature Store.

It supports many built-in transformations, which reduces the need for custom preprocessing code.

After the transformations are complete, the resulting engineered features should not be left only in unmanaged files.

They should be stored in Amazon SageMaker Feature Store.

Amazon SageMaker Feature Store provides a centralized repository for features.

It supports feature reuse across training and inference workflows.

It also improves governance by making feature definitions easier to discover, manage, and share across teams.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Large-scale data preparation: Use Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler.

    Visual transformation workflow: Apply built-in transformations with minimal custom code.

    Feature engineering: Convert raw data into model-ready features.

    Feature governance: Store engineered features in Amazon SageMaker Feature Store.

    Training and inference reuse: Serve consistent features across ML workflows.

    Avoid service misuse: Do not use Amazon SageMaker Clarify for ETL or Amazon SageMaker Feature Store as a transformation engine.

Exam tip.

Visual SageMaker data transformation + engineered feature storage + reuse across training and inference = Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler → Amazon SageMaker Feature Store.

Remember:

    Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler: Data preparation, cleaning, transformation, and feature engineering.

Question 22Skipped

A metropolitan smart city government agency, CivicData Solutions, operates a document retrieval system that helps urban planners and policy analysts find relevant public records and reports using natural language queries.

The existing system uses a vector database for storing embeddings of government documents.


The agency has migrated all its archived records to an Amazon S3 bucket and now needs a solution on AWS to provide semantic search capabilities for these documents. 


Which solution will meet these requirements most efficiently?

Use Amazon Comprehend to extract key phrases and entities from the records and build a custom search engine for semantic retrieval.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon Comprehend is a natural language processing (NLP) service designed for text analytics tasks such as entity recognition, key phrase extraction, sentiment analysis, and topic modeling. It does not natively provide search indexing or query retrieval capabilities. Building a custom semantic search engine on top of Comprehend's output would require substantial additional engineering effort, making it a far less efficient solution than Amazon Kendra for this scenario. Keyword tip: Amazon Comprehend = NLP analytics and text enrichment; it is not a search service and should not be confused with Amazon Kendra in search-oriented scenarios involving unstructured document retrieval.
Deploy a SageMaker endpoint with a fine-tuned language model to process user queries and manually integrate the model with Amazon S3 for retrieving documents.
Explanation
Incorrect. While deploying a SageMaker endpoint with a fine-tuned language model is technically feasible, it introduces unnecessary complexity by requiring a custom semantic search pipeline, manual integration with Amazon S3, and ongoing infrastructure management. This approach does not satisfy the 'most efficiently' constraint in the scenario. Keyword tip: When a question emphasizes efficiency and minimal development effort for a well-defined document search use case, custom SageMaker inference endpoints are typically distractors compared to purpose-built managed services such as Amazon Kendra.
Leverage AWS Glue to preprocess the archived records and create a metadata catalog, then use Amazon Athena to perform semantic queries on the catalog.
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS Glue is designed for ETL operations and metadata cataloging of structured data, while Amazon Athena is optimized for SQL-based analytical queries on structured datasets stored in Amazon S3. Neither service provides semantic search or natural language understanding capabilities. This combination is appropriate for structured data analytics pipelines, not for document retrieval using natural language queries. Keyword tip: When you see 'AWS Glue + Amazon Athena,' think structured ETL and SQL analytics — not semantic or natural language search over unstructured documents.
Correct answer
Use Amazon Kendra to index the archived records in the S3 bucket and enable semantic search with natural language queries.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon Kendra is a fully managed intelligent search service purpose-built for semantic and natural language search over enterprise documents. It natively integrates with Amazon S3 as a data source connector, allowing direct indexing of archived records stored in S3 with minimal configuration. Kendra's ML-powered engine understands the intent behind natural language queries and returns contextually relevant results without requiring custom model development or complex pipeline orchestration. Keyword tip: When you see 'semantic search,' 'natural language queries,' and 'documents stored in Amazon S3,' Amazon Kendra is the canonical AWS managed solution for enterprise document search scenarios.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests the ability to select the right AWS managed service for semantic document search over unstructured content stored in Amazon S3.

It focuses on choosing Amazon Kendra when the requirement is natural language search across enterprise documents with minimal custom development.

It is tied to the Data Preparation for Machine Learning (ML) domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Leverage AWS Glue to preprocess the archived records and create a metadata catalog, then use Amazon Athena to perform semantic queries on the catalog: This option is incorrect. AWS Glue is used for ETL and metadata cataloging. Amazon Athena is used for SQL queries over data in Amazon S3. This combination is useful for structured analytics, not semantic search over unstructured documents.

    Deploy a SageMaker endpoint with a fine-tuned language model to process user queries and manually integrate the model with Amazon S3 for retrieving documents: This option is technically possible, but not the most efficient. A custom Amazon SageMaker endpoint would require model hosting, retrieval logic, indexing, ranking, and integration with Amazon S3. That adds unnecessary engineering overhead when a managed search service already exists.

    Use Amazon Kendra to index the archived records in the S3 bucket and enable semantic search with natural language queries: This is the correct option. Amazon Kendra is a fully managed intelligent search service designed for semantic and natural language search across enterprise documents. It can index content stored in Amazon S3 using a native data source connector.

    Use Amazon Comprehend to extract key phrases and entities from the records and build a custom search engine for semantic retrieval: This option is incorrect. Amazon Comprehend performs NLP tasks such as entity detection, key phrase extraction, sentiment analysis, and topic modeling. It does not provide a complete semantic search and document retrieval engine by itself.

Why Amazon Kendra fits this scenario.

The data consists of archived records.

The records are stored in Amazon S3.

Users need semantic search using natural language queries.

The solution should be efficient and require minimal operational overhead.

That points directly to Amazon Kendra.

Amazon Kendra is purpose-built for intelligent enterprise search.

It can index unstructured documents and make them searchable through natural language queries.

Its ML-powered search capabilities help return contextually relevant documents instead of relying only on exact keyword matching.

It also provides a native Amazon S3 connector, which avoids building a custom ingestion and indexing pipeline.

This is more efficient than building a custom semantic search system with Amazon SageMaker.

It is also a better fit than AWS Glue and Amazon Athena, which focus on ETL, cataloging, and SQL analytics.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Semantic search: Use Amazon Kendra.

    Natural language queries: Let users search with conversational or intent-based questions.

    S3 document source: Use the Amazon S3 data source connector.

    Unstructured content: Index archived records and enterprise documents.

    Minimal development effort: Avoid custom embedding, indexing, and retrieval pipelines.

    Purpose-built managed service: Prefer Amazon Kendra over generic NLP or analytics tools.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Documents in Amazon S3 + semantic search + natural language queries + minimal development effort = Amazon Kendra.

Remember:

    Amazon Kendra: Intelligent enterprise search and semantic document retrieval.

    Amazon S3 connector: Allows Kendra to index documents stored in S3.

    Amazon Comprehend: NLP enrichment, not a full search engine.

    AWS Glue: ETL and metadata cataloging.

    Amazon Athena: SQL queries over structured or semi-structured data.

Question 23Skipped

A renewable energy company called SolarGrid Analytics has deployed a sentiment analysis machine learning model to an Amazon SageMaker endpoint to assess public and investor perception of its clean energy initiatives.


Given the involvement of multiple stakeholders — including regulatory bodies, investors, and operations teams — the data scientist responsible for the model needs to explain how it generates its predictions and clarify the factors influencing its decisions. 


Which solution will provide an explanation for the model's predictions?


Use Amazon Textract to automatically extract text and data from the ML model and provide insights on its predictions.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon Textract is a document analysis service that extracts text, handwriting, tables, and key-value pairs from scanned documents and digital forms. It has no capability to introspect a machine learning model, compute feature attributions, or explain prediction logic; it operates exclusively on document content, not on model internals or inference outputs. Keyword tip: Amazon Textract is relevant only when the task involves extracting structured or unstructured content from physical or digital documents — never for ML model interpretability or explainability.
Leverage Amazon CloudWatch Logs Insights derived from SageMaker endpoint invocation metrics to analyze the model for bias and explainability.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon CloudWatch collects and processes raw operational telemetry from SageMaker endpoints, such as invocation counts, latency, throttling events, and error rates. While these metrics are valuable for monitoring infrastructure health and usage patterns, they provide no insight into model decision-making, feature importance, or algorithmic bias — they reflect endpoint behavior, not model reasoning. Keyword tip: CloudWatch endpoint invocation metrics are operational observability tools — use them for infrastructure performance monitoring, not for model explainability or bias detection.
Correct answer
Configure a SageMaker Clarify processing job to analyze the model for bias and explainability.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon SageMaker Clarify enables ML practitioners to understand model fairness and explainability by computing SHAP-based feature attributions and bias metrics across training data and live inference. A SageMaker Clarify processing job can be configured to generate human-readable reports that quantify which input features most influenced a given prediction, making it the purpose-built tool for satisfying stakeholder explainability requirements in multi-stakeholder or regulated environments. Keyword tip: whenever a scenario involves explaining individual predictions, detecting model or data bias, or generating feature attribution reports for stakeholder transparency — SageMaker Clarify is the definitive answer.
Use Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor, which offers tools to provide explanations on model quality.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor is designed to continuously monitor the quality of deployed machine learning models in production and to trigger automated alerts when deviations such as data drift, model quality degradation, or feature distribution shifts are detected. It does not compute feature-level attributions or explain the reasoning behind individual model predictions; it tracks statistical properties of inputs and outputs over time rather than surfacing interpretability reports. Keyword tip: when the requirement is about detecting data drift, baseline violations, or model quality degradation over time — not prediction-level explainability — SageMaker Model Monitor is the correct service to associate.
Overall explanation

This question tests the ability to distinguish model explainability services from operational monitoring and document processing services.

It is tied to the ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security domain.

Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor tracks production model behavior over time, including data quality, model quality, bias drift, and feature attribution drift. It does not generate prediction-level explanations or SHAP-based feature attribution reports by itself.

    Configure a SageMaker Clarify processing job to analyze the model for bias and explainability: This is the correct option. Amazon SageMaker Clarify is designed for bias detection and model explainability. It can compute SHAP-based feature attributions and generate reports that help explain model predictions.

    Use Amazon Textract to automatically extract text and data from the ML model and provide insights on its predictions: This option is incorrect. Amazon Textract extracts text, handwriting, tables, and key-value pairs from documents. It does not analyze model internals, compute feature importance, or explain predictions.

    Leverage Amazon CloudWatch Logs Insights derived from SageMaker endpoint invocation metrics to analyze the model for bias and explainability: This option is incorrect. Amazon CloudWatch provides operational observability, such as logs, latency, invocation counts, and error metrics. It does not provide model explainability or bias analysis.

The scenario requires explaining model predictions.

It also mentions bias and stakeholder transparency.

That points directly to Amazon SageMaker Clarify.

Question 24Skipped

An industrial manufacturing company, PrecisionForge Industries, requires a scalable solution for preprocessing and transforming big data for machine learning.


Sensor telemetry, quality control logs, and supply chain records are spread across multiple heterogeneous sources and must be processed using a distributed computing framework before being fed into ML training pipelines.


Which of the following solutions would you recommend?


Correct answer
Use Amazon EMR in Amazon SageMaker Studio
Explanation
Correct. Amazon EMR integrated with Amazon SageMaker Studio provides a scalable, distributed computing environment for large-scale data preparation using open-source frameworks such as Apache Spark, Apache Hive, and Presto. Users can connect to Amazon EMR clusters directly from SageMaker Studio notebooks, enabling them to preprocess and transform massive datasets across multiple heterogeneous sources — such as manufacturing sensor telemetry, quality logs, and supply chain records — before feeding them into ML training pipelines. This combination is the recommended architecture for big data preprocessing in the MLA-C01 exam context because it satisfies both the distributed computing and multi-source ingestion requirements simultaneously. Keyword tip: When the scenario mentions 'big data,' 'distributed computing framework,' and 'multiple heterogeneous data sources,' Amazon EMR integrated with SageMaker Studio is the correct choice.
Use the built-in SQL extension in Amazon SageMaker Studio
Explanation
Incorrect. The built-in SQL extension in Amazon SageMaker Studio allows data scientists to perform tasks such as sampling, exploratory data analysis, and lightweight feature engineering directly within JupyterLab notebooks by querying data sources interactively. However, it is not designed for large-scale, distributed data transformation workloads, as it does not provision or leverage a distributed compute cluster. It lacks the throughput and horizontal scalability required to process big data spread across heterogeneous sources such as sensor telemetry and supply chain records. Keyword tip: When you see 'SQL extension' paired with 'big data at scale' or 'distributed computing framework,' recognize this as a distractor — SQL extensions in Studio are for interactive, small-to-medium workloads, not distributed batch processing.
Use Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth is a fully managed data labeling service that orchestrates annotation workflows using human labelers from Amazon Mechanical Turk, private workforces, or third-party vendors, optionally accelerated by ML-based auto-labeling. It is entirely unrelated to data preprocessing, feature engineering, or distributed transformation of big data for ML training pipelines. Selecting Ground Truth in a scenario focused on scalable data transformation indicates a fundamental misunderstanding of the service's purpose. Keyword tip: Whenever 'Ground Truth' appears in a scenario about data preprocessing, transformation, or distributed computing, it is always a distractor — Ground Truth is exclusively for data annotation and labeling tasks.
Use Data Wrangler within Amazon SageMaker Canvas
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler is a feature of Amazon SageMaker Studio Classic that provides a visual, end-to-end solution for importing, preparing, transforming, and featurizing data for ML. Amazon SageMaker Canvas is a separate no-code visual interface intended for building and deploying ML models without writing code. While Data Wrangler supports a range of data transformation steps, pairing it with Canvas targets business analysts working on small-to-medium datasets, not engineers processing big data across distributed infrastructure. This combination does not provision a distributed compute cluster and cannot horizontally scale to handle large, multi-source manufacturing datasets. Keyword tip: 'Data Wrangler within Canvas' signals a no-code, low-scale analyst workflow — whenever a scenario requires distributed batch processing of big data, this pairing is always a distractor.
Overall explanation

When connected from SageMaker Studio, it allows ML engineers to run large-scale preprocessing and transformation workloads before model training.

    Use Data Wrangler within Amazon SageMaker Canvas: This option is incorrect. SageMaker Data Wrangler is a visual data preparation tool, and SageMaker Canvas is a no-code ML interface. This pairing does not provide a distributed compute framework for big data preprocessing at scale.

    Use Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth: This option is incorrect. SageMaker Ground Truth is used for data labeling and annotation workflows. It is not used for distributed preprocessing, feature engineering, or transformation of large datasets.

The data comes from multiple heterogeneous sources.

Question 25Skipped

A sports analytics company, AthleteIQ Analytics, is using Amazon SageMaker to train a deep learning model for real-time player tracking and performance analysis.

The training process requires distributed training across multiple GPU instances due to the large size of the video and sensor dataset.


During training, the ML engineer observes that the model training is slower than expected because of network communication delays between the instances.

The engineer must optimize the network configuration to minimize communication overhead and improve training performance.

Which of the following options should be combined for a solution that meets these requirements? (Select TWO.)


Correct selection
Store the training data in the same AWS Region and Availability Zone where the instances are deployed
Explanation
Correct. Storing training data in the same AWS Region and Availability Zone as the training instances ensures that all data access occurs over the high-speed, low-latency infrastructure local to that AZ. This eliminates cross-AZ transfer latency and avoids associated data transfer costs, enabling training instances to retrieve data more efficiently during each training step. For distributed training workflows where data throughput and synchronization speed are critical — such as video-based player tracking models — same-AZ data colocation is an established best practice. Keyword tip: 'Same AZ data colocation' signals the minimum-latency, zero-cross-AZ-transfer-cost configuration for distributed SageMaker training jobs.
Store the training data in the same AWS Region but different Availability Zones from where the instances are deployed
Explanation
Incorrect. Placing training data in a different Availability Zone from the training instances introduces cross-AZ network latency and potential data transfer costs. For distributed deep learning training, which requires frequent and fast data access alongside inter-instance gradient synchronization, cross-AZ communication adds measurable overhead that degrades performance. Co-locating data and compute within the same Availability Zone is a best practice when minimizing latency is a hard requirement. Keyword tip: When a question describes distributed training slowdowns caused by network delays, 'same AZ' data colocation is the correct signal — 'different AZ, same Region' is a plausible but incorrect distractor.
Correct selection
Launch the training instances in the same VPC subnet
Explanation
Correct. Launching all training instances within the same VPC subnet ensures that inter-instance communication occurs entirely over the local network segment, without any intermediate routing hops between subnets. This is critical for distributed training frameworks such as Horovod and PyTorch DDP, which perform frequent all-reduce gradient synchronization operations that are highly sensitive to network latency. Placing instances in a single subnet eliminates the routing overhead introduced by cross-subnet traffic, directly reducing the communication delays observed during training. Keyword tip: 'Same VPC subnet' for all training instances is the lowest-latency intra-cluster network configuration for distributed deep learning on Amazon SageMaker.
Launch the training instances in different subnets within the same VPC to balance network traffic and reduce communication bottlenecks
Explanation
Incorrect. While all subnets within the same VPC can communicate freely, routing traffic across different subnets introduces additional routing overhead compared to keeping all instances within a single subnet. In distributed training scenarios that require low-latency, high-throughput inter-instance communication — such as all-reduce gradient synchronization in Horovod or PyTorch DDP — placing instances in separate subnets increases network path complexity and adds measurable latency. Keyword tip: 'Different subnets within the same VPC' is a distractor that sounds network-efficient but conflicts with the tight coupling requirements of distributed deep learning training clusters.
Store the training data in a different AWS Region from where the instances are deployed
Explanation
Incorrect. Storing training data in a different AWS Region from the training instances introduces significant cross-region network latency and substantial data transfer costs. Cross-region communication is entirely unsuitable for distributed deep learning training, which depends on near-instantaneous data access and rapid inter-instance gradient synchronization. This option would severely degrade training performance rather than improve it. Keyword tip: 'Cross-region data storage' is always incorrect for latency-sensitive, tightly coupled distributed training workloads on Amazon SageMaker.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of network optimization for distributed deep learning training on Amazon SageMaker.

It focuses on minimizing network latency and communication overhead between distributed GPU training instances.

It is tied to the Deployment and Orchestration of ML Workflows domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Store the training data in the same AWS Region but different Availability Zones from where the instances are deployed: This option is incorrect. Keeping data in the same Region is useful, but placing it in a different Availability Zone introduces cross-AZ latency and possible data transfer costs. Distributed training is highly sensitive to this overhead.

    Launch the training instances in different subnets within the same VPC to balance network traffic and reduce communication bottlenecks: This option is incorrect. Different subnets inside the same VPC can communicate, but cross-subnet communication still introduces additional routing. For tightly coupled distributed training, the goal is to reduce network hops, not spread instances across subnets.

    Store the training data in a different AWS Region from where the instances are deployed: This option is incorrect. Cross-Region data access introduces significant latency and data transfer cost. It is unsuitable for latency-sensitive distributed training workloads.

    Store the training data in the same AWS Region and Availability Zone where the instances are deployed: This is a correct option. Co-locating training data and compute in the same Region and Availability Zone reduces data access latency and avoids cross-AZ transfer overhead.

    Launch the training instances in the same VPC subnet: This is a correct option. Placing distributed training instances in the same subnet minimizes inter-instance communication latency and reduces routing overhead during synchronization operations.

Why same Availability Zone and same VPC subnet fit this scenario.

The scenario describes distributed deep learning training.

Distributed training frameworks such as Horovod and PyTorch Distributed Data Parallel (DDP) rely on frequent communication between training instances.

During training, each worker processes part of the data and then synchronizes gradients or model updates with the other workers.

This synchronization is sensitive to network latency.

If the instances are separated across Regions, Availability Zones, or subnets, the training job may experience additional network delay.

That can slow down each training step and increase the total training time.

The first correct optimization is to keep the training data in the same AWS Region and Availability Zone as the training instances.

This reduces the latency between compute and storage access paths and avoids unnecessary cross-AZ data movement.

The second correct optimization is to launch the training instances in the same VPC subnet.

This helps keep inter-instance communication on the shortest possible network path.

Together, these choices reduce communication overhead during operations such as all-reduce gradient synchronization and parameter updates.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Low-latency distributed training: Reduce network boundaries.

    High-throughput GPU communication: Keep instances close together.

    Efficient data access: Co-locate training data and compute.

    Avoid cross-AZ overhead: Keep data and instances in the same Availability Zone.

    Avoid cross-subnet routing: Place training instances in the same subnet.

    Reduce training time: Minimize synchronization delays between workers.


Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Distributed training + network delays + GPU instances + gradient synchronization = same Availability Zone and same VPC subnet.

Remember:

    Same Availability Zone: Reduces data access latency and avoids cross-AZ transfer overhead.

    Same VPC subnet: Reduces inter-instance routing overhead.

    Different Availability Zones: Adds latency and possible transfer costs.

    Different Regions: Adds major latency and is unsuitable for tightly coupled training.

    Distributed training: Highly sensitive to network communication delays.

    All-reduce synchronization: Requires fast communication between workers.

Official AWS references.

    How Amazon VPC works.

    AWS Regions and Availability Zones.

Key exam terms: distributed training, gradient synchronization, same Availability Zone, same VPC subnet, cross-AZ latency, inter-instance communication, Amazon SageMaker, network optimization.

Question 26Skipped

An education technology company, LearnBridge Academy, is using Amazon SageMaker to train a machine learning model on AWS infrastructure.


The training dataset comprises millions of files, each several megabytes in size, stored in an Amazon S3 bucket.


What immediate change can be made to quickly enhance the training performance with minimal effort?


Enable S3 Transfer Acceleration for the existing Amazon S3 bucket. Update the training job configuration to enhance training performance by leveraging the accelerated data transfer.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon S3 Transfer Acceleration improves upload and download speeds for data transferred over the public internet by routing traffic through AWS CloudFront edge locations closer to the client. Because a SageMaker training job runs entirely within AWS infrastructure, data transfers between S3 and the training instances occur over the AWS internal network rather than the public internet, rendering Transfer Acceleration irrelevant and ineffective for this use case. Enabling it would add cost with no measurable performance benefit in an intra-AWS scenario. Keyword tip: S3 Transfer Acceleration is only beneficial for long-distance internet transfers involving remote external clients — not for intra-AWS compute-to-S3 access patterns such as SageMaker training jobs.
Set up an Amazon Elastic File System (Amazon EFS) and link it to the existing S3 bucket. Update the training job configuration to read data directly from the file system.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon EFS can be integrated with SageMaker training jobs and provides a shared, POSIX-compliant file system accessible from multiple compute instances simultaneously. However, EFS is a general-purpose network file system and does not deliver the same level of aggregate throughput and IOPS as Amazon FSx for Lustre, which is purpose-built for high-performance, parallel I/O workloads. For a training job accessing millions of files at high concurrency, EFS represents a meaningful improvement over raw S3 File mode but still falls well short of the throughput FSx for Lustre provides. Keyword tip: When a question contrasts EFS and FSx for Lustre in a large-scale ML training context, the presence of millions of files and throughput-intensive access patterns is the decisive signal favoring FSx for Lustre over EFS.
Set up an Amazon S3 access point for the existing Amazon S3 bucket. Update the training job configuration to read data directly via the access point.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon S3 access points are named network endpoints attached to S3 buckets that simplify access control and permission management for shared datasets across multiple applications or teams. They do not improve the underlying I/O throughput, reduce API call overhead, or accelerate data transfer between S3 and SageMaker training instances in any way. This option is a distractor that conflates access management capabilities with storage performance optimization. Keyword tip: S3 access points address security and access control concerns — not data throughput or SageMaker training performance — so any question framed around I/O speed or training bottlenecks should eliminate this option immediately.
Correct answer
Set up an Amazon FSx for Lustre file system and link it to the existing S3 bucket. Update the training job configuration to read data directly from the file system.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon FSx for Lustre is a fully managed, high-performance parallel file system optimized for compute-intensive workloads such as machine learning, HPC, and large-scale data analysis. It natively integrates with Amazon S3, allowing you to link an S3 bucket as its backing data repository so that data is lazily loaded on first access without requiring a full data copy. This approach provides high-throughput, low-latency file access that far exceeds what standard S3 API calls can deliver during training, making it the most effective and operationally minimal change for improving training performance at LearnBridge Academy's scale. Keyword tip: 'High-performance training', 'millions of files', 'S3 integration', and 'minimal effort' are strong signals pointing to FSx for Lustre in MLA-C01 scenarios involving SageMaker training throughput optimization.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of high-performance storage options for Amazon SageMaker training jobs at scale.

It focuses on choosing Amazon FSx for Lustre when the training workload involves millions of files stored in Amazon S3 and requires high-throughput, low-latency access with minimal configuration change.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Set up an Amazon Elastic File System and link it to the existing S3 bucket. Update the training job configuration to read data directly from the file system: This option is incorrect. Amazon EFS provides a shared POSIX file system and can be useful for shared storage, but it is not optimized for the highest-throughput parallel I/O workloads. For millions of training files and intensive ML reads, Amazon FSx for Lustre is the stronger fit.

    Set up an Amazon FSx for Lustre file system and link it to the existing S3 bucket. Update the training job configuration to read data directly from the file system: This is the correct option. Amazon FSx for Lustre is a high-performance parallel file system designed for ML, HPC, and analytics workloads. It can link to an Amazon S3 bucket and provide fast file access for Amazon SageMaker training jobs.

    Set up an Amazon S3 access point for the existing Amazon S3 bucket. Update the training job configuration to read data directly via the access point: This option is incorrect. Amazon S3 Access Points simplify access management for shared datasets, but they do not improve training throughput or reduce the file access bottleneck caused by millions of S3 objects.

    Enable S3 Transfer Acceleration for the existing Amazon S3 bucket. Update the training job configuration to enhance training performance by leveraging the accelerated data transfer: This option is incorrect. Amazon S3 Transfer Acceleration improves long-distance transfers over the public internet. It is not useful for accelerating access between Amazon SageMaker training jobs and Amazon S3 inside AWS.

Why FSx for Lustre fits this scenario.

The dataset is stored in Amazon S3.

The training job needs to access millions of files.

The current access pattern creates an I/O bottleneck.

The solution must improve throughput with minimal operational overhead.

That points to Amazon FSx for Lustre.

Amazon FSx for Lustre is optimized for high-performance parallel file access.

It can be linked to an existing Amazon S3 bucket through a data repository association.

This allows training data stored in Amazon S3 to be presented through a fast POSIX-compatible file system.

The file system can lazily load data from Amazon S3 as files are accessed.

This avoids manually copying the entire dataset before training.

It also reduces the overhead of repeatedly accessing many small files through standard Amazon S3 API calls.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Millions of files: Use a parallel file system.

    High-throughput training: Use Amazon FSx for Lustre.

    S3-backed dataset: Link the file system to Amazon S3.

    Minimal configuration change: Update the training job to read from the file system.

    Avoid access-management distractors: Do not use Amazon S3 Access Points for performance optimization.

    Avoid internet-transfer distractors: Do not use Amazon S3 Transfer Acceleration for intra-AWS training access.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

SageMaker training + millions of S3 files + high I/O throughput + minimal change = Amazon FSx for Lustre.

Remember:

    Amazon FSx for Lustre: High-performance parallel file system for ML, HPC, and analytics.

    Amazon S3 integration: Links file data to an S3 data repository.

    File mode bottleneck: Can appear when training reads many S3 objects.

    Amazon EFS: General-purpose shared file system, not the strongest high-throughput training option.

    Amazon S3 Access Points: Access management, not performance acceleration.

    Amazon S3 Transfer Acceleration: Internet transfer optimization, not intra-AWS ML training optimization.

Official AWS references.

    What is Amazon FSx for Lustre?

    Create a data repository association linked to an S3 bucket.

    Amazon FSx for Lustre.

    Amazon S3 Access Points.

    Amazon S3 Transfer Acceleration.

Key exam terms: Amazon FSx for Lustre, Amazon SageMaker training job, high-performance parallel file system, Amazon S3 data repository integration, File mode bottleneck, training throughput optimization, intra-AWS data transfer, millions of training files.

Question 27Skipped

A regional banking institution, MeridianBank, relies on an ML model to forecast daily transaction volumes, which helps manage compute resource allocation across its core banking processing nodes.


The model runs once every evening to generate predictions for the following business day. It uses transaction data from the past several days as input with a maximum payload size of 3.5 MB, and the prediction process completes within 60 seconds. 


What is the most suitable deployment option on Amazon SageMaker to meet these requirements?


Correct answer
Use a serverless inference endpoint, with the MaxConcurrency parameter set to 1
Explanation
Correct. Amazon SageMaker Serverless Inference is purpose-built for workloads with intermittent or infrequent traffic patterns, including scheduled jobs that run once per day. It automatically provisions and scales compute resources without requiring instance type selection or manual scaling policy configuration. Serverless Inference supports payload sizes up to 4 MB and processing times up to 60 seconds — both constraints comfortably satisfied by this scenario (3.5 MB payload, 60-second completion time). Setting MaxConcurrency to 1 is appropriate because only a single request is issued per evening run, and the pay-per-invocation pricing model eliminates idle compute costs for infrequent workloads. Keyword tip: When a scenario describes intermittent or once-daily scheduled inference, a payload at or below 4 MB, and processing within 60 seconds with no tolerance for idle-instance costs, Serverless Inference is the signal-aligned choice.
Use a Real-time inference endpoint, with the minimum number of copies set to 1
Explanation
Incorrect. A Real-time inference endpoint deploys a persistent, always-on REST API backed by dedicated compute instances, making it well suited for sustained low-latency or high-throughput online inference workloads. Running a continuously provisioned endpoint for a single nightly prediction job is both unnecessary and cost-inefficient, as the endpoint would remain idle for the majority of every 24-hour period. The minimum number of copies parameter controls how many model copies are hosted concurrently, but it does not resolve the fundamental mismatch between a persistent, always-on deployment model and an intermittent, once-daily inference workload with no sustained traffic. Keyword tip: When a scenario requires consistently low-latency responses under sustained or unpredictable traffic — not a single scheduled invocation — a Real-time inference endpoint is the appropriate choice.
Use Batch Transform for inference with Amazon SageMaker
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Batch Transform is designed for offline, large-scale inference over datasets that are fully available upfront and where a persistent endpoint is not required. Although this daily forecasting scenario shares some offline characteristics, Batch Transform is architecturally intended for bulk processing of large datasets stored in Amazon S3, not for a single small-payload prediction request issued once per day. Batch Transform also introduces unnecessary overhead — such as job provisioning, input/output S3 staging, and job lifecycle management — that is disproportionate to the simplicity of a single nightly invocation with a 3.5 MB payload. Keyword tip: When the workload involves processing thousands of records or large files in bulk without requiring a live endpoint, Batch Transform is the appropriate signal — not a single small-payload scheduled invocation.
Use an Asynchronous Inference endpoint and place the request payload in Amazon S3
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Asynchronous Inference is optimized for scenarios involving large payload sizes (up to 1 GB) and long processing times (up to one hour), where near-real-time rather than immediate responses are acceptable. This scenario involves a small payload (3.5 MB) and a short processing time (60 seconds), neither of which justifies the added operational complexity of staging request payloads in Amazon S3 and polling for or receiving asynchronous result notifications. Asynchronous Inference is best suited for computationally intensive tasks such as document analysis, video processing, or large-scale NLP inference with substantial input sizes. Keyword tip: When you see payload sizes approaching or exceeding hundreds of megabytes and processing times measured in minutes, Asynchronous Inference is the appropriate signal — not small, fast, once-daily jobs.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests the ability to select the correct Amazon SageMaker inference deployment strategy based on traffic pattern, payload size, processing time, and cost model.

It focuses on choosing SageMaker Serverless Inference when the workload is intermittent, the payload is small, the processing time fits the serverless limit, and avoiding idle compute cost is important.

It is tied to the Deployment and Orchestration of ML Workflows domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Use a serverless inference endpoint, with the MaxConcurrency parameter set to 1: This is the correct option. SageMaker Serverless Inference is designed for intermittent or infrequent inference workloads. It automatically provisions compute, scales based on demand, and avoids charges for idle instances. A MaxConcurrency value of 1 fits a single scheduled nightly invocation.

    Use an Asynchronous Inference endpoint and place the request payload in Amazon S3: SageMaker Asynchronous Inference is better suited for large payloads and long-running inference jobs. It supports much larger payloads and longer processing times, but adds unnecessary complexity for a small, once-daily request.

    Use a Real-time inference endpoint, with the minimum number of copies set to 1: SageMaker real-time endpoints are designed for sustained, low-latency, always-available inference. They use continuously provisioned compute, which would be cost-inefficient for a workload that runs only once per day.

    Use Batch Transform for inference with Amazon SageMaker: Batch Transform is designed for offline bulk scoring over large datasets. It is not the best fit for a single small-payload scheduled prediction request.

Why SageMaker Serverless Inference fits this scenario.

The workload runs once every evening.

That means traffic is infrequent and scheduled.

The payload is 3.5 MB, which fits within the SageMaker Serverless Inference payload limit.

The prediction completes within 60 seconds, which also fits the serverless processing window.

Because the workload does not require a continuously running endpoint, keeping a real-time endpoint provisioned all day would waste compute.

SageMaker Serverless Inference solves this by provisioning resources only when the endpoint is invoked.

This provides a pay-per-use deployment model and eliminates idle instance cost between runs.

Setting MaxConcurrency to 1 is appropriate because the workload sends only one scheduled request at a time.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Intermittent traffic: Use Serverless Inference.

    Small payload: 3.5 MB fits the serverless payload limit.

    Short processing time: 60 seconds fits the serverless execution window.

    Cost efficiency: Avoid always-on endpoint billing.

    Minimal infrastructure management: Let SageMaker provision and scale compute automatically.

    Single scheduled request: Set MaxConcurrency to 1.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Intermittent or once-daily inference + payload ≤ 4 MB + processing ≤ 60 seconds + avoid idle cost = SageMaker Serverless Inference.

Remember:

    Serverless Inference: Best for intermittent workloads with small payloads and short processing times.

    Real-time Inference: Best for sustained low-latency traffic with persistent endpoints.

    Asynchronous Inference: Best for large payloads or long-running inference requests.

    Batch Transform: Best for offline bulk scoring over large datasets.

    MaxConcurrency: Controls how many concurrent invocations a serverless endpoint can process.

    No idle cost: Strong signal for serverless inference.

Official AWS references.

    Amazon SageMaker deployment options.

    Amazon SageMaker Serverless Inference.

    Invoke a SageMaker Serverless Inference endpoint.

    Deploy models to Amazon SageMaker real-time endpoints.

Key exam terms: Serverless Inference, intermittent traffic, payload size limit, MaxConcurrency, once-daily scheduled inference, no idle cost, Asynchronous Inference, Batch Transform.
