Question 28Skipped

An aerospace and defense contractor, OrbitShield Systems, stores encrypted sensor telemetry and satellite imagery datasets in an Amazon S3 bucket.


The S3 bucket uses server-side encryption with AWS KMS keys (SSE-KMS) to secure the classified data.


A data scientist needs to use an Amazon SageMaker notebook instance to analyze the datasets stored in the bucket. The solution must ensure that the notebook instance can access the data in the S3 bucket and decrypt the data using the KMS key, while adhering to AWS best practices for security and permissions. 


Which options can meet these requirements independently? (Select TWO.)


Add the SageMaker notebook instance's IAM role to the S3 bucket's access control list (ACL) and include the kms:Decrypt action in the IAM role's policy
Explanation
Incorrect. Using S3 bucket access control lists (ACLs) to grant permissions to an IAM role is an outdated and discouraged approach. AWS recommends using IAM identity-based policies and S3 bucket policies for fine-grained access control rather than ACLs. Furthermore, S3 ACLs do not natively integrate with the permission model required for AWS KMS key operations, making this pattern insecure and difficult to manage at scale — especially in a regulated environment such as aerospace and defense. Keyword tip: when you see 'bucket ACL + IAM role' in an MLA-C01 scenario, recognize it as a legacy pattern that violates AWS security best practices and cannot independently satisfy KMS decryption requirements.
Update the security group of the SageMaker notebook instance to allow inbound access from the S3 bucket and outbound access to the KMS key
Explanation
Incorrect. Security groups operate at the network layer and control inbound and outbound TCP/IP traffic at the EC2 instance level. They do not govern AWS IAM permissions, S3 bucket access, or AWS KMS key operations. Modifying a security group cannot grant a SageMaker notebook instance the ability to read S3 objects or decrypt KMS-encrypted data, because S3 and KMS access is governed entirely by IAM identity-based policies and resource-based policies, not by network-level controls. Keyword tip: whenever an MLA-C01 scenario involves S3 data access and KMS decryption, security group modifications are irrelevant distractors — these are IAM and resource policy concerns, not VPC network policy concerns.
Correct selection
Attach an IAM role to the SageMaker notebook instance with s3:GetObject permissions for the S3 bucket and kms:Decrypt permissions for the KMS key
Explanation
Correct. Attaching an IAM role to the SageMaker notebook instance with s3:GetObject permissions for the S3 bucket and kms:Decrypt permissions for the KMS key is the most straightforward and AWS-recommended approach. The IAM role grants the notebook instance the ability to retrieve SSE-KMS encrypted objects from S3 and decrypt them using the specified KMS key, without requiring modifications to the S3 bucket policy or the KMS key policy. This self-contained, identity-based approach follows the principle of least privilege and is the canonical pattern for granting SageMaker notebook instances access to encrypted S3 data. Keyword tip: 'IAM role + s3:GetObject + kms:Decrypt defined entirely in the role's policy' is the canonical, self-contained solution for SageMaker access to SSE-KMS encrypted S3 data.
Use the SageMaker notebook instance's IAM role to configure an inline policy that grants kms:Decrypt permissions for the KMS key and s3:GetBucketAcl permissions for the S3 bucket
Explanation
Incorrect. While including kms:Decrypt in an inline policy is partially correct, specifying s3:GetBucketAcl instead of s3:GetObject means the notebook instance can only inspect the bucket's ACL metadata — it cannot retrieve or read the actual objects stored within the bucket. Without s3:GetObject, the notebook instance is unable to access the encrypted telemetry datasets, rendering this configuration incomplete and non-functional for the stated requirement. Keyword tip: 's3:GetBucketAcl' is a bucket metadata permission, not an object read permission — it does not allow reading object contents, and its presence in an option is a strong signal of an incorrect answer in S3 data access scenarios.
Correct selection
Grant the SageMaker notebook instance's IAM role s3:GetObject permissions for the S3 bucket. Include the role's ARN in the KMS key policy to allow kms:Decrypt permissions
Explanation
Correct. Granting the SageMaker notebook instance's IAM role s3:GetObject permissions for the S3 bucket and explicitly adding the role's ARN to the KMS key policy to allow kms:Decrypt is a valid and independently functional approach. This method distributes the permission grant across two policy types — the IAM role policy for S3 object access and the KMS key resource policy for decryption authorization — which is fully supported by AWS and commonly used when centralized KMS key governance is required, such as in defense or compliance-driven environments. While slightly more operationally complex than consolidating all permissions in the IAM role alone, this dual-policy approach is an accepted AWS pattern for tightly controlling KMS key usage through explicit key policies. Keyword tip: 'IAM role for s3:GetObject + role ARN explicitly listed in the KMS key policy for kms:Decrypt' is a valid resource-policy-based alternative to the all-in-IAM-role approach, and both patterns are independently sufficient.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of IAM, Amazon S3, and AWS KMS permissions for accessing SSE-KMS encrypted data from an Amazon SageMaker notebook instance.

It focuses on understanding that reading encrypted objects requires both s3:GetObject permission and kms:Decrypt permission.

It is tied to the ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Add the SageMaker notebook instance's IAM role to the S3 bucket's access control list (ACL) and include the kms:Decrypt action in the IAM role's policy: This option is incorrect. Amazon S3 ACLs are a legacy access control mechanism and are not the recommended way to grant access to an IAM role. Also, ACLs do not solve the full AWS KMS authorization requirement.

    Attach an IAM role to the SageMaker notebook instance with s3:GetObject permissions for the S3 bucket and kms:Decrypt permissions for the KMS key: This is a correct option. The Amazon SageMaker notebook instance uses its attached IAM role. That role needs s3:GetObject to read the object and kms:Decrypt to decrypt the SSE-KMS encrypted data.

    Use the SageMaker notebook instance's IAM role to configure an inline policy that grants kms:Decrypt permissions for the KMS key and s3:GetBucketAcl permissions for the S3 bucket: This option is incorrect. s3:GetBucketAcl only allows reading bucket ACL metadata. It does not allow the notebook instance to read object contents. The required object-level permission is s3:GetObject.

    Update the security group of the SageMaker notebook instance to allow inbound access from the S3 bucket and outbound access to the KMS key: This option is incorrect. Security groups are network controls. They do not grant Amazon S3 object access or AWS KMS decrypt permissions.

    Grant the SageMaker notebook instance's IAM role s3:GetObject permissions for the S3 bucket. Include the role's ARN in the KMS key policy to allow kms:Decrypt permissions: This is also a correct option. The IAM role can grant s3:GetObject, while the AWS KMS key policy explicitly allows that role to use kms:Decrypt.

Why IAM role permissions and KMS key policy permissions fit this scenario.

The notebook instance needs to read data from Amazon S3.

The objects are encrypted with SSE-KMS.

That means two authorization checks must succeed.

First, the Amazon SageMaker notebook instance needs permission to read the object.

That is handled with s3:GetObject.

Second, the notebook instance needs permission to use the AWS KMS key that encrypted the object.

That is handled with kms:Decrypt.

One valid approach is to attach an IAM role to the notebook instance and grant both s3:GetObject and kms:Decrypt through the role policy.

Another valid approach is to grant s3:GetObject through the IAM role and explicitly allow kms:Decrypt in the AWS KMS key policy for that role ARN.

Both approaches can work.

The first approach is simpler and self-contained.

The second approach is common when teams want tighter centralized governance over AWS KMS key usage.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Read encrypted S3 objects: Grant s3:GetObject.

    Decrypt SSE-KMS data: Grant kms:Decrypt.

    Use notebook execution identity: Attach permissions to the Amazon SageMaker notebook instance role.

    Support centralized KMS governance: Use the AWS KMS key policy when required.

    Avoid legacy controls: Do not rely on Amazon S3 ACLs.

    Avoid network-layer confusion: Do not use security groups for IAM or AWS KMS authorization.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

SageMaker notebook + SSE-KMS encrypted S3 data = s3:GetObject + kms:Decrypt.

Remember:

    s3:GetObject: Allows reading object contents from Amazon S3.

    kms:Decrypt: Allows decrypting data encrypted with an AWS KMS key.

    IAM role: Execution identity used by the Amazon SageMaker notebook instance.

    AWS KMS key policy: Resource-based policy that can explicitly allow key usage.

    s3:GetBucketAcl: Reads ACL metadata only; it does not read objects.

    Security groups: Network controls, not data authorization controls.

    Amazon S3 ACLs: Legacy pattern; prefer IAM policies and bucket policies.

Official AWS references.

    Using KMS keys in IAM policies.

    AWS KMS key policy overview.

    Using server-side encryption with AWS KMS keys in Amazon S3.

    Amazon SageMaker roles.

Key exam terms: SSE-KMS, s3:GetObject, kms:Decrypt, IAM role, AWS KMS key policy, Amazon SageMaker notebook instance, resource-based policy, least privilege.

Question 29Skipped

A media streaming company, StreamVault Media, needs to fine-tune a large language model (LLM) for a technical document summarization application. The dataset includes anonymized content licensing reports and operational summaries, stored in Amazon S3.


The company requires a low-code/no-code (LCNC) solution to simplify the fine-tuning process while ensuring scalability and fast deployment.


The solution should leverage Amazon SageMaker capabilities to minimize manual coding and operational overhead. 


Which of the following Amazon SageMaker services can be used to implement the solution? (Select THREE.)
Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor is a production monitoring service that continuously evaluates deployed model quality, detects data drift and concept drift, and generates CloudWatch alerts when statistical deviations exceed defined thresholds. It operates entirely post-deployment and plays no role in fine-tuning, training, or building LLMs. Keyword tip: when an MLA-C01 scenario involves detecting data drift, monitoring prediction quality of a live endpoint, or triggering alerts on model degradation in production, Model Monitor is the appropriate service — not a training or fine-tuning tool.
Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler is a data preparation feature within SageMaker Studio that provides a visual interface for importing, transforming, featurizing, and analyzing datasets. While it reduces coding effort for data engineering tasks, it does not support model training, LLM fine-tuning, or model deployment of any kind. Its scope is strictly limited to the data preparation phase of the ML pipeline. Keyword tip: when a question centers on visual data transformation, feature engineering workflows, or preparing datasets for downstream training without any model-building component, Data Wrangler is the appropriate choice — not a training or fine-tuning service.
Amazon SageMaker Clarify
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Clarify is designed to detect bias in datasets and models and to provide model explainability through feature attribution reports such as SHAP values. While it is a valuable component of responsible AI practices, it does not offer fine-tuning capabilities for LLMs and does not qualify as a low-code/no-code solution for model training or deployment. Keyword tip: when a question focuses on fairness auditing, bias detection, or post-hoc model explainability rather than training or fine-tuning an LLM, Clarify is the correct service to select.
Correct selection
Amazon SageMaker Autopilot
Explanation
Correct. Amazon SageMaker Autopilot provides a low-code automated machine learning (AutoML) capability that handles data preprocessing, algorithm selection, and hyperparameter optimization with minimal user intervention. Users supply a labeled dataset and a target variable, and Autopilot generates fully transparent Jupyter notebooks that can be reviewed and customized. This makes it suitable for organizations seeking automation with retained visibility into the model-building process. Keyword tip: when an MLA-C01 scenario mentions AutoML, transparent model generation, or low-code training with explainable steps on tabular or text datasets, Autopilot is the primary candidate to identify.
Correct selection
Amazon SageMaker JumpStart
Explanation
Correct. Amazon SageMaker JumpStart is a model hub that provides access to hundreds of pre-trained foundation models and LLMs, including models for text summarization, question answering, and text generation. It enables users to fine-tune these models on custom datasets through a graphical interface in SageMaker Studio with minimal configuration, requiring no deep ML expertise. This makes it the most directly relevant LCNC service for LLM fine-tuning scenarios such as StreamVault Media's content summarization use case. Keyword tip: whenever an MLA-C01 scenario involves fine-tuning a pre-trained LLM or foundation model with low-code tooling and rapid deployment from a model hub, JumpStart is the key service to identify.
Correct selection
Amazon SageMaker Canvas
Explanation
Correct. Amazon SageMaker Canvas is a fully no-code visual interface that enables business analysts and non-technical users to build, train, and deploy ML models without writing any code. It provides a point-and-click experience for data preparation, model training via built-in AutoML, and generating predictions. Canvas connects directly to Amazon S3 and other data sources, making it well-suited for LCNC scenarios where operational overhead must be minimized and coding expertise is unavailable. Keyword tip: when a scenario requires a zero-code interface for non-developers or business stakeholders to build and deploy ML models, Canvas is the defining service to recognize.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests the ability to identify Amazon SageMaker low-code/no-code (LCNC) services for model development and LLM fine-tuning scenarios.

It focuses on distinguishing services used for model building and fine-tuning from services used for bias analysis, monitoring, and data preparation.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Amazon SageMaker Autopilot: This is a correct option. SageMaker Autopilot provides low-code AutoML capabilities. It can automate data preprocessing, algorithm selection, model training, and hyperparameter tuning while generating transparent notebooks that can be reviewed and customized.

    Amazon SageMaker Clarify: This option is incorrect. SageMaker Clarify is used for bias detection and model explainability. It helps with responsible AI analysis, but it is not a service for training, fine-tuning, or deploying LLMs.

    Amazon SageMaker Canvas: This is a correct option. SageMaker Canvas provides a no-code visual interface for building, training, and deploying ML models. It is designed for business analysts and non-technical users who need ML capabilities without writing code.

    Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor: This option is incorrect. SageMaker Model Monitor is used after deployment to monitor data quality, model quality, bias drift, and feature attribution drift. It does not build or fine-tune models.

    Amazon SageMaker JumpStart: This is a correct option. SageMaker JumpStart provides access to pre-trained models and foundation models, including models for text generation, summarization, and question answering. It supports fine-tuning selected models on custom datasets with minimal setup.

    Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler: This option is incorrect. SageMaker Data Wrangler is used for data preparation, transformation, feature engineering, and visual analysis. It does not perform model training or LLM fine-tuning.

Why SageMaker Autopilot, Canvas, and JumpStart fit this scenario.

The scenario requires low-code/no-code capabilities for model development and LLM fine-tuning.

That means the correct services must reduce manual coding effort while still helping the team build or customize models.

Amazon SageMaker Autopilot supports low-code AutoML.

It helps automate model development tasks such as preprocessing, algorithm selection, training, and hyperparameter tuning.

It is useful when the team wants automation but still wants transparency into the generated workflow.

Amazon SageMaker Canvas supports no-code model building.

It provides a visual interface that allows users to import data, build models, evaluate results, and generate predictions without writing code.

This makes it suitable when the scenario emphasizes non-technical users or minimal coding.

Amazon SageMaker JumpStart is the strongest fit for the LLM-specific part of the scenario.

It provides a model hub with pre-trained models and foundation models.

For LLM use cases such as summarization, text generation, and question answering, JumpStart can reduce time to deployment and support customization with less manual engineering.

The other services are important, but they belong to different stages of the ML lifecycle.

Clarify is for bias and explainability.

Model Monitor is for post-deployment monitoring.

Data Wrangler is for data preparation.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Low-code model development: Use SageMaker Autopilot.

    No-code visual ML workflow: Use SageMaker Canvas.

    LLM or foundation model fine-tuning: Use SageMaker JumpStart.

    Minimal manual coding: Prefer LCNC SageMaker services.

    Avoid monitoring distractors: Do not choose Model Monitor for training.

    Avoid data-prep distractors: Do not choose Data Wrangler when the task is model development or fine-tuning.

Question 30Skipped

You are an AI/ML engineer at PropVision Realty Group, a commercial real estate firm that is rapidly expanding its use of generative AI and machine learning to create personalized property search experiences, automate lease document analysis, and generate market valuation insights.


The firm is exploring AWS services to quickly prototype and deploy both generative AI models and traditional machine learning models with minimal operational overhead.


The team is particularly interested in services that provide pre-built models, templates, and the ability to scale solutions into production seamlessly. Given these requirements, which of the following statements BEST highlights the differences between Amazon Bedrock and Amazon SageMaker JumpStart to help your team make an informed decision? 

Amazon Bedrock provides a simplified interface for training and tuning models from scratch, while Amazon SageMaker JumpStart is primarily for deploying third-party models with limited customization.
Explanation
Incorrect. This option misrepresents Amazon Bedrock as a service for training models from scratch, which is fundamentally incorrect. Bedrock does not provide tooling for training foundation models from scratch; it is focused on inference, fine-tuning via continued pre-training, and application development using pre-trained foundation models. Additionally, SageMaker JumpStart is not limited to deploying third-party models with constrained customization — it provides a rich set of tools including full-featured notebooks, algorithms, and solution templates that support a wide range of customization and fine-tuning scenarios across the ML lifecycle. Keyword tip: if an option positions Bedrock as a model training service for building models from scratch, it is incorrect by definition and represents a common MLA-C01 distractor pattern.
Amazon Bedrock is ideal for the quick deployment of computer vision models, while Amazon SageMaker JumpStart specializes in deploying natural language processing models.
Explanation
Incorrect. This option incorrectly implies that each service specializes in a single modality — computer vision for Bedrock and NLP for JumpStart. In reality, both services are highly versatile across modalities. Amazon Bedrock supports a variety of generative AI tasks including text generation, image generation (e.g., via Stability AI models), summarization, question answering, and more. Amazon SageMaker JumpStart similarly spans multiple domains including NLP, computer vision, tabular data, and generative AI. Neither service is architecturally confined to a single modality or use case. Keyword tip: options that narrowly assign a single ML modality — such as CV or NLP only — to multi-purpose AWS ML services like Bedrock or JumpStart are classic MLA-C01 distractors designed to test breadth-of-service awareness.
Amazon Bedrock is designed specifically for building and deploying custom machine learning models, while Amazon SageMaker JumpStart is tailored for deploying pre-trained large language models (LLMs) with minimal customization.
Explanation
Incorrect. This option inverts the actual roles of the two services. Amazon Bedrock is not designed for building and deploying custom machine learning models from scratch — its primary purpose is to provide access to pre-trained foundation models from providers such as Anthropic, AI21 Labs, Cohere, Meta, and Amazon through a single managed API. Conversely, Amazon SageMaker JumpStart is not limited to deploying LLMs; it offers a broad ML hub encompassing pre-built algorithms, notebooks, and both traditional ML and generative AI model solutions that span multiple domains. Keyword tip: whenever an option claims Bedrock is primarily for custom model training or development from scratch, treat it as a distractor in questions comparing Bedrock to other SageMaker capabilities.
Correct answer
Amazon Bedrock focuses on providing a managed service for deploying pre-trained foundation models from various providers, whereas Amazon SageMaker JumpStart offers a range of pre-built solutions, including models, notebooks, and algorithms for both machine learning and generative AI use cases.
Explanation
Correct. This option accurately captures the architectural and functional distinction between the two services. Amazon Bedrock is a fully managed service that provides access to high-performing foundation models (FMs) from multiple leading AI providers via a single API, with built-in security, privacy, and responsible AI capabilities — optimized for generative AI application development and production scaling. Amazon SageMaker JumpStart, by contrast, is a comprehensive ML hub that accelerates the ML journey by offering pre-built solution templates, algorithms, and Jupyter notebooks, supporting both traditional machine learning workflows and generative AI use cases across the full development lifecycle. Keyword tip: Bedrock = managed, unified API access to third-party and Amazon FMs for generative AI applications; JumpStart = broad ML hub with notebooks, algorithms, and diverse model types spanning traditional ML and generative AI.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests understanding of the difference between Amazon Bedrock and Amazon SageMaker JumpStart.

It focuses on distinguishing managed access to foundation models through a unified API from a broader ML hub that includes models, notebooks, algorithms, and solution templates.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Amazon Bedrock is designed specifically for building and deploying custom machine learning models, while Amazon SageMaker JumpStart is tailored for deploying pre-trained large language models with minimal customization: This option is incorrect. Amazon Bedrock is not primarily for building custom ML models from scratch. It provides managed access to pre-trained foundation models. Amazon SageMaker JumpStart is also not limited to LLM deployment; it includes models, notebooks, algorithms, and solution templates across multiple ML domains.

    Amazon Bedrock focuses on providing a managed service for deploying pre-trained foundation models from various providers, whereas Amazon SageMaker JumpStart offers a range of pre-built solutions, including models, notebooks, and algorithms for both machine learning and generative AI use cases: This is the correct option. Amazon Bedrock provides managed access to foundation models through a unified API. Amazon SageMaker JumpStart is a broader ML hub with pre-built assets for traditional ML and generative AI workflows.

    Amazon Bedrock provides a simplified interface for training and tuning models from scratch, while Amazon SageMaker JumpStart is primarily for deploying third-party models with limited customization: This option is incorrect. Amazon Bedrock is not a model-training-from-scratch service. It is used to build generative AI applications with pre-trained foundation models. Amazon SageMaker JumpStart supports a broader set of ML assets and customization paths than this option suggests.

    Amazon Bedrock is ideal for the quick deployment of computer vision models, while Amazon SageMaker JumpStart specializes in deploying natural language processing models: This option is incorrect. Both services support multiple modalities and use cases. Neither service should be reduced to only computer vision or only NLP.

Why Bedrock and JumpStart fit this distinction.

The question asks for the correct difference between two AWS services that accelerate ML and generative AI adoption.

Amazon Bedrock is focused on foundation model access and generative AI application development.

It provides a managed API for using foundation models from Amazon and third-party model providers.

This allows teams to build applications such as chatbots, summarization tools, content generation systems, and retrieval-augmented generation workflows without managing model infrastructure.

Amazon SageMaker JumpStart is different.

It is a broader ML hub inside the Amazon SageMaker ecosystem.

It provides pre-built models, notebooks, algorithms, and solution templates.

Teams can use it to accelerate traditional ML projects, computer vision workflows, NLP use cases, and generative AI experimentation.

So the key distinction is scope.

Amazon Bedrock is the managed foundation model service for generative AI applications.

Amazon SageMaker JumpStart is the broader ML acceleration hub with reusable assets for multiple ML workflows.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Foundation model access: Use Amazon Bedrock.

    Unified managed API: Use Amazon Bedrock for managed FM consumption.

    Pre-built ML assets: Use Amazon SageMaker JumpStart.

    Traditional ML and generative AI: Use Amazon SageMaker JumpStart for broader ML coverage.

    Avoid custom training confusion: Do not treat Amazon Bedrock as a from-scratch model training service.

    Avoid modality narrowing: Do not reduce either service to only NLP or only computer vision.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Managed access to foundation models through a unified API = Amazon Bedrock.

Pre-built models, notebooks, algorithms, and ML solution templates = Amazon SageMaker JumpStart.

Remember:

    Amazon Bedrock: Fully managed service for building generative AI applications with foundation models.

    Foundation models: Pre-trained models used for tasks such as text generation, summarization, chat, image generation, and embeddings.

    Amazon SageMaker JumpStart: ML hub with pre-built models, notebooks, algorithms, and solutions.

    Custom training from scratch: Not the primary purpose of Amazon Bedrock.

    Traditional ML acceleration: Stronger signal for Amazon SageMaker JumpStart.

    Generative AI application API: Stronger signal for Amazon Bedrock.

Question 31Skipped

A food and beverage producer, FreshHarvest Foods, is building an application that uses multiple scikit-learn models to predict ingredient spoilage risk factors based on sensor data collected across its production lines. The company wants to deploy these models using Amazon SageMaker to serve real-time predictions with low latency.

The solution must be cost-effective while ensuring efficient traffic handling for all deployed models. 

Which of the following options must be combined to develop a solution that meets these requirements? (Select TWO.)
Deploy each scikit-learn model individually on separate SageMaker Real-Time Endpoints to ensure traffic is isolated for each model
Explanation
Incorrect. Deploying each scikit-learn model on its own dedicated SageMaker Real-Time Endpoint maximizes traffic isolation but directly contradicts the cost-effectiveness requirement — each endpoint requires its own continuously provisioned compute instance. Managing dozens of separate endpoints for FreshHarvest Foods' production-line sensors would also significantly increase operational complexity. Keyword tip: 'separate endpoints per model' is a distractor whenever cost optimization and multi-model hosting are explicitly stated requirements in the scenario.
Correct selection
Use Amazon SageMaker Multi-Model Endpoint to host multiple scikit-learn models in one container behind a single endpoint, reducing infrastructure costs
Explanation
Correct. Amazon SageMaker Multi-Model Endpoints (MME) allow a single endpoint to host thousands of models within a shared inference container, dynamically loading and unloading models from memory as needed. This approach significantly reduces infrastructure costs because all models share the same compute resources rather than requiring dedicated instances per model. For scikit-learn workloads, SageMaker provides a built-in MME-compatible container, making this a natural fit for FreshHarvest Foods' multi-model deployment. Keyword tip: when the scenario requires hosting multiple models cost-effectively on SageMaker, Multi-Model Endpoint is the primary solution to identify.
Use SageMaker Serverless Inference to deploy each model and configure an AWS Application Load Balancer (ALB) to route traffic across all model endpoints
Explanation
Incorrect. SageMaker Serverless Inference is designed for sporadic, intermittent traffic patterns where cold-start latency is acceptable, making it unsuitable for the consistently low-latency, high-volume real-time predictions required by FreshHarvest Foods' production-line monitoring. Furthermore, routing traffic through an AWS Application Load Balancer across multiple serverless endpoints introduces unnecessary architectural complexity and does not leverage SageMaker's native multi-model hosting capabilities. Keyword tip: a 'Serverless Inference + ALB' combination is a distractor for real-time, low-latency multi-model scenarios — the canonical solution is MME with Real-Time Inference.
Package all scikit-learn models into separate containers and deploy them behind a single SageMaker Real-Time Endpoint
Explanation
Incorrect. SageMaker endpoints cannot natively host multiple independent containers behind a single endpoint without leveraging the Multi-Model Endpoint (MME) feature. Packaging each model into a separate container and attempting to unify them behind one endpoint without MME requires custom orchestration logic, substantially increasing architectural complexity and operational overhead. This approach also fails to provide the dynamic model loading and shared compute that make MME cost-effective. Keyword tip: when you see 'separate containers behind a single endpoint' without explicit mention of the Multi-Model Endpoint feature, treat it as a distractor — MME is the AWS-managed solution for consolidating multiple models under one endpoint.
Correct selection
Use SageMaker Real-Time Inference to deploy the Multi-Model Endpoint for low-latency predictions and optimized traffic handling
Explanation
Correct. SageMaker Real-Time Inference is the appropriate inference type when the workload demands consistently low latency and synchronous, interactive predictions. Deploying a Multi-Model Endpoint using Real-Time Inference ensures that live spoilage-risk requests are served with minimal latency, the endpoint is fully managed, and auto scaling can be configured to handle traffic spikes from multiple production lines. This deployment mode pairs directly with the MME feature to satisfy both the low-latency and cost-efficiency requirements simultaneously. Keyword tip: 'real-time, low latency, synchronous inference' signals SageMaker Real-Time Inference as the correct deployment type, distinguishing it from Batch Transform (offline bulk processing) or Serverless Inference (intermittent, cold-start-tolerant workloads).
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of Amazon SageMaker multi-model hosting and the correct inference deployment mode for real-time, cost-sensitive workloads.

It focuses on combining Amazon SageMaker Multi-Model Endpoints with SageMaker Real-Time Inference when the scenario requires multiple models, low latency, and cost optimization.

It is tied to the Deployment and Orchestration of ML Workflows domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Package all scikit-learn models into separate containers and deploy them behind a single SageMaker Real-Time Endpoint: This option is incorrect. A standard SageMaker real-time endpoint does not automatically provide managed multi-model hosting across separate independent containers. This approach would require custom routing or orchestration and would not provide the native dynamic model loading benefits of Multi-Model Endpoints.

    Use Amazon SageMaker Multi-Model Endpoint to host multiple scikit-learn models in one container behind a single endpoint, reducing infrastructure costs: This is a correct option. Amazon SageMaker Multi-Model Endpoints allow multiple models to be hosted behind a single endpoint using shared compute resources. Models are loaded dynamically as needed, which reduces the cost of serving many models.

    Deploy each scikit-learn model individually on separate SageMaker Real-Time Endpoints to ensure traffic is isolated for each model: This option is incorrect. Separate SageMaker Real-Time Endpoints provide isolation, but they also require dedicated provisioned infrastructure for each model. That increases cost and operational overhead, which conflicts with the cost-effectiveness requirement.

    Use SageMaker Real-Time Inference to deploy the Multi-Model Endpoint for low-latency predictions and optimized traffic handling: This is a correct option. SageMaker Real-Time Inference is the right deployment mode when applications need synchronous, consistently low-latency predictions.

    Use SageMaker Serverless Inference to deploy each model and configure an AWS Application Load Balancer (ALB) to route traffic across all model endpoints: This option is incorrect. SageMaker Serverless Inference is better suited for intermittent traffic where cold starts are acceptable. Adding an Application Load Balancer across multiple endpoints adds unnecessary complexity and does not use native SageMaker multi-model hosting.

Why Multi-Model Endpoint with Real-Time Inference fits this scenario.

The scenario requires hosting multiple scikit-learn models.

The company wants a cost-effective deployment.

The predictions must be served with low latency.

That points to Amazon SageMaker Multi-Model Endpoints deployed with SageMaker Real-Time Inference.

A Multi-Model Endpoint allows many models to share the same endpoint and compute infrastructure.

Instead of provisioning one endpoint per model, the models are stored externally and loaded into the inference container when needed.

This reduces infrastructure cost and operational complexity.

SageMaker Real-Time Inference is the correct deployment mode because the application requires synchronous predictions with low latency.

This is different from Batch Transform, which is for offline batch inference.

It is also different from SageMaker Serverless Inference, which is better for intermittent workloads that can tolerate cold-start latency.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Multiple models: Use Amazon SageMaker Multi-Model Endpoints.

    Cost optimization: Share compute resources across models.

    Low-latency predictions: Use SageMaker Real-Time Inference.

    Managed deployment: Avoid custom routing and manual orchestration.

    Dynamic model loading: Load models on demand behind one endpoint.

    Avoid separate endpoint sprawl: Do not deploy one endpoint per model when cost is a priority.

Question 32Skipped

An aerospace and defense contractor, OrbitShield Systems, has a significant amount of operational telemetry and sensor data stored in an Amazon DynamoDB database.


As part of a new machine learning project, a data engineer needs to access this data from an Amazon SageMaker notebook for analysis and model development.


The goal is to ensure that the data is efficiently and directly accessible from the notebook while maintaining performance and minimizing potential delays. Which approach best satisfies these requirements?



Use Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler to directly import data from the Amazon DynamoDB database into the SageMaker notebook.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler does not support direct data import from Amazon DynamoDB. Data Wrangler's supported data sources are limited to Amazon S3, Amazon Athena, Amazon Redshift, and Snowflake; DynamoDB is explicitly excluded. Selecting this option would not fulfill the requirement and reflects a common misconception that Data Wrangler is a universal data ingestion tool. Keyword tip: Memorize SageMaker Data Wrangler's supported sources — S3, Athena, Redshift, and Snowflake — and recognize DynamoDB as an unsupported source in any Data Wrangler scenario.
Export the DynamoDB table into a .csv file, upload the file to an Amazon S3 bucket, and configure the SageMaker notebook to connect to the S3 bucket for accessing the data.
Explanation
Incorrect. Manually exporting a DynamoDB table to a .csv file and uploading it to Amazon S3 is a functional but highly suboptimal workflow for ML notebook development. This approach requires manual intervention at each iteration, does not reflect real-time or updated data, and introduces unnecessary steps that contradict the requirement for efficiency and minimized delays. While it may be acceptable as a one-time data migration, it is an anti-pattern for iterative ML development workflows. Keyword tip: Manual CSV export followed by S3 upload is an anti-pattern for interactive ML notebook workflows — prefer programmatic or automated ingestion methods when efficiency and freshness are required.
Correct answer
Use the AWS SDK for Python (Boto3) to query DynamoDB directly from the SageMaker notebook.
Explanation
Correct. Using the AWS SDK for Python (Boto3) to query DynamoDB directly from a SageMaker notebook is the most direct and efficient approach for accessing DynamoDB data during exploratory analysis and model development. A DynamoDB client is initialized within the notebook, and Scan or Query operations retrieve the required data in JSON format, which can then be converted into a pandas DataFrame for further processing. This approach requires no intermediate storage layer or ETL job, thereby minimizing latency and operational overhead. Keyword tip: The Boto3 DynamoDB client with Scan or Query operations is the standard pattern for direct, programmatic DynamoDB access from SageMaker notebooks when minimal delays and no intermediary steps are required.
Use AWS Glue to transfer the DynamoDB table to an Amazon S3 bucket, then configure the SageMaker notebook to connect to the S3 bucket for accessing the data.
Explanation
Incorrect. Using AWS Glue to transfer the DynamoDB table to Amazon S3 before accessing it from a SageMaker notebook is a recognized pattern for large-scale or recurring ETL workflows, but it introduces additional pipeline complexity and latency compared to querying DynamoDB directly. A separate Glue job must be authored, scheduled, and monitored, adding overhead that is unnecessary for interactive notebook exploration. This approach is not optimal when the stated goal is efficient and direct access with minimal delays. Keyword tip: AWS Glue DynamoDB-to-S3 connectors are best suited for scheduled batch ETL pipelines, not ad hoc notebook-based exploratory analysis.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests the ability to choose the most efficient way to access Amazon DynamoDB data from an Amazon SageMaker notebook.

It focuses on using the AWS SDK for Python (Boto3) for direct, programmatic access without intermediate ETL jobs, manual exports, or extra storage layers.

It is tied to the Data Preparation for Machine Learning (ML) domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Use AWS Glue to transfer the DynamoDB table to an Amazon S3 bucket, then configure the SageMaker notebook to connect to the S3 bucket for accessing the data: This option is incorrect. AWS Glue can be used for ETL workflows, but moving data from Amazon DynamoDB to Amazon S3 adds latency, pipeline complexity, and operational overhead. The scenario asks for direct and efficient notebook access.

    Use the AWS SDK for Python (Boto3) to query DynamoDB directly from the SageMaker notebook: This is the correct option. Boto3 allows an Amazon SageMaker notebook to call Amazon DynamoDB APIs directly. The notebook can use Query or Scan operations and then convert the returned data into a pandas DataFrame for analysis and model development.

    Use Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler to directly import data from the Amazon DynamoDB database into the SageMaker notebook: This option is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler does not directly support Amazon DynamoDB as a data source. Its supported sources include services such as Amazon S3, Amazon Athena, Amazon Redshift, and Snowflake.

    Export the DynamoDB table into a .csv file, upload the file to an Amazon S3 bucket, and configure the SageMaker notebook to connect to the S3 bucket for accessing the data: This option is incorrect. Manual CSV export introduces unnecessary delay and manual work. It is not suitable for efficient, iterative ML development when direct programmatic access is available.

Why Boto3 direct access fits this scenario.

The data is stored in Amazon DynamoDB.

The user is working from an Amazon SageMaker notebook.

The requirement is direct access with minimal delay.

That points to Boto3.

With Boto3, the notebook can initialize an Amazon DynamoDB client or resource and retrieve data directly.

For targeted access, the notebook can use Query.

For broader table reads, it can use Scan, although scan operations should be used carefully for large tables because they can be expensive and slow.

The returned records can then be transformed into a pandas DataFrame for exploration, feature engineering, and model development.

This avoids creating an AWS Glue ETL job.

It avoids exporting data to Amazon S3.

It avoids manual CSV workflows.

It also avoids relying on Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler, which does not directly import from Amazon DynamoDB.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Direct notebook access: Use Boto3.

    DynamoDB-native reads: Use Query or Scan.

    Minimal latency: Avoid intermediate ETL or export steps.

    No extra storage layer: Do not copy the data to Amazon S3 unless a batch pipeline requires it.

    Interactive ML development: Load results into pandas for exploration.

    Avoid unsupported sources: Do not use Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler for direct Amazon DynamoDB import.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

SageMaker notebook + direct DynamoDB access + minimal delay = Boto3 with Query or Scan.

Remember:

    Boto3: Direct AWS API access from Python.

    Amazon DynamoDB Query: Efficient when using partition keys and sort key conditions.

    Amazon DynamoDB Scan: Reads across the table; useful but less efficient for large datasets.

    AWS Glue: ETL and batch movement, not the lowest-overhead direct notebook pattern.

    Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler: Visual data preparation, but no direct Amazon DynamoDB import.

    Manual CSV export: High-friction and stale for iterative ML workflows.

Question 33Skipped

An education technology company, LearnPath Analytics, manages a data lake using AWS Lake Formation to store and analyze learner behavior and content engagement data.


The data lake contains both structured and unstructured data, including course metadata, learner activity logs, and clickstream data.


The company's data analysts are assigned to specific content categories, such as STEM, humanities, or professional development. Each analyst needs access to only the data relevant to their assigned category for analysis through Amazon Athena. The company wants to ensure fine-grained access control while reducing operational complexity. 


Which solution will meet these requirements?


Create separate S3 buckets for each content category and assign bucket-level permissions to analysts based on their roles.
Explanation
Incorrect. Partitioning data into separate S3 buckets per content category introduces data fragmentation, complicates cross-category analytics, and creates significant operational overhead as the number of categories grows. Bucket-level IAM permissions also do not provide the column-level or row-level granularity that AWS Lake Formation supports, and this architecture does not scale well for large, diverse datasets with evolving category structures. Keyword tip: separate S3 buckets per data domain is an anti-pattern for fine-grained data lake access control — it trades security flexibility for infrastructure complexity and is always inferior to LF-TBAC when Lake Formation is already in use.
Use S3 bucket policies to restrict access to folders corresponding to each content category and configure Athena workgroups for query-level access control.
Explanation
Incorrect. S3 bucket policies operate at the object and prefix level and cannot enforce fine-grained, query-level access controls in Amazon Athena on their own. Athena workgroups control query execution settings such as output location and cost controls, but do not provide data-level access enforcement per content category. Combining these two mechanisms also increases operational complexity significantly as the number of analysts and categories grows. Keyword tip: S3 bucket policies paired with Athena workgroups is a distractor whenever the requirement is fine-grained, attribute-based data access control within a Lake Formation-managed data lake.
Correct answer
Use AWS Lake Formation to define permissions based on Lake Formation tags. Assign category-specific tags to the data and associate them with the analysts' IAM roles for fine-grained access control.
Explanation
Correct. AWS Lake Formation Tag-Based Access Control (LF-TBAC) allows administrators to assign metadata tags (such as 'STEM', 'humanities', or 'professional development') to databases, tables, and columns, then map those tags to IAM principals (roles or users). This approach enforces fine-grained, attribute-driven access control across all integrated query engines, including Amazon Athena, without requiring per-resource policy updates for every new category or analyst. It is operationally efficient because adding new categories or onboarding new analysts only requires updating tag assignments rather than rewriting complex, resource-specific policies. Keyword tip: when a scenario combines a data lake managed by AWS Lake Formation, category-specific data access, Amazon Athena as the query engine, and a requirement to minimize operational overhead, LF-TBAC is the authoritative MLA-C01 answer.
Use AWS Glue workflows to tag data with category-specific tags and manage access using IAM policy conditions based on these tags.
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS Glue workflows are ETL orchestration constructs designed to manage and schedule data transformation jobs, not to enforce access control. While AWS Glue can apply metadata tags to resources in the AWS Glue Data Catalog, it has no authorization engine to interpret or apply tag-based permissions at query time. Access control enforcement within a Lake Formation-managed data lake requires Lake Formation permissions, not IAM policy conditions derived from Glue workflow tags. Keyword tip: AWS Glue is the correct service for data ingestion and transformation; AWS Lake Formation is the correct service for centralized data lake security and fine-grained permission enforcement.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of fine-grained data lake access control using AWS Lake Formation.

It focuses on choosing Lake Formation Tag-Based Access Control (LF-TBAC) when the requirement is scalable, centralized, category-based access control for data queried through Amazon Athena.

It is tied to the ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Use AWS Lake Formation to define permissions based on Lake Formation tags. Assign category-specific tags to the data and associate them with the analysts' IAM roles for fine-grained access control: This is the correct option. AWS Lake Formation supports tag-based access control, allowing administrators to assign LF-Tags to databases, tables, or columns and grant permissions to principals based on those tags.

    Use S3 bucket policies to restrict access to folders corresponding to each content category and configure Athena workgroups for query-level access control: This option is incorrect. Amazon S3 bucket policies operate at the bucket, object, or prefix level. Amazon Athena workgroups control query execution settings and cost controls, but they do not provide fine-grained data-level authorization by content category.

    Use AWS Glue workflows to tag data with category-specific tags and manage access using IAM policy conditions based on these tags: This option is incorrect. AWS Glue workflows orchestrate ETL jobs. They do not enforce fine-grained access control at query time. Access enforcement in a governed data lake should be handled by AWS Lake Formation.

    Create separate S3 buckets for each content category and assign bucket-level permissions to analysts based on their roles: This option is incorrect. Creating separate Amazon S3 buckets per category increases operational complexity and does not provide scalable fine-grained governance. It also makes cross-category analytics harder to manage.

Why Lake Formation tag-based access control fits this scenario.

The scenario requires category-specific access to datasets in a data lake.

Different analysts need access to different data categories.

The data is queried using Amazon Athena.

The solution must reduce operational overhead as users and data categories grow.

That points directly to AWS Lake Formation with Lake Formation Tag-Based Access Control.

With LF-TBAC, administrators define tags such as content category, sensitivity level, business domain, or data classification.

Those tags can be attached to AWS Glue Data Catalog resources such as databases, tables, and columns.

Permissions can then be granted to IAM users or roles based on those tags.

This avoids creating and maintaining separate policies for every individual dataset.

It also avoids splitting data across many Amazon S3 buckets just to enforce access boundaries.

Because AWS Lake Formation integrates with query engines such as Amazon Athena, the access rules are enforced when users query governed data.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Fine-grained access control: Use AWS Lake Formation permissions.

    Category-based access: Assign LF-Tags to databases, tables, or columns.

    Principal-based authorization: Grant access to IAM roles based on tags.

    Athena integration: Enforce permissions for queries through Amazon Athena.

    Operational scalability: Avoid per-resource policy sprawl.

    Centralized governance: Manage permissions through AWS Lake Formation.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Lake Formation data lake + category-specific access + Athena queries + least operational overhead = LF-TBAC.

Remember:

    AWS Lake Formation: Centralized data lake permissions and governance.

    LF-Tags: Metadata tags used for scalable access control.

    LF-TBAC: Tag-based access control for databases, tables, and columns.

    Amazon Athena: Query engine that can enforce AWS Lake Formation permissions.

    Amazon S3 bucket policies: Object or prefix-level controls, not the best fit for fine-grained data lake governance.

    AWS Glue workflows: ETL orchestration, not authorization enforcement.

ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security
Question 34Skipped

A sports analytics company, PeakPlay Analytics, is developing an ML model using the Amazon SageMaker XGBoost algorithm to classify athletes as either high-risk or low-risk for a specific type of musculoskeletal injury.


During evaluation, the model performs exceptionally well on the training dataset but fails to accurately classify new athlete data.


The ML engineer suspects that the model's performance issues may be related to noise in the dataset and needs to optimize the model to improve its performance on unseen data.


As an AWS Certified Machine Learning Engineer Associate, what do you recommend?
Correct answer
Reduce the max_depth parameter in the XGBoost algorithm to prevent the model from overfitting to the training dataset
Explanation
Correct. Reducing the max_depth parameter in the Amazon SageMaker XGBoost algorithm directly limits the depth of each decision tree in the ensemble, thereby reducing model complexity. A high max_depth allows the model to capture very fine-grained, noise-specific patterns in the training data, which leads to overfitting — the condition where the model memorizes training examples instead of learning generalizable patterns. Lowering max_depth forces the model to rely on broader, more statistically robust trends, which improves its ability to generalize to unseen data. Keyword tip: when an XGBoost model excels on training data but degrades on new data (classic overfitting signal), reducing max_depth is the primary hyperparameter intervention to consider on the MLA-C01 exam.
Remove irrelevant features from the training dataset to reduce noise and improve the generalization of the model
Explanation
Incorrect. While removing irrelevant features (feature selection) is a valid data preparation technique that can reduce noise and improve generalization, it addresses the problem at the data level rather than at the model parameter level. In this specific scenario, the question emphasizes optimizing model hyperparameters to address overfitting behavior observed between training and evaluation performance. Feature selection is a complementary practice but is not the most direct lever for controlling model complexity in XGBoost. Keyword tip: when the scenario explicitly points to model complexity and overfitting in XGBoost, look for hyperparameter-based solutions such as max_depth, min_child_weight, or subsample adjustments rather than data preprocessing steps.
Increase the size of the training dataset by duplicating examples of high-risk athlete records to improve the model's recall
Explanation
Incorrect. Duplicating existing examples of high-risk athlete records is a form of naive oversampling that does not introduce new information into the training process. It creates data leakage risk when duplicates appear in both training and validation splits, and it causes the model to overweight repeated examples, resulting in biased training and poor generalization to real-world distributions. Techniques such as SMOTE (Synthetic Minority Over-sampling Technique) are preferable when class imbalance must be addressed. Keyword tip: on MLA-C01, duplicating training samples is always a distractor — it introduces bias and does not represent a sound MLOps data augmentation strategy.
Increase the learning_rate parameter to ensure the model converges faster and performs better on unseen data
Explanation
Incorrect. Increasing the learning_rate (also known as eta in XGBoost) controls the step size shrinkage applied after each boosting round. While a higher learning_rate can speed up convergence, it also increases the risk of overshooting the optimal solution, resulting in suboptimal weight updates and reduced generalization capability. This adjustment does not directly address the model complexity or overfitting issue described in the scenario. Keyword tip: on MLA-C01, increasing learning_rate is associated with faster but potentially less stable convergence — it does not reduce overfitting and can in fact worsen it when combined with a high max_depth setting.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests understanding of XGBoost hyperparameter tuning as a way to control model complexity and reduce overfitting.

It focuses on recognizing that high training performance combined with poor performance on unseen data is a classic overfitting signal, and that reducing max_depth is a direct way to constrain tree complexity in XGBoost.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Remove irrelevant features from the training dataset to reduce noise and improve the generalization of the model: Feature selection can help reduce noise and improve generalization, but it is not the most direct answer here. The scenario points to overfitting in an XGBoost model, so the stronger intervention is to tune the hyperparameter that controls tree complexity.

    Reduce the max_depth parameter in the XGBoost algorithm to prevent the model from overfitting to the training dataset: This is the correct option. max_depth controls how deep each tree can grow. Lowering this value reduces model complexity and helps prevent the model from memorizing noise in the training data.

    Increase the learning_rate parameter to ensure the model converges faster and performs better on unseen data: Increasing learning_rate may make training faster, but it can also make optimization less stable and does not directly reduce overfitting. It may worsen generalization if the model is already too complex.

    Increase the size of the training dataset by duplicating examples of high-risk athlete records to improve the model's recall: Duplicating existing samples does not add new information. It can bias the model, create leakage risk, and degrade generalization. It is not a sound fix for overfitting.

Why reducing max_depth fits this scenario.

The model performs very well on the training dataset.

However, it performs poorly on new, unseen data.

That pattern indicates overfitting.

Overfitting happens when a model learns training-specific noise or overly specific patterns instead of learning generalizable relationships.

In XGBoost, max_depth controls the maximum depth of each decision tree.

A higher max_depth allows trees to create very specific splits.

That can improve training accuracy, but it can also cause the model to memorize the training data.

Reducing max_depth constrains the model.

The trees become shallower.

The model is forced to learn broader patterns instead of fitting noise.

This improves generalization and reduces the risk of overfitting.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    High training accuracy: Identify overfitting.

    Poor unseen-data performance: Improve generalization.

    XGBoost model: Tune tree-specific hyperparameters.

    Model complexity control: Reduce max_depth.

    Noise sensitivity: Prevent the model from learning overly specific patterns.

    Better validation performance: Encourage simpler, more robust decision boundaries.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

XGBoost + high training accuracy + poor test/validation performance = overfitting; reduce max_depth.

Remember:

    max_depth: Controls tree depth and model complexity.

    High max_depth: Can increase overfitting risk.

    Lower max_depth: Encourages simpler, more generalizable trees.

    learning_rate: Controls step size, not tree complexity directly.

    Feature removal: Can help, but is less direct when the question asks about XGBoost hyperparameter tuning.

    Duplicating records: Not a valid overfitting fix and may introduce bias.

Official AWS references.

    Amazon SageMaker XGBoost hyperparameters.

Key exam terms: XGBoost, max_depth, overfitting, model complexity, hyperparameter tuning, Amazon SageMaker, generalization, learning_rate.

Question 35Skipped

A logistics and freight company, FreightPath Analytics, is building an automated pipeline to update its shipment anomaly detection ML model every week using Amazon SageMaker Pipelines. The pipeline will consist of the following steps: 


1. A data preprocessing step to clean and transform freight transactional data.

2. A model training step to build the shipment anomaly detection model.

3. An evaluation step to calculate accuracy and other metrics.

4. A model registration step to store the new model in the SageMaker Model Registry. 


The preprocessing step involves large-scale data transformations and joins across multiple datasets stored in Amazon S3. The data transformations currently run on a distributed Amazon EMR cluster. The data science team wants to integrate these transformations into the SageMaker Pipelines workflow seamlessly. 



Which options should be combined for a solution that addresses these requirements? (Select TWO.)


Set up an Amazon EMR job as the processing step of the SageMaker Pipelines workflow
Explanation
Incorrect. A processing step in SageMaker Pipelines is specifically designed to run SageMaker Processing Jobs using SageMaker-managed infrastructure and custom or prebuilt containers. It does not natively integrate with external compute environments such as Amazon EMR. To incorporate an existing EMR-based Spark workload into a SageMaker Pipelines workflow, a callback step is the appropriate mechanism, as it allows the pipeline to pause execution and delegate work to an external system. Keyword tip: When a scenario mentions integrating an existing EMR Spark workload into SageMaker Pipelines, 'processing step' is incorrect — look for 'callback step' paired with a task token pattern.
Swap out the SageMaker Pipeline with AWS Step Functions as the ML workflow orchestration service
Explanation
Incorrect. Replacing SageMaker Pipelines with AWS Step Functions provides no meaningful advantage for this ML use case and would require rebuilding the entire pipeline outside of the SageMaker-native ecosystem. SageMaker Pipelines offers tighter integration with SageMaker training jobs, evaluation steps, and the SageMaker Model Registry, making it the superior choice for end-to-end ML workflow orchestration when all downstream steps are SageMaker-centric. Keyword tip: Do not swap SageMaker Pipelines for AWS Step Functions when the workflow is primarily SageMaker-centric — the callback step pattern is the proper extension mechanism for external compute rather than a full orchestration layer replacement.
Set up an Amazon EMR job as the first step of the ML workflow orchestrated by AWS Step Functions
Explanation
Incorrect. Orchestrating the EMR job as the first step of an AWS Step Functions workflow moves the overall workflow coordination away from SageMaker Pipelines entirely, requiring a separate orchestration service and sacrificing the native integration SageMaker Pipelines provides for ML-specific steps such as training, evaluation, and model registration. This introduces unnecessary architectural complexity and operational overhead for a use case that SageMaker Pipelines can handle natively via its callback step construct. Keyword tip: Introducing AWS Step Functions as the primary orchestrator when SageMaker Pipelines is already in place adds redundancy without benefit — prefer the callback step pattern to keep orchestration within a single SageMaker-native layer.
Correct selection
Set up an Amazon EMR job as a callback step of the SageMaker Pipelines workflow
Explanation
Correct. A callback step in SageMaker Pipelines enables integration with any external compute resource or service outside of SageMaker. When the callback step is invoked, the pipeline execution pauses and sends a task token to an external system via Amazon SQS. The external job — such as a Spark job running on an Amazon EMR cluster — performs its work and then returns the task token to resume the pipeline, making this the recommended pattern for incorporating existing EMR-based transformations without replacing the orchestration layer. Keyword tip: 'callback step' + 'task token' + Amazon SQS = the correct construct for integrating external workloads such as Amazon EMR or AWS Glue ETL jobs within a SageMaker Pipelines workflow.
Correct selection
Add a policy to the SageMaker Pipelines execution role to allow the role to invoke an Amazon EMR job flow
Explanation
Correct. Attaching the AmazonSageMakerPipelinesIntegrations managed policy (or an equivalent custom policy) to the SageMaker Pipelines execution role grants the IAM permissions required to trigger external jobs — including invoking an Amazon EMR job flow — from within a pipeline callback step. Without these permissions, the callback step cannot interact with EMR or other external services, causing the pipeline execution to fail at the integration boundary. Keyword tip: Whenever a SageMaker Pipelines callback step or Lambda step is used to invoke an external AWS service, the pipeline execution role must hold explicit IAM permissions for that service — 'AmazonSageMakerPipelinesIntegrations' is the managed policy signal.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of Amazon SageMaker Pipelines extensibility patterns.

It focuses on integrating an external distributed compute workload, such as an Amazon EMR Spark job, into a native Amazon SageMaker Pipelines workflow without replacing the orchestration layer.

It is tied to the Deployment and Orchestration of ML Workflows domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Set up an Amazon EMR job as the processing step of the SageMaker Pipelines workflow: This option is incorrect. A SageMaker Processing step runs Amazon SageMaker Processing jobs on SageMaker-managed infrastructure. It is not the correct construct for directly running an external Amazon EMR Spark workload.

    Add a policy to the SageMaker Pipelines execution role to allow the role to invoke an Amazon EMR job flow: This is a correct option. The Amazon SageMaker Pipelines execution role needs permissions to invoke or interact with the external Amazon EMR job. This can be done with the AmazonSageMakerPipelinesIntegrations managed policy or an equivalent custom policy.

    Set up an Amazon EMR job as the first step of the ML workflow orchestrated by AWS Step Functions: This option is incorrect. AWS Step Functions can orchestrate workflows, but moving the orchestration out of Amazon SageMaker Pipelines adds unnecessary complexity when the rest of the workflow is SageMaker-centric.

    Set up an Amazon EMR job as a callback step of the SageMaker Pipelines workflow: This is a correct option. A SageMaker Pipelines callback step is designed to integrate external systems. It pauses the pipeline, sends a task token through Amazon SQS, and resumes the pipeline after the external job completes.

    Swap out the SageMaker Pipeline with AWS Step Functions as the ML workflow orchestration service: This option is incorrect. Replacing Amazon SageMaker Pipelines is unnecessary. The callback step pattern allows external compute integration while preserving SageMaker-native training, evaluation, and model registry steps.

Why the callback step and execution role policy fit this scenario.

The workflow is already centered on Amazon SageMaker Pipelines.

The preprocessing job runs externally on Amazon EMR.

The goal is to integrate that external Spark workload without abandoning the SageMaker-native pipeline.

That points to a SageMaker Pipelines callback step.

A callback step allows the pipeline to pause while an external system performs work.

The pipeline sends a task token to an external service through Amazon SQS.

The external workload, such as an Amazon EMR Spark job, runs independently.

When the external job completes, it returns the task token so the pipeline can continue to the next step.

The pipeline execution role also needs the correct IAM permissions.

Without permissions to invoke or interact with Amazon EMR, the integration fails even if the callback step is correctly configured.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    External compute integration: Use a SageMaker Pipelines callback step.

    EMR Spark preprocessing: Run the distributed workload on Amazon EMR.

    Pipeline continuity: Resume the pipeline after the external job completes.

    Task token pattern: Use Amazon SQS to coordinate the callback.

    Correct permissions: Grant the pipeline execution role access to invoke Amazon EMR.

    SageMaker-native orchestration: Keep training, evaluation, and registration inside Amazon SageMaker Pipelines.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

SageMaker Pipelines + external EMR/Spark workload + pause and resume pipeline = callback step with task token.

Remember:

    SageMaker Pipelines callback step: Integrates external workloads into a pipeline.

    Task token: Used to resume pipeline execution after external work completes.

    Amazon SQS: Delivers the callback task token to the external workflow.

    Amazon EMR: Runs distributed Spark preprocessing jobs.

    Pipeline execution role: Must have the required IAM permissions.

    AmazonSageMakerPipelinesIntegrations: Managed policy signal for external service integrations.

    AWS Step Functions: Useful for general orchestration, but unnecessary when SageMaker Pipelines can stay as the ML orchestration layer.

Question 36Skipped

A public transportation authority operates an Amazon SageMaker training job in a public subnet within an Amazon VPC.


The network is properly configured, allowing seamless data transfer between the SageMaker training job and Amazon S3. Recently, the authority identified malicious traffic originating from a specific IP address, targeting resources within the VPC.


The authority needs to block all traffic from the suspicious IP address while ensuring legitimate traffic remains unaffected. 


Which of the following would you recommend to address this requirement?


Enable AWS WAF (Web Application Firewall) for the SageMaker domain and create a rule to block traffic from the specific IP address
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS WAF is designed to protect web applications by filtering HTTP/HTTPS traffic at the application layer (Layer 7), and it integrates with services such as Amazon CloudFront, Application Load Balancer, Amazon API Gateway, and AWS AppSync. AWS WAF cannot be directly applied to a SageMaker training job or to VPC subnet-level traffic, and it does not provide a mechanism for blocking IP-based traffic at the network layer for SageMaker workloads. Applying AWS WAF to a SageMaker training job is not a supported configuration. Keyword tip: AWS WAF protects web application endpoints (CloudFront, ALB, API Gateway) at Layer 7 — when the scenario involves VPC networking or SageMaker training jobs, AWS WAF is not the correct traffic-blocking tool.
Modify the VPC route table to direct all traffic from the specific IP address to a target with a blackhole routing state
Explanation
Incorrect. VPC route tables control the routing of traffic based on destination CIDR blocks, directing packets to targets such as internet gateways, virtual private gateways, or NAT gateways. They do not provide a mechanism to selectively filter or block traffic based on a specific source IP address. A 'blackhole' routing state in AWS indicates that a route target is unavailable (for example, a gateway that is not attached to the VPC or a terminated NAT instance), and is not an intentional or supported security control mechanism. Route tables operate exclusively on destination-based routing, not source IP filtering. Keyword tip: VPC route table blackhole entries reflect unavailable routing targets, not deliberate IP blocking — in VPC security scenarios on the MLA-C01 exam, route table modifications are a common distractor when source IP filtering is required.
Update the security group associated with the SageMaker training job to include a deny rule for traffic from the specific IP address
Explanation
Incorrect. Security groups are stateful firewalls that operate at the instance level and support only allow rules — they do not support explicit deny rules. It is therefore not possible to create a deny rule for a specific IP address using a security group. To block traffic from a specific source IP address within a VPC, a network ACL (NACL) must be used instead, as NACLs support both allow and deny rules at the subnet level. Keyword tip: whenever an exam option pairs 'deny rule' with 'security group,' recognize it as a distractor — security groups are stateful and allow-only, making them incapable of explicitly blocking a source IP.
Correct answer
Create a network ACL (NACL) for the subnet hosting the SageMaker training job and add a deny rule to block traffic from the specific IP address
Explanation
Correct. Network ACLs (NACLs) are stateless, subnet-level firewalls that support explicit allow and deny rules for both inbound and outbound traffic. By creating a NACL for the subnet hosting the SageMaker training job and adding an inbound deny rule for the suspicious IP address, all traffic from that IP is blocked before it reaches any resource in the subnet, while legitimate traffic continues to flow normally. This is the appropriate AWS mechanism for blocking specific source IP addresses at the network layer within a VPC. Keyword tip: when a scenario requires blocking a specific source IP address at the subnet level within a VPC — particularly for SageMaker workloads — NACLs with explicit deny rules are the correct and only AWS tool that supports this capability.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of VPC network security controls for protecting Amazon SageMaker workloads.

It focuses on distinguishing security groups, network ACLs (NACLs), AWS WAF, and VPC route tables when blocking traffic from a specific source IP address.

It is tied to the ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Update the security group associated with the SageMaker training job to include a deny rule for traffic from the specific IP address: This option is incorrect. Security groups are stateful firewalls that support only allow rules. They do not support explicit deny rules. You cannot use a security group to explicitly block one source IP address.

    Enable AWS WAF for the SageMaker domain and create a rule to block traffic from the specific IP address: This option is incorrect. AWS WAF protects supported Layer 7 web entry points such as Amazon CloudFront, Application Load Balancer, Amazon API Gateway, and AWS AppSync. It cannot be directly attached to a SageMaker training job or used for subnet-level VPC traffic filtering.

    Create a network ACL (NACL) for the subnet hosting the SageMaker training job and add a deny rule to block traffic from the specific IP address: This is the correct option. Network ACLs (NACLs) are stateless subnet-level firewalls that support both allow and deny rules. A NACL can explicitly deny inbound traffic from a suspicious source IP before it reaches resources in the subnet.

    Modify the VPC route table to direct all traffic from the specific IP address to a target with a blackhole routing state: This option is incorrect. VPC route tables route traffic based on destination CIDR blocks, not source IP addresses. A blackhole route usually means the configured target is unavailable, not that traffic is intentionally blocked as a security control.

Why a network ACL fits this scenario.

The requirement is to block traffic from one specific source IP address.

The workload runs inside an Amazon VPC.

The traffic must be blocked before it reaches the subnet resources.

That points to a network ACL.

A network ACL operates at the subnet level.

It is stateless.

It supports explicit allow and deny rules for inbound and outbound traffic.

This makes it the correct AWS-native control when the requirement is to deny traffic from a specific source IP address.

A security group cannot satisfy this requirement because it supports only allow rules.

AWS WAF cannot satisfy this requirement because it protects supported web application entry points, not general subnet-level traffic for SageMaker training jobs.

A VPC route table cannot satisfy this requirement because route tables perform destination-based routing, not source-based filtering.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Block a specific source IP: Use a network ACL deny rule.

    Subnet-level protection: Apply the NACL to the subnet hosting the workload.

    Preserve legitimate traffic: Add a targeted deny rule for the suspicious IP only.

    Avoid security group misuse: Do not use security groups for explicit deny rules.

    Avoid Layer 7 mismatch: Do not use AWS WAF for VPC subnet traffic filtering.

    Avoid route table misuse: Do not use VPC route tables for source IP blocking.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Block a specific source IP inside a VPC = network ACL deny rule.

Remember:

    Security group: Stateful, instance or ENI level, allow rules only.

    Network ACL: Stateless, subnet level, allow and deny rules.

    AWS WAF: Layer 7 web application filtering for supported services.

    VPC route table: Destination-based routing, not source IP filtering.

    Blackhole route: Indicates an unavailable route target, not an intentional IP-blocking control.

    SageMaker VPC networking: Use VPC-native controls such as security groups and network ACLs appropriately.

Official AWS references.

    Network ACLs for your VPC.

    Amazon EC2 Route API.

Key exam terms: network ACL, NACL deny rule, security group allow-only, stateless subnet firewall, VPC traffic blocking, source IP filtering, SageMaker VPC networking, blackhole route distractor.
