Question 37Skipped

A data science team at OceanRoute Logistics, a maritime shipping company, is evaluating foundational machine learning algorithms for two separate projects: one involving the automatic grouping of unlabeled vessel voyage patterns, and another involving the classification of shipment delay risk based on historical labeled records.


How would you differentiate between K-Means and K-Nearest Neighbors (KNN) algorithms in the context of these use cases?


K-Means is primarily used for regression tasks, while KNN is used for reducing the dimensionality of data
Explanation
Incorrect. K-Means is not a regression algorithm, and KNN is not a dimensionality reduction technique. K-Means partitions unlabeled data into clusters by iteratively minimizing intra-cluster variance, while KNN is a distance-based algorithm used for classification and regression on labeled datasets. Techniques such as Principal Component Analysis (PCA) — not KNN — are used for dimensionality reduction, and Amazon SageMaker provides a built-in PCA algorithm distinct from both K-Means and KNN. Keyword tip: When you see 'regression' or 'dimensionality reduction' attributed to K-Means or KNN respectively, treat these as classic distractor pairings on the MLA-C01 exam.
K-Means requires labeled data to form clusters, whereas KNN does not use labeled data for making predictions
Explanation
Incorrect. K-Means does not require labeled data — it is an unsupervised algorithm that discovers inherent structure in unlabeled datasets by minimizing intra-cluster distances. KNN is a supervised algorithm that explicitly relies on labeled training examples to assign a class to a new instance based on the majority label among its k nearest neighbors. This option incorrectly inverts the label requirements of both algorithms. Keyword tip: Any option claiming K-Means requires labeled data is incorrect; labeled ground-truth data is the exclusive prerequisite of supervised algorithms such as KNN.
Correct answer
K-Means is an unsupervised learning algorithm used for clustering data points into groups, while KNN is a supervised learning algorithm used for classifying data points based on their proximity to labeled examples
Explanation
Correct. K-Means is an unsupervised clustering algorithm that partitions a dataset into k distinct groups by iteratively minimizing the within-cluster sum of squared distances, requiring no labeled data — making it ideal for discovering natural groupings in unlabeled vessel voyage patterns. KNN is a supervised, instance-based learning algorithm that classifies new data points by majority vote among the k nearest labeled neighbors in the training set, making it well suited for predicting shipment delay risk from historical labeled records. Amazon SageMaker provides built-in implementations of both: the K-Means algorithm for clustering tasks and the K-Nearest Neighbors algorithm for classification and regression tasks. Keyword tip: 'Unsupervised + no labels + grouping = K-Means' and 'Supervised + labeled examples + proximity-based classification = KNN' is the core distinction tested in MLA-C01.
K-Means is a supervised learning algorithm used for classification, while KNN is an unsupervised learning algorithm used for clustering
Explanation
Incorrect. This option reverses the learning paradigms of both algorithms. K-Means is an unsupervised algorithm that requires no labels, discovering structure purely from the input feature space. KNN is a supervised algorithm that relies entirely on labeled training examples to make predictions via majority vote among nearest neighbors. Keyword tip: A deliberate reversal of the supervised/unsupervised labels between K-Means and KNN is a recurring distractor pattern in MLA-C01 — always verify which algorithm operates without ground-truth labels.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests foundational knowledge of unsupervised versus supervised machine learning.

It focuses on distinguishing K-Means as an unsupervised clustering algorithm from KNN as a supervised algorithm used for classification or regression based on labeled examples.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    K-Means is primarily used for regression tasks, while KNN is used for reducing the dimensionality of data: This option is incorrect. K-Means is not a regression algorithm. It is used for clustering. KNN is not a dimensionality reduction technique. Algorithms such as PCA are more appropriate for dimensionality reduction.

    K-Means is a supervised learning algorithm used for classification, while KNN is an unsupervised learning algorithm used for clustering: This option is incorrect. It reverses the learning paradigms. K-Means is unsupervised and does not require labels. KNN is supervised and relies on labeled examples.

    K-Means is an unsupervised learning algorithm used for clustering data points into groups, while KNN is a supervised learning algorithm used for classifying data points based on their proximity to labeled examples: This is the correct option. K-Means discovers natural groupings in unlabeled data. KNN classifies new data points by comparing them with nearby labeled examples.

    K-Means requires labeled data to form clusters, whereas KNN does not use labeled data for making predictions: This option is incorrect. K-Means does not require labeled data. KNN depends on labeled training data to make predictions.

Why K-Means and KNN fit this scenario.

The question asks for the correct distinction between K-Means and KNN.

K-Means is an unsupervised learning algorithm.

It groups similar data points into clusters without using labels.

This makes it useful when the goal is to discover patterns or natural segments in data.

For example, it can group vessel voyage behaviors, customer profiles, or sensor patterns when no target label exists.

KNN, or K-Nearest Neighbors, is a supervised learning algorithm.

It requires labeled training examples.

When a new data point arrives, KNN looks at the closest labeled examples and predicts based on their labels.

This makes it suitable for classification tasks, such as predicting shipment delay risk using historical labeled records.

Amazon SageMaker provides built-in implementations for both algorithms.

Amazon SageMaker K-Means is used for clustering.

Amazon SageMaker K-Nearest Neighbors can be used for classification and regression.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Unlabeled data: Use K-Means for clustering.

    Labeled data: Use KNN for supervised prediction.

    Pattern discovery: Use unsupervised learning.

    Classification: Use supervised learning.

    Distance-based logic: Both algorithms use distance concepts, but for different purposes.

    Correct learning paradigm: Do not confuse clustering with classification.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Unsupervised + unlabeled data + grouping = K-Means.

Supervised + labeled examples + proximity-based classification = KNN.

Remember:

    K-Means: Unsupervised clustering.

    KNN: Supervised classification or regression.

    Clustering: Finds groups without labels.

    Classification: Predicts labels from labeled training data.

    Labeled data: Required for supervised learning.

    Unlabeled data: Common signal for unsupervised learning.

    PCA: Dimensionality reduction, not KNN.

Official AWS references.

    K-means clustering with Amazon SageMaker.

    Amazon SageMaker K-Nearest Neighbors algorithm.

Key exam terms: K-Means, K-Nearest Neighbors, unsupervised learning, supervised learning, clustering, classification, labeled data, Amazon SageMaker built-in algorithms.

Question 38Skipped

A consumer electronics manufacturer, NovaTech Devices, is building a recommendation system to provide personalized product suggestions to its customers browsing an online storefront.


The company has collected historical user interaction data — including product views, purchases, and ratings across thousands of SKUs. An ML engineer needs to select the most suitable built-in Amazon SageMaker algorithm to train the recommendation model efficiently.


The engineer wants a solution that minimizes development effort while providing high-quality, personalized recommendations from sparse, high-dimensional user-item interaction data. 


Which built-in SageMaker algorithm should the ML engineer use to meet these requirements?
Correct answer
Factorization Machines
Explanation
Correct. Factorization Machines is the appropriate built-in Amazon SageMaker algorithm for building recommendation systems from user-item interaction data. It is a general-purpose supervised learning algorithm that extends linear models by efficiently learning pairwise feature interactions through latent factor decomposition — exactly the capability needed for high-dimensional, sparse datasets such as product view, purchase, and rating logs. Factorization Machines are purpose-built for click prediction and item recommendation tasks, delivering high-quality results with minimal custom development effort. Keyword tip: whenever a scenario mentions 'recommendation system,' 'sparse high-dimensional data,' 'user-item interactions,' 'collaborative filtering,' or 'pairwise feature interactions' in the context of SageMaker built-in algorithms, Factorization Machines is the correct choice.
K-Means Clustering
Explanation
Incorrect. K-Means is an unsupervised clustering algorithm that groups data points into k clusters based on feature similarity, making it useful for customer segmentation or product grouping. While K-Means could segment users or products into cohorts, it does not model personalized user-item interaction patterns, learn latent representations, or generate ranked recommendations from historical interaction data. Keyword tip: K-Means is appropriate for customer segmentation, anomaly detection, or data exploration tasks — not for building personalized recommendation systems from user-item interaction logs.
XGBoost (eXtreme Gradient Boosting)
Explanation
Incorrect. XGBoost is a highly effective gradient boosted trees algorithm well suited for structured, tabular data classification and regression tasks. However, it is not specifically designed to handle the high-dimensional, sparse user-item interaction matrices that characterize recommendation systems, and it lacks a built-in mechanism to factorize latent feature interactions between users and items efficiently. Applying XGBoost here would require significant feature engineering and would not natively exploit the collaborative filtering signal in the interaction data. Keyword tip: associate XGBoost with structured or tabular ML tasks such as fraud detection or churn prediction — not with collaborative filtering, sparse matrices, or personalized item recommendation.
Linear Learner
Explanation
Incorrect. The Amazon SageMaker Linear Learner algorithm is designed for binary and multiclass classification as well as regression tasks, optimizing objectives such as mean squared error, cross-entropy loss, F1 score, precision, and recall. It models linear relationships between input features and targets and does not capture the complex pairwise feature interactions — such as user-product affinity — that are essential to collaborative filtering-based recommendation systems. Keyword tip: when you see 'binary classification,' 'regression,' or 'linear relationship' as the core task, consider Linear Learner — not recommendation or user-item interaction use cases.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests the ability to select the correct Amazon SageMaker built-in algorithm for a recommendation use case.

It focuses on choosing Factorization Machines when the dataset contains sparse, high-dimensional user-item interaction data.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Linear Learner: This option is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Linear Learner is used for linear classification and regression tasks. It does not natively model the pairwise user-item interactions required for personalized recommendation systems.

    XGBoost: This option is incorrect. XGBoost is strong for structured tabular classification and regression problems, but it is not purpose-built for sparse user-item recommendation matrices. It would require significant feature engineering to approximate collaborative filtering behavior.

    K-Means Clustering: This option is incorrect. K-Means is an unsupervised clustering algorithm. It can group users or products into clusters, but it does not learn personalized user-item affinity or generate ranked recommendations from interaction history.

    Factorization Machines: This is the correct option. Factorization Machines are designed to model pairwise feature interactions in sparse, high-dimensional datasets. This makes them well suited for recommendation systems, click prediction, and user-item interaction modeling.

Why Factorization Machines fit this scenario.

The scenario involves personalized recommendations.

The data likely contains user-item interactions, such as views, clicks, purchases, ratings, or engagement events.

This type of data is often sparse and high dimensional.

That points directly to Factorization Machines.

Factorization Machines extend linear models by learning latent factors for features.

These latent factors allow the model to estimate pairwise interactions efficiently.

In a recommendation system, this means the model can learn patterns such as which users are likely to interact with which items.

This is the foundation of collaborative filtering-style recommendation.

Linear Learner does not capture these interactions natively.

XGBoost is strong for tabular data, but it is not specialized for sparse recommendation matrices.

K-Means clusters similar records but does not produce personalized ranked recommendations.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Recommendation system: Use Factorization Machines.

    Sparse data: Handle high-dimensional user-item matrices efficiently.

    User-item interactions: Learn relationships between users and products.

    Pairwise feature interactions: Model second-order interactions.

    SageMaker built-in algorithm: Use the managed Amazon SageMaker implementation.

    Avoid task mismatch: Do not choose clustering, linear regression, or general tabular boosting for this recommendation-specific pattern.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Recommendation system + sparse high-dimensional data + user-item interactions = Factorization Machines.

Remember:

    Factorization Machines: Recommendation systems, click prediction, and sparse feature interactions.

    User-item matrix: Common structure for recommendation problems.

    Collaborative filtering: Learns patterns from user behavior and item interactions.

    Linear Learner: Classification and regression with linear relationships.

    XGBoost: Tabular classification and regression.

    K-Means: Clustering and segmentation, not personalized recommendation.

Official AWS references.

    Amazon SageMaker Factorization Machines algorithm.

    How Factorization Machines work in Amazon SageMaker.

    K-means clustering with Amazon SageMaker.

Question 39Skipped

A petrochemical company, PetroStream Analytics, collects real-time streaming logs of pipeline sensor readings and operational events, storing them in an Amazon S3 bucket.

The raw logs contain millions of records daily, including timestamps, sensor IDs, and equipment metadata.


The data analytics team uses Amazon Athena to generate weekly reports and analyze trends in sensor activity over the past 7 days. The company needs to store the data for 90 days before archiving it to lower-cost storage. The solution must optimize Athena query performance while maintaining cost efficiency. 


What approach do you recommend?


Use an S3 lifecycle policy to delete logs older than 90 days and configure Athena to query the raw logs directly from the S3 bucket without using partitions or prefixes.
Explanation
Incorrect. This option violates the stated requirement to archive — not permanently delete — the data after 90 days. An S3 lifecycle policy configured with an Expiration action permanently removes the objects, resulting in data loss that is incompatible with the archival objective. Additionally, querying raw, unpartitioned logs forces full table scans, degrading Athena performance. Keyword tip: carefully distinguish between S3 lifecycle Transition actions (moving objects to S3 Glacier or other storage tiers) and Expiration actions (permanent deletion) whenever an exam scenario explicitly requires archival rather than deletion.
Store all logs in uncompressed JSON format and rely on Athena filters to query the data for the 7-day trend analysis.
Explanation
Incorrect. Storing data in uncompressed JSON format without any partitioning forces Athena to perform full table scans on every query, even when only 7 days of data are needed out of 90 days stored. This approach maximizes both storage costs and query costs, directly violating the cost-efficiency requirement. The correct approach is to partition the data by date so that Athena's partition pruning can skip irrelevant data entirely. Keyword tip: 'uncompressed JSON with no partitioning' is a classic cost and performance anti-pattern in Athena scenarios — recognize it as an incorrect choice whenever query selectivity and cost efficiency are stated requirements.
Correct answer
Partition the data in the S3 bucket by year, month, and day (e.g., year=YYYY/month=MM/day=DD). Use prefixes for organizing logs in daily folders and configure an S3 lifecycle policy to transition data to Amazon S3 Glacier after 90 days.
Explanation
Correct. Partitioning the data by year, month, and day enables Amazon Athena partition pruning, which restricts data scans to only the relevant date-range partitions required for the 7-day trend analysis. This significantly reduces the volume of data scanned per query, directly lowering both query execution time and per-terabyte cost. Configuring an S3 lifecycle policy with a Transition action to Amazon S3 Glacier after 90 days satisfies the archival requirement without permanently deleting data. Keyword tip: when a scenario combines Athena performance on date-range queries with a long-term archival requirement, the canonical solution is date-based S3 partitioning paired with an S3 lifecycle Transition policy to Amazon S3 Glacier.
Compress all daily logs into a single large file and store them in the S3 bucket. Use Athena to scan the compressed files for the required 7-day trend analysis.
Explanation
Incorrect. Although compression reduces storage costs and the volume of data Athena must read, consolidating all daily logs into a single large compressed file prevents Athena from leveraging partition pruning. Because the entire 90-day dataset resides in one file, Athena must decompress and scan the complete file for every query regardless of the date range requested, negating any performance benefit. Partitioning and compression are complementary, not interchangeable. Keyword tip: when a question requires selective date-range queries, compression alone is insufficient — partitioning is the mechanism that enables Athena to skip irrelevant data; a single large compressed file defeats this optimization entirely.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of Amazon Athena query optimization for time-series log data stored in Amazon S3.

It focuses on using date-based partitioning to reduce scanned data and using an Amazon S3 lifecycle Transition policy to archive older logs without deleting them.

It is tied to the Data Preparation for Machine Learning (ML) domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Partition the data in the S3 bucket by year, month, and day. Use prefixes for organizing logs in daily folders and configure an S3 lifecycle policy to transition data to Amazon S3 Glacier after 90 days: This is the correct option. Date-based partitioning enables Amazon Athena partition pruning, so queries scan only the relevant date ranges. An Amazon S3 lifecycle Transition policy moves older data to Amazon S3 Glacier for lower-cost archival without deleting it.

    Store all logs in uncompressed JSON format and rely on Athena filters to query the data for the 7-day trend analysis: This option is incorrect. Uncompressed JSON without partitioning forces Amazon Athena to scan much more data than necessary. Filters alone do not prevent full scans if the data is not partitioned effectively.

    Use an S3 lifecycle policy to delete logs older than 90 days and configure Athena to query the raw logs directly from the S3 bucket without using partitions or prefixes: This option is incorrect. Deleting logs violates the archival requirement. Also, querying raw unpartitioned logs from Amazon S3 increases scan volume, latency, and cost.

    Compress all daily logs into a single large file and store them in the S3 bucket. Use Athena to scan the compressed files for the required 7-day trend analysis: This option is incorrect. Compression can reduce storage and scan size, but one large compressed file prevents Amazon Athena from skipping irrelevant dates. Partitioning is required for selective date-range queries.

Why date-based partitioning with S3 lifecycle transition fits this scenario.

The logs are stored in Amazon S3.

The queries focus on recent date ranges, such as 7-day trend analysis.

The data must also be archived after 90 days.

That points to date-based partitioning plus lifecycle transition.

By organizing data with prefixes such as:

year=YYYY/month=MM/day=DD

Amazon Athena can use partition pruning.

Partition pruning allows Athena to scan only the partitions that match the query date range.

This reduces the amount of data scanned.

It also lowers query cost because Athena charges based on scanned data volume.

For archival, the correct lifecycle action is Transition, not Expiration.

An Amazon S3 lifecycle Transition policy can move older objects to Amazon S3 Glacier after 90 days.

This preserves the data at lower storage cost.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Date-range query optimization: Partition data by year, month, and day.

    Lower Athena scan cost: Use partition pruning.

    Faster queries: Avoid scanning irrelevant log data.

    Archival after 90 days: Use an Amazon S3 lifecycle Transition action.

    No data loss: Do not use Expiration when the requirement is archival.

    S3 organization: Use meaningful prefixes for time-series logs.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Athena date-range queries + S3 logs + reduce scanned data = date-based partitioning.

Archive after 90 days without deletion = Amazon S3 lifecycle Transition to Amazon S3 Glacier.

Remember:

    Amazon Athena: Queries data in Amazon S3.

    Partition pruning: Skips irrelevant partitions during query execution.

    Date-based partitioning: Common pattern for logs and time-series data.

    AWS Glue Data Catalog: Stores table and partition metadata for Athena.

    Compression: Helpful, but not a substitute for partitioning.

    Lifecycle Transition: Moves data to cheaper storage classes.

    Lifecycle Expiration: Deletes data and is wrong when archival is required.

Question 40Skipped

You are a data engineer responsible for monitoring the performance of a suite of machine learning models deployed across multiple environments at AgroVantage Technologies, an agricultural technology company.

The models are used for various tasks, including crop yield forecasting, precision irrigation recommendations, and soil health segmentation. 


To ensure that these models are performing optimally, you need to set up a centralized dashboard that allows stakeholders — including field agronomists, operations managers, and data scientists — to monitor key performance metrics such as latency, accuracy, throughput, and resource utilization.

The dashboard should be user-friendly, provide insights at a glance, and support both technical and non-technical users.   


Which approach is MOST SUITABLE for setting up a dashboard to monitor the performance metrics of these ML models?


Correct answer
Use Amazon QuickSight to create a visual dashboard that integrates data from Amazon CloudWatch Logs via Amazon S3, providing interactive charts and graphs that allow stakeholders to drill down into specific metrics as needed.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon QuickSight is a fully managed, serverless business intelligence service that enables the creation of interactive, visually rich dashboards accessible to both technical and non-technical users without requiring custom application development. By routing CloudWatch Logs data through a subscription filter to Amazon Data Firehose and ultimately into Amazon S3, QuickSight can query and visualize key ML model performance metrics — such as latency, accuracy, throughput, and resource utilization — with drill-down interactivity suited for diverse stakeholder audiences at AgroVantage Technologies. This architecture satisfies the requirements for centralization, real-time accessibility, and ease of use while leveraging fully managed AWS services that minimize operational overhead. Keyword tip: when a scenario requires a centralized, interactive monitoring dashboard accessible to mixed technical and non-technical audiences with data sourced from Amazon CloudWatch Logs, the canonical AWS-recommended pattern is Amazon QuickSight integrated with Amazon S3 via a CloudWatch Logs subscription filter and Amazon Data Firehose.
Set up a CloudWatch Dashboard to aggregate and display performance metrics such as CPU utilization, memory usage, and latency from various services. Use Amazon SNS to send daily reports based on these metrics to stakeholders.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon CloudWatch Dashboards are well-suited for aggregating and displaying infrastructure metrics such as CPU utilization, memory usage, and request latency directly from AWS services in near real-time. However, CloudWatch Dashboards are generally designed for technical operators rather than non-technical stakeholders such as agronomists and operations managers, and they lack the advanced interactive visualization, drill-down capabilities, and self-service analytics required for business-level ML model performance analysis. Supplementing with Amazon SNS daily reports further reduces the self-service, on-demand nature demanded by the scenario. Keyword tip: CloudWatch Dashboards are the right tool for operational infrastructure monitoring by engineers; when the audience includes non-technical business users requiring rich, interactive BI-style dashboards, Amazon QuickSight is the preferred AWS service.
Create a custom web application that pulls data from CloudWatch Logs, generating real-time visualizations of performance metrics. Embed the application within AgroVantage Technologies' internal portal for easy access.
Explanation
Incorrect. Building a custom web application offers flexibility and real-time capabilities, but it introduces significant development effort, ongoing maintenance overhead, and operational complexity compared to managed business intelligence services. The question specifically asks for the MOST SUITABLE approach, and a purpose-built managed service such as Amazon QuickSight delivers equivalent or superior interactive visualization capabilities with far less engineering investment and no custom infrastructure to maintain. Keyword tip: 'custom web application' as a monitoring solution is typically a distractor on MLA-C01 when managed BI tools like Amazon QuickSight can fulfill the same stakeholder requirements with lower total cost of ownership and faster time to value.
Use AWS Lambda to query CloudWatch Logs and send the results to an S3 bucket daily, where stakeholders can manually review the metrics and create their own visualizations using spreadsheet software.
Explanation
Incorrect. Although using AWS Lambda to collect CloudWatch Logs data and store the results in Amazon S3 is technically feasible, this approach produces a manual, batch-oriented process with no real-time interactivity. Requiring stakeholders — including non-technical field agronomists and operations managers at AgroVantage Technologies — to download data and build their own visualizations in spreadsheet software is neither scalable nor user-friendly, and it fails to meet the requirement for an accessible, centralized dashboard. This pattern also introduces a 24-hour lag that undermines timely decision-making for time-sensitive agricultural operations. Keyword tip: when a scenario requires a centralized, real-time monitoring dashboard for mixed audiences and you see 'manual review' or 'spreadsheet software' as the visualization mechanism, recognize this as a scalability and usability anti-pattern.
Overall explanation

Question 41Skipped

A healthcare diagnostics provider, MediVision Analytics, uses a machine learning model to predict patient diagnostic demand across its clinic network.


The model vendor supplies cleaned and prepared training data every 3–4 days, which is uploaded to an Amazon S3 bucket. MediVision Analytics has set up an Amazon SageMaker pipeline to retrain the model whenever new data becomes available.


Until now, triggering the SageMaker pipeline has been a manual task. An ML engineer is tasked with implementing an automated solution to trigger the pipeline automatically whenever new data is uploaded to the S3 bucket. 


What solution should the ML engineer implement to achieve this with the LEAST operational effort?
Create an S3 Lifecycle configuration for the S3 bucket with an event pattern that matches the S3 upload event. Create a rule to trigger the SageMaker pipeline.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon S3 Lifecycle configurations are designed exclusively for object lifecycle management: transitioning objects to lower-cost storage classes (e.g., S3 Glacier) or expiring objects after a defined period. S3 Lifecycle policies do not support event-driven patterns for object uploads, nor can they trigger SageMaker Pipelines or any compute service. Keyword tip: S3 Lifecycle = storage cost optimization and object expiration — never used for event-driven pipeline triggering or compute invocation.
Set up a cron job to check for S3 data uploads once a day. If an S3 data file is detected, the cron job will invoke an AWS Lambda function, which will then trigger the SageMaker pipeline.
Explanation
Incorrect. Configuring a cron job paired with a custom AWS Lambda function introduces significant operational overhead: the cron infrastructure must be provisioned and maintained, the Lambda code must be written and tested, and the polling-based approach can introduce up to a 24-hour delay — which is fundamentally incompatible with near-real-time, event-driven automation. This approach also violates the 'least operational effort' requirement by adding multiple layers of custom infrastructure. Keyword tip: Polling-based cron jobs are high-effort distractors on MLA-C01; prefer fully managed, event-driven AWS integrations whenever the question specifies minimal operational overhead.
Correct answer
Enable Amazon EventBridge for the S3 bucket and create an EventBridge rule with an event pattern that matches the S3 upload event. Set the SageMaker pipeline as the target for the rule.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon EventBridge natively supports Amazon SageMaker Pipelines as a target. By enabling EventBridge on the S3 bucket, all S3 events — including object uploads — are automatically forwarded to EventBridge. An EventBridge rule can then match the specific S3 object-created event pattern and directly invoke the SageMaker pipeline execution, requiring no custom code and minimal configuration. This is the lowest-operational-effort, fully managed, event-driven integration available on AWS for this use case. Keyword tip: SageMaker Pipelines + automated trigger + S3 upload → EventBridge rule with SageMaker Pipeline as target is the canonical MLA-C01 pattern for least-effort pipeline automation.
Using Amazon S3 Event Notifications, add a notification configuration that identifies the event you want Amazon S3 to publish. Add the SageMaker pipeline as the destination to this notification configuration.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon S3 Event Notifications can route events to Amazon SNS topics, Amazon SQS queues, AWS Lambda functions, and Amazon EventBridge — but NOT directly to an Amazon SageMaker pipeline. Because SageMaker Pipelines is not a supported direct destination for S3 Event Notifications, this approach cannot fulfill the requirement without an intermediary service such as EventBridge. Keyword tip: When a question asks about direct S3 notification destinations, remember the supported list: SNS, SQS, Lambda, and EventBridge — not SageMaker Pipelines directly.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of event-driven automation for Amazon SageMaker Pipelines.

It focuses on triggering a pipeline automatically when new data lands in Amazon S3, using a fully managed, low-operational-overhead architecture.

It is tied to the Deployment and Orchestration of ML Workflows domain.

Set the SageMaker pipeline as the target for the rule: This is the correct option. Amazon EventBridge can receive Amazon S3 object-created events and invoke Amazon SageMaker Pipelines as a target. This creates a managed event-driven trigger without custom polling or glue code.

    Create an S3 Lifecycle configuration for the S3 bucket with an event pattern that matches the S3 upload event. Create a rule to trigger the SageMaker pipeline: This option is incorrect. Amazon S3 Lifecycle is used for object expiration and storage class transitions. It does not trigger compute workflows or Amazon SageMaker Pipelines.

    Set up a cron job to check for S3 data uploads once a day. If an S3 data file is detected, the cron job will invoke an AWS Lambda function, which will then trigger the SageMaker pipeline: This option is incorrect. A cron-based polling workflow adds operational overhead and delay. The scenario asks for low-overhead event-driven automation, so Amazon EventBridge is the better fit.

Why EventBridge with SageMaker Pipelines fits this scenario.

The solution should require minimal operational effort.

That points directly to Amazon EventBridge.

The rule can then invoke an Amazon SageMaker Pipeline as the target.

This avoids polling.

It avoids cron jobs.

It avoids custom trigger infrastructure.

It also keeps the workflow fully managed and event-driven.

This satisfies the key requirements:

Question 42Skipped

A data science team at a construction and engineering firm, BuildCore Analytics, is developing a machine learning model to predict structural equipment failure on active job sites.

The dataset contains hundreds of sensor-derived features collected from heavy machinery, but the team suspects that not all of these features contribute equally to the model's accuracy.

To improve model performance and reduce complexity, the team wants to focus on identifying only the most relevant features that contribute significantly to minimizing the model's error rate. 


Which feature engineering process should the team apply to select a subset of features that are the most relevant toward minimizing the error rate of the trained model? 



Feature extraction
Explanation
Incorrect. Feature extraction refers to reducing the dimensionality of the input data by projecting the original features into a new, lower-dimensional space using techniques such as Principal Component Analysis (PCA), Independent Component Analysis (ICA), and Linear Discriminant Analysis (LDA). Unlike feature selection, these methods produce transformed representations that are combinations of the original features and do not directly rank or retain original features based on their individual contribution to minimizing error. Keyword tip: when you see 'PCA', 'ICA', 'LDA', or 'projection into a lower-dimensional space', think feature extraction rather than feature selection.
Feature creation
Explanation
Incorrect. Feature creation involves generating entirely new features from existing data to improve predictive power. Common techniques include one-hot encoding of categorical variables, numeric binning, string splitting, and computing derived or aggregated fields from raw inputs. This process expands the feature space by adding new attributes rather than selecting the most relevant features from an existing set. Keyword tip: when you see 'one-hot encoding', 'derived fields', 'aggregated attributes', or 'new feature generation from raw inputs', think feature creation rather than feature selection.
Feature transformation
Explanation
Incorrect. Feature transformation involves replacing missing or invalid feature values and applying mathematical operations to existing features — such as forming Cartesian products, applying non-linear transformations (e.g., binning numeric variables into categories), and creating domain-specific representations. It modifies or imputes features rather than identifying and retaining only the most predictive subset from the original feature space. Keyword tip: when you see 'imputation', 'binning', 'non-linear transformation', or 'standardization of existing features', think feature transformation rather than feature selection.
Correct answer
Feature selection
Explanation
Correct. Feature selection is the process of identifying and retaining only the subset of original features that are most relevant and that contribute most to minimizing the error rate of a trained model. Techniques such as feature importance scores derived from tree-based models, correlation matrices, and wrapper or filter methods are commonly used to rank and filter features. Applying feature selection reduces model complexity, mitigates the risk of overfitting, and can improve both training efficiency and inference latency — all critical considerations when deploying ML models at scale on AWS using services like Amazon SageMaker. Keyword tip: when you see 'subset of original features', 'minimize error rate', 'feature importance scores', or 'reduce model complexity by filtering features', think feature selection.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests the ability to distinguish the main processes used during feature engineering for machine learning.

It focuses on identifying feature selection when the goal is to choose a subset of the original features that most contributes to reducing model error.

It is tied to the Data Preparation for Machine Learning (ML) domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Feature transformation: Feature transformation modifies existing feature values. Examples include imputing missing values, standardizing numerical columns, normalizing values, applying mathematical transformations, or binning continuous variables. It changes feature representation, but it does not specifically select the most useful original features.

    Feature extraction: Feature extraction creates a new lower-dimensional representation from the original features. Techniques such as PCA, ICA, and LDA combine or project the original variables into a new feature space. This is different from feature selection because the original features are transformed into new derived components.

    Feature creation: Feature creation generates new features from raw or existing data. Examples include creating derived fields, splitting strings, extracting date parts, creating aggregated attributes, or encoding categorical values. It expands or changes the feature set, but the main goal is not to retain only the most predictive original features.

    Feature selection: This is the correct option. Feature selection identifies and keeps only the most relevant original features for the model. It helps reduce model complexity, remove noisy or redundant variables, improve training efficiency, and reduce overfitting risk.

Why feature selection fits this scenario.

The scenario states that the goal is to select a subset of original features that minimizes the error rate.

That language points directly to feature selection.

Feature selection keeps the original features but filters them based on usefulness.

The model training process then uses only the most relevant columns instead of the full feature set.

Common approaches include feature importance scores, correlation analysis, filter methods, wrapper methods, and embedded methods.

This is different from feature extraction.

Feature extraction creates new components or representations from the original features.

It is also different from feature transformation, which changes the values or scale of existing features.

And it is different from feature creation, which generates new features from raw inputs.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Subset of original features: Use feature selection.

    Minimize error rate: Keep the most predictive features.

    Reduce model complexity: Remove irrelevant or redundant inputs.

    Improve training efficiency: Train with fewer useful columns.

    Reduce overfitting risk: Remove noisy features.

    Preserve original feature meaning: Select existing features instead of projecting them into a new space.


Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Subset of original features + minimize error rate + reduce model complexity = feature selection.

Remember:

    Feature selection: Keeps the best original features.

    Feature extraction: Creates new lower-dimensional representations, such as PCA components.

    Feature transformation: Changes existing feature values, scale, or format.

    Feature creation: Generates new features from existing or raw data.

    PCA, ICA, LDA: Usually signal feature extraction.

    Feature importance scores: Usually signal feature selection.

Official AWS references.

    AWS Well-Architected Machine Learning Lens: feature engineering.

Key exam terms: feature selection, feature extraction, feature transformation, feature creation, minimize error rate, feature importance scores, dimensionality reduction, Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler.

Domain
Data Preparation for Machine Learning (ML)
Question 43Skipped

A mining and minerals company, OreVault Analytics, is using Amazon Redshift ML in its primary AWS account to build predictive models for ore grade classification and equipment failure forecasting.


The training data for these models, which includes sensor readings from drilling operations and historical assay records, is stored in an Amazon S3 bucket in a secondary AWS account.


The company requires a secure pipeline that allows Redshift ML in the primary account to access this training data while ensuring that no public IPv4 addresses are used in the data transfer process. What do you recommend? 



Use S3 Transfer Acceleration to optimize data transfers between the accounts. Configure Redshift ML in the primary account to access the S3 bucket through the accelerated endpoint
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon S3 Transfer Acceleration leverages Amazon CloudFront edge locations and routes data transfers over the public internet to reduce latency. Because it traverses the public internet, it inherently uses public IPv4 addresses, which directly violates the stated requirement to avoid public IPv4 addresses in the data transfer process. Transfer Acceleration is designed for geographically dispersed users uploading to S3 at speed, not for private, account-to-account data access. Keyword tip: whenever a question explicitly prohibits the use of public IP addresses or the public internet, immediately eliminate any option that references S3 Transfer Acceleration or public-facing accelerated endpoints.
Deploy a VPN connection between the VPCs in the primary and secondary accounts to securely transfer training data to Redshift ML in the primary account
Explanation
Incorrect. A VPN connection provides encrypted network connectivity between VPCs or between an on-premises environment and a VPC — it does not apply to Amazon S3 bucket access. Amazon S3 is a globally managed AWS service whose buckets are not deployed inside any VPC and therefore cannot be reached through a VPN tunnel in a meaningful way. Creating a VPN connection between VPCs in two separate accounts does not establish a functional or supported path for cross-account S3 bucket access in this context. Keyword tip: remember that Amazon S3 is a regionally scoped, managed service that resides outside any VPC boundary; VPN-based solutions are relevant for VPC-to-VPC or on-premises-to-VPC connectivity, not for controlling access to S3 objects.
Export the data from the S3 bucket in the secondary account to a new bucket in the primary account using AWS Glue, then configure Redshift ML to access the data from the primary bucket
Explanation
Incorrect. Exporting data from the secondary account's S3 bucket to a new bucket in the primary account using AWS Glue introduces redundant data duplication and significant operational overhead. This approach violates the principle of least privilege and operational efficiency for cross-account access, and it adds ongoing storage costs as well as a synchronization burden whenever the source data changes. The preferred approach is to use a VPC endpoint combined with a cross-account bucket policy to enable direct, private access without copying data. Keyword tip: when a cross-account access strategy relies on physically copying or exporting data to a new bucket rather than granting direct access, recognize it as an anti-pattern in scenarios where VPC endpoints and bucket policies can achieve the same result more efficiently.
Correct answer
Set up a VPC endpoint for Amazon S3 in the primary account to enable private communication. Configure a cross-account bucket policy in the secondary account to grant Redshift ML access to the S3 bucket securely
Explanation
Correct. Setting up a VPC endpoint for Amazon S3 in the primary account — either a Gateway endpoint or an Interface endpoint backed by AWS PrivateLink — enables private communication between resources in the primary account's VPC and Amazon S3 over the AWS private network, entirely bypassing the public internet and avoiding the use of public IPv4 addresses. Configuring a cross-account S3 bucket policy in the secondary account then grants Redshift ML in the primary account the necessary IAM permissions to read training data directly and securely from that bucket, without duplicating data or introducing additional operational complexity. This two-part combination satisfies both the security constraint (no public IPv4) and the operational efficiency requirement in a fully supported AWS architecture. Keyword tip: for cross-account S3 access with no public IP exposure, the canonical MLA-C01 solution is a VPC endpoint for S3 (Gateway or Interface) in the consuming account paired with a cross-account bucket policy in the owning account.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of secure cross-account data access patterns on AWS.

It focuses on allowing Amazon Redshift ML in one account to access training data stored in an Amazon S3 bucket in another account without using public IPv4 traffic.

It is tied to the ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Export the data from the S3 bucket in the secondary account to a new bucket in the primary account using AWS Glue, then configure Redshift ML to access the data from the primary bucket: This option is incorrect. AWS Glue could copy or transform data, but duplicating data into another bucket adds storage cost, synchronization overhead, and unnecessary operational complexity. The scenario requires secure direct access, not data duplication.

    Use S3 Transfer Acceleration to optimize data transfers between the accounts. Configure Redshift ML in the primary account to access the S3 bucket through the accelerated endpoint: This option is incorrect. Amazon S3 Transfer Acceleration uses edge locations and public endpoints. It does not satisfy a requirement to avoid public IPv4 traffic.

    Deploy a VPN connection between the VPCs in the primary and secondary accounts to securely transfer training data to Redshift ML in the primary account: This option is incorrect. A VPN connects networks such as VPCs or on-premises environments. Amazon S3 buckets are not deployed inside a VPC, so a VPC-to-VPC VPN is not the right mechanism for private S3 object access.

    Set up a VPC endpoint for Amazon S3 in the primary account to enable private communication. Configure a cross-account bucket policy in the secondary account to grant Redshift ML access to the S3 bucket securely: This is the correct option. A VPC endpoint for Amazon S3 enables private connectivity to Amazon S3 without using public internet paths. A cross-account Amazon S3 bucket policy grants the required access to the consuming account.

Why a VPC endpoint for S3 with a cross-account bucket policy fits this scenario.

The training data is stored in Amazon S3 in a secondary account.

Amazon Redshift ML in the primary account needs to access that data.

The traffic must avoid public IPv4 addresses.

The solution should avoid copying or duplicating the dataset.

That points to a VPC endpoint for Amazon S3 plus a cross-account bucket policy.

A VPC endpoint for Amazon S3 allows resources in a VPC to reach Amazon S3 over the AWS private network.

This avoids routing traffic through an internet gateway, NAT gateway, or public IPv4 path.

The cross-account bucket policy in the secondary account grants the required permissions to the principal in the primary account.

Together, these controls allow secure private access to the original training data without exporting it to another bucket.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Private S3 access: Use a VPC endpoint for Amazon S3.

    No public IPv4 traffic: Avoid public internet paths.

    Cross-account authorization: Use an Amazon S3 bucket policy in the data-owning account.

    No data duplication: Read directly from the source bucket.

    Redshift ML training data access: Allow Amazon Redshift ML to access training data securely.

    Least operational overhead: Avoid AWS Glue export pipelines, S3 Transfer Acceleration, or VPN-based workarounds.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Cross-account S3 access + no public internet/public IPv4 + Redshift ML training data = VPC endpoint for Amazon S3 + cross-account bucket policy.

Remember:

    VPC endpoint for Amazon S3: Private access path from a VPC to Amazon S3.

    Gateway endpoint: Common endpoint type for Amazon S3.

    Interface endpoint / AWS PrivateLink: PrivateLink-based option for supported endpoint patterns.

    Cross-account bucket policy: Grants access to principals from another AWS account.

    S3 Transfer Acceleration: Uses public accelerated endpoints, not the right fit for no-public-IP requirements.

    AWS Glue export: Creates duplicated data and synchronization overhead.

    VPN: Useful for network connectivity, not the primary mechanism for Amazon S3 bucket authorization.

Question 44Skipped

A food and beverage producer, NutriFlow Foods, is running containerized ML applications to process production-line sensor telemetry data and generate quality control and predictive maintenance insights.


These applications are deployed across Amazon EC2 instances, an Amazon Elastic Container Service (Amazon ECS) cluster, and AWS Lambda functions.


The EC2 and ECS workloads store predictions and artifacts on Amazon Elastic Block Store (Amazon EBS) volumes. To optimize costs, the company wants to identify inefficiently used resources and receive actionable recommendations to reduce expenses.


What is the most efficient solution to achieve this goal with minimal development effort?


Correct answer
Use AWS Compute Optimizer to analyze the specifications and utilization metrics of your AWS compute resources
Explanation
Correct. AWS Compute Optimizer analyzes the configuration and utilization metrics of AWS compute resources — including EC2 instances, ECS services on AWS Fargate, EBS volumes, and Lambda functions — and generates rightsizing recommendations to reduce costs and improve performance. It leverages Amazon CloudWatch utilization metric history to produce projected utilization graphs, savings opportunity metrics, and performance improvement opportunity metrics at the account, resource type, and individual resource levels. This directly addresses all resource types present in NutriFlow Foods' environment (EC2, ECS, EBS, Lambda) with no custom instrumentation or development effort required. Keyword tip: When a scenario involves rightsizing recommendations across EC2, ECS, EBS, and Lambda with minimal development effort — always choose AWS Compute Optimizer.
Use AWS Budgets to analyze the specifications and utilization metrics of your AWS resources
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS Budgets is primarily a cost tracking and alerting service used to monitor aggregate utilization and coverage metrics for Reserved Instances (RIs) or Savings Plans, and to set cost and usage thresholds. It does not analyze the configuration and utilization metrics of individual compute resources such as EC2 instances, ECS tasks, EBS volumes, or Lambda functions to generate rightsizing recommendations. NutriFlow Foods' requirement is resource-level rightsizing, which is outside the scope of AWS Budgets entirely. Keyword tip: When the requirement is cost threshold alerting or RI/Savings Plan coverage tracking — think AWS Budgets; when the requirement is compute-level rightsizing recommendations across EC2, ECS, EBS, and Lambda — think AWS Compute Optimizer.
Use AWS Trusted Advisor to analyze the specifications and utilization metrics of your AWS resources
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS Trusted Advisor provides broad best-practice recommendations across cost optimization, performance, resilience, security, operational excellence, and service limits. While it includes some cost optimization checks, its compute recommendations are less granular and less actionable than those provided by AWS Compute Optimizer, and it does not deliver deep, utilization-driven rightsizing analysis for specific resource types such as EBS volumes or Lambda functions. Trusted Advisor is designed for overall account health assessments rather than the targeted compute rightsizing that NutriFlow Foods requires. Keyword tip: When the scenario requires broad, multi-pillar AWS best-practice checks across an entire account — think AWS Trusted Advisor; for targeted, utilization-driven compute rightsizing across specific resource types — think AWS Compute Optimizer.
Use AWS Cost Explorer to analyze the specifications and utilization metrics of your AWS compute resources
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS Cost Explorer provides a visualization interface to understand and manage AWS costs and usage over time, and it does offer rightsizing recommendations for EC2 instances; however, those recommendations are sourced by integrating with AWS Compute Optimizer internally. Cost Explorer is best suited for budget planning, anomaly detection, and high-level cost trend analysis rather than deep, resource-level utilization analysis across EC2, ECS on AWS Fargate, EBS volumes, and Lambda functions simultaneously. Selecting Cost Explorer here introduces an unnecessary layer of indirection compared to using AWS Compute Optimizer directly. Keyword tip: When the scenario involves visualizing cost trends, building custom cost and usage reports, or detecting spending anomalies — think AWS Cost Explorer; for actionable, compute-specific rightsizing across multiple resource types — think AWS Compute Optimizer.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of AWS cost optimization services for compute rightsizing.

It focuses on selecting AWS Compute Optimizer when the requirement is to analyze utilization metrics and generate rightsizing recommendations across resources such as Amazon EC2, Amazon ECS, AWS Lambda, and Amazon EBS.

It is tied to the ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Use AWS Budgets to analyze the specifications and utilization metrics of your AWS resources: This option is incorrect. AWS Budgets is used to set cost and usage thresholds, monitor budgets, and send alerts. It does not perform resource-level rightsizing analysis for compute resources.

    Use AWS Cost Explorer to analyze the specifications and utilization metrics of your AWS compute resources: This option is not the best answer. AWS Cost Explorer is useful for cost visualization, trend analysis, forecasting, and cost reporting. It can surface some rightsizing insights, but the purpose-built service for compute rightsizing recommendations is AWS Compute Optimizer.

    Use AWS Compute Optimizer to analyze the specifications and utilization metrics of your AWS compute resources: This is the correct option. AWS Compute Optimizer analyzes utilization metrics and configuration data to recommend right-sized resources for services such as Amazon EC2, Amazon ECS on AWS Fargate, AWS Lambda, and Amazon EBS.

    Use AWS Trusted Advisor to analyze the specifications and utilization metrics of your AWS resources: This option is incorrect. AWS Trusted Advisor provides broad best-practice checks across cost optimization, security, performance, resilience, operational excellence, and service limits. It is not as targeted or granular as AWS Compute Optimizer for compute rightsizing.

Why AWS Compute Optimizer fits this scenario.

The scenario requires resource-level rightsizing.

The environment includes multiple compute-related resource types.

The goal is to find over-provisioned or underutilized resources.

The solution should require minimal development effort.

That points directly to AWS Compute Optimizer.

AWS Compute Optimizer uses utilization metrics, such as metrics from Amazon CloudWatch, to analyze how resources are actually being used.

It then generates recommendations to improve performance, reduce cost, or right-size resources.

This is useful for identifying oversized Amazon EC2 instances, inefficient Amazon ECS services, over-provisioned Amazon EBS volumes, and AWS Lambda functions with suboptimal memory settings.

AWS Budgets can alert on cost thresholds.

AWS Cost Explorer can analyze spending trends.

AWS Trusted Advisor can provide general account recommendations.

But AWS Compute Optimizer is the purpose-built service for detailed compute rightsizing.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Compute rightsizing: Use AWS Compute Optimizer.

    Utilization analysis: Analyze metrics from resources over time.

    Multi-resource coverage: Include Amazon EC2, Amazon ECS, AWS Lambda, and Amazon EBS.

    Cost optimization: Identify savings opportunities.

    Performance optimization: Detect under-provisioned or over-provisioned resources.

    Minimal development effort: Use managed recommendations instead of custom analysis.

    AWS Cost Explorer: Cost visualization, reporting, and trend analysis.

Question 45Skipped

A telecommunications provider, NexWave Communications, uses Amazon SageMaker Studio to develop its ML models.


A team of ten developers is working on a proof-of-concept model, with all users linked to a single SageMaker Studio domain.


The company needs an automated alert system that notifies them when SageMaker compute costs exceed a specific threshold.


Which of the following options would meet these requirements?


Add tags to the user profile in the SageMaker domain. Configure AWS Cost Explorer to send an alert when the threshold is breached.
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS Cost Explorer is a powerful tool for visualizing, querying, and analyzing historical AWS costs and usage over time, but it does not natively support automated, threshold-based alert notifications. AWS Budgets is the purpose-built AWS service for proactively alerting teams when cost or usage thresholds are exceeded. Choosing Cost Explorer here would require manual inspection rather than fulfilling the automated alerting requirement. Keyword tip: AWS Cost Explorer is used for post-hoc cost visualization and analysis; AWS Budgets is used for proactive, automated threshold alerts — do not conflate these two services on cost-monitoring questions.
Correct answer
Add tags to the user profile in the SageMaker domain. Configure AWS Budgets to send an alert when the threshold is breached.
Explanation
Correct. In Amazon SageMaker Studio, a user profile represents a single user within a domain and is the primary entity for cost attribution, sharing, and reporting. Tags added to a user profile are automatically propagated to all resources that user subsequently creates — including training jobs, processing jobs, and hosted endpoints — via the domain ARN tag and user profile ARN tag. AWS Budgets allows teams to define custom spend thresholds and delivers automated alerts through Amazon SNS (and optionally AWS Lambda) when actual or forecasted costs breach those thresholds. This combination of user profile tagging and AWS Budgets directly satisfies both the per-user cost-tracking and automated-alerting requirements described in the scenario. Keyword tip: 'SageMaker user profile tags + AWS Budgets alerts' is the canonical AWS pattern for per-user cost monitoring and automated threshold notifications in SageMaker Studio.
Add resource tagging to the IAM profile of each user. Configure AWS Trusted Advisor to track the resource costs and generate recommendations.
Explanation
Incorrect. Resource tagging for SageMaker cost allocation must be applied at the SageMaker user profile level within the SageMaker domain, not to IAM profiles. Furthermore, AWS Trusted Advisor inspects the AWS environment and surfaces best-practice recommendations across performance, security, and cost optimization categories, but it does not support proactive, automated threshold-based budget alerts. Keyword tip: AWS Trusted Advisor provides reactive, advisory recommendations — when a scenario requires automated cost threshold alerts, AWS Budgets is the correct service, not Trusted Advisor.
Add custom tags to resources by editing the SageMaker user profile in the SageMaker domain. Modify the custom tag propagation settings at the user profile level to suit each user's requirements. Configure AWS Budgets to send an alert when the threshold is breached.
Explanation
Incorrect. Although adding tags to a SageMaker user profile is a valid cost-allocation practice, tag propagation settings can only be configured at the SageMaker domain level — not at the individual user profile level. There is no mechanism to customize tag propagation behavior independently for a specific user profile. AWS Budgets is the correct alerting service, but the premise about per-user tag propagation configuration makes this option technically invalid. Keyword tip: When a scenario mentions 'per-user tag propagation settings' in SageMaker Studio, recognize this as incorrect — tag propagation scope is a domain-level configuration, not a user-profile-level one.
Overall explanation

Adding tags to a SageMaker user profile is valid, but tag propagation settings are configured at the SageMaker Studio domain level, not at the individual user profile level. AWS Cost Explorer is used for cost visualization and analysis. It is not the purpose-built service for automated threshold-based budget alerts. AWS Budgets is the correct alerting service.

Question 46Skipped

A food and beverage producer, NutriCraft Foods, is planning to use Amazon SageMaker to train a machine learning model for quality defect detection in product imagery captured on the production line.


The training dataset, consisting of 8 TB of high-resolution images, is stored on an Amazon FSx for NetApp ONTAP system virtual machine (SVM) that is configured in the same VPC as the SageMaker environment.


The ML engineer needs to ensure that the SageMaker training job can efficiently access the dataset during training. 


Which of the following represents the best solution for the given use case?


Copy the training data from the FSx for NetApp ONTAP system to an Amazon S3 bucket and configure SageMaker to use the S3 bucket for training
Explanation
Incorrect. Copying 8 TB of data from FSx for NetApp ONTAP to Amazon S3 introduces significant time overhead, egress data transfer costs, and storage duplication before training can even begin. Since the FSx SVM is already configured in the same VPC as SageMaker, this approach unnecessarily moves data that is already directly accessible at high throughput. S3 as an intermediary staging layer adds latency and operational waste when a native VPC-based integration already exists. Keyword tip: When source data already resides on a file system co-located with SageMaker in the same VPC, copying to S3 as an intermediary is an anti-pattern — prefer direct mounting instead.
Correct answer
Mount the FSx for NetApp ONTAP file system directly as an NFS volume to the SageMaker training instance
Explanation
Correct. Mounting the FSx for NetApp ONTAP file system directly as an NFS volume to the SageMaker training instance is the most efficient approach for this scenario. It eliminates data duplication, avoids unnecessary transfer costs, and delivers low-latency, high-throughput access to the full 8 TB dataset from the storage system. Because the FSx SVM is configured in the same VPC as SageMaker, the NFS mount can be established seamlessly without additional networking or data movement steps, allowing the training job to begin immediately with full data fidelity. Keyword tip: When an FSx for NetApp ONTAP SVM is co-located in the same VPC as SageMaker, direct NFS volume mounting is the optimal pattern for large-scale, high-throughput training data access.
Use AWS DataSync to continuously synchronize the FSx for NetApp ONTAP system with Amazon S3 and set up SageMaker to read the data from S3 during training
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS DataSync is designed for scheduled or continuous data movement between on-premises storage, AWS file systems, and Amazon S3 — particularly across environments or regions. Configuring DataSync to synchronize the FSx for NetApp ONTAP system to S3 introduces unnecessary operational complexity, additional cost, and replication lag for a large 8 TB dataset that is already reachable within the same VPC. This pattern is redundant when the storage system supports direct NFS access from the training instance. Keyword tip: AWS DataSync is the right tool for cross-environment or cross-region data migration workflows, not for training scenarios where data is already locally accessible within the same VPC.
Export the training data from the FSx for NetApp ONTAP system to a local system and re-upload the data to SageMaker through the SageMaker Python SDK
Explanation
Incorrect. Exporting 8 TB of data to a local system and then re-uploading it via the SageMaker Python SDK is extremely time-consuming, bandwidth-intensive, and operationally impractical at this scale. This approach completely disregards the existing VPC-based infrastructure and introduces unnecessary data egress, local storage requirements, and re-ingestion latency. It represents the least efficient option and contradicts cloud-native MLOps best practices for large dataset access. Keyword tip: Re-uploading large datasets through the SageMaker Python SDK from a local system is never a best practice at scale — always leverage cloud-native storage integrations such as direct NFS mounting or S3 when the data already resides in the cloud.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests understanding of efficient data access patterns for Amazon SageMaker training jobs when large datasets are stored on Amazon FSx for NetApp ONTAP.

It focuses on minimizing data movement by directly mounting the file system through NFS when the file system is available in the same Amazon VPC as the training job.

It is tied to the Data Preparation for Machine Learning (ML) domain.

    Copy the training data from the FSx for NetApp ONTAP system to an Amazon S3 bucket and configure SageMaker to use the S3 bucket for training: This option is incorrect. Since the FSx for NetApp ONTAP file system is already accessible in the same VPC, direct mounting is more efficient.

    Use AWS DataSync to continuously synchronize the FSx for NetApp ONTAP system with Amazon S3 and set up SageMaker to read the data from S3 during training: This option is incorrect. AWS DataSync is useful for migration or synchronization between storage systems, regions, or environments. In this scenario, the data is already accessible to Amazon SageMaker through the same VPC, so synchronizing to Amazon S3 is unnecessary.

    Mount the FSx for NetApp ONTAP file system directly as an NFS volume to the SageMaker training instance: This is the correct option. Mounting Amazon FSx for NetApp ONTAP directly through NFS provides high-throughput, low-latency access to the dataset without copying or duplicating the data.

    Export the training data from the FSx for NetApp ONTAP system to a local system and re-upload the data to SageMaker through the SageMaker Python SDK: This option is incorrect. Exporting and re-uploading a large dataset is slow, bandwidth-intensive, and operationally inefficient. It ignores the existing cloud-native network path between Amazon SageMaker and Amazon FSx for NetApp ONTAP.

The dataset is large.

The data already resides in Amazon FSx for NetApp ONTAP.

The FSx for NetApp ONTAP storage virtual machine is configured in the same Amazon VPC as Amazon SageMaker.

That points directly to mounting the file system as an NFS volume to the SageMaker training instance.

With this approach, the training job can read the data directly from the file system.

There is no need to synchronize the dataset with AWS DataSync.

Question 47Skipped

A data science team at an agricultural technology company, AgroVision Analytics, is evaluating different machine learning paradigms for use in their generative AI initiatives — including synthetic crop disease image generation and AI-assisted agronomic recommendation systems.


What is the primary distinction between discriminative models and generative models in the context of generative AI?


Correct answer
Generative models focus on generating new data from learned patterns, whereas discriminative models classify data by distinguishing between different classes
Explanation
Correct. Generative models learn the underlying joint probability distribution of inputs and outputs — P(X, Y), or P(X) in unsupervised settings — enabling them to synthesize new data samples that resemble the training distribution, such as novel crop disease images or synthetic sensor readings. Discriminative models, by contrast, learn the conditional probability P(Y|X), focusing on decision boundaries to separate classes, making them well suited for tasks such as pest species classification or yield-quality grading. Examples of generative models include GPT-4, GANs, VAEs, and diffusion models, while logistic regression, SVMs, and gradient-boosted trees are classic discriminative models. Keyword tip: when a question contrasts 'data generation or synthesis' with 'classification or decision boundaries,' the generative-vs-discriminative distinction based on P(X,Y) versus P(Y|X) is the defining characteristic to identify.
Discriminative models are used to generate new data, while generative models are used only for classification
Explanation
Incorrect. This option directly inverts the roles of the two model types. It is generative models that produce new data samples — such as synthesizing labeled training images of rare plant diseases — while discriminative models are primarily used for classification and prediction tasks. Reversing these roles is a classic distractor technique on the MLA-C01 exam. Keyword tip: whenever an answer option swaps the well-established capabilities of two contrasting concepts — such as generative versus discriminative, or batch versus real-time processing — it is almost certainly incorrect.
Generative models are trained on labeled data, while discriminative models can be trained on both labeled and unlabeled data
Explanation
Incorrect. The distinction between generative and discriminative models is not primarily about whether they use labeled or unlabeled data. Both model types can, under certain architectures, leverage labeled or unlabeled data — for example, semi-supervised generative models exist alongside supervised discriminative classifiers. The fundamental difference lies in what each model learns: generative models learn the full data distribution P(X, Y), while discriminative models learn the conditional distribution P(Y|X). Keyword tip: watch for distractors that reframe the generative/discriminative distinction as a supervised versus unsupervised learning question — this is a common misdirection on the MLA-C01 exam.
Discriminative models are only used for text classification, while generative models are only used for image classification
Explanation
Incorrect. This statement is factually wrong on both counts. Discriminative models are applicable to a wide range of classification and regression tasks — including crop disease detection from imagery, soil nutrient level prediction, and supply-chain fraud detection — not solely text classification. Similarly, generative models are not restricted to image generation; they are used for text generation, audio synthesis, molecular design, and many other domains. Keyword tip: any option that artificially restricts a model type to a single modality or task type should be treated with strong suspicion in MLA-C01 scenarios.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests foundational knowledge of generative AI model paradigms.

It focuses on distinguishing generative models, which can create new data from learned patterns, from discriminative models, which classify or predict labels by learning decision boundaries.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Generative models focus on generating new data from learned patterns, whereas discriminative models classify data by distinguishing between different classes: This is the correct option. Generative models learn the underlying data distribution and can create new samples. Discriminative models learn boundaries between classes and are used for classification or prediction tasks.

    Generative models are trained on labeled data, while discriminative models can be trained on both labeled and unlabeled data: This option is incorrect. The main distinction is not whether the data is labeled or unlabeled. The key difference is what the model learns: generative models learn the data distribution, while discriminative models learn the relationship between inputs and outputs.

    Discriminative models are only used for text classification, while generative models are only used for image classification: This option is incorrect. Both model types can be used across many modalities. Discriminative models are not limited to text, and generative models are not limited to images.

    Discriminative models are used to generate new data, while generative models are used only for classification: This option is incorrect. It reverses the definitions. Generative models generate new data. Discriminative models are mainly used to classify, rank, or predict outputs.

Why generative models create data and discriminative models classify data.

The question asks for the correct distinction between two ML model paradigms.

Generative models learn patterns in the data distribution.

They can then generate new examples that resemble the training data.

Examples include large language models, diffusion models, GANs, and VAEs.

These models can create text, images, audio, synthetic samples, or other generated outputs.

Discriminative models focus on prediction.

They learn how to map input features to labels or target values.

For classification, they learn decision boundaries between classes.

Examples include logistic regression, support vector machines, gradient-boosted trees, and many neural network classifiers.

In AWS terms, Amazon Bedrock is commonly associated with generative AI applications using foundation models.

Amazon SageMaker can be used for traditional supervised models, including classification and regression workloads.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Data generation: Use generative models.

    Classification: Use discriminative models.

    Learned distribution: Generative models learn patterns that allow synthesis.

    Decision boundaries: Discriminative models separate classes.

    Correct ML taxonomy: Do not confuse generative AI with supervised classification.

    AWS service mapping: Associate Amazon Bedrock with foundation-model-powered generative AI and Amazon SageMaker with broader ML model development.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Generate new data = generative model.

Classify or distinguish between classes = discriminative model.

Remember:

    Generative models: Learn data patterns and synthesize new outputs.

    Discriminative models: Learn class boundaries or prediction functions.

    Joint distribution: Generative models may model P(X, Y) or P(X).

    Conditional distribution: Discriminative models model P(Y|X).

    Amazon Bedrock: Strong signal for generative AI and foundation model use cases.

    Amazon SageMaker: Broad ML platform for training, deploying, and managing ML models.

    Modality distractors: Do not assume generative means only images or discriminative means only text.

Official AWS references.

    What is generative AI?

Key exam terms: generative models, discriminative models, joint distribution P(X,Y), conditional distribution P(Y|X), decision boundaries, Amazon Bedrock, Amazon SageMaker, data synthesis, generative AI paradigms.
